## Appendix A Colonel Roosevelt’s Report to the Secretary of War of September 10th / 附录 A 罗斯福上校 9 月 10 日致陆军部长报告

===Original===
[Before it was sent, this letter was read to and approved by every officer of the regiment who had served through the Santiago campaign.]
[Copy.]

===Chinese===
[此信发出之前，曾向随团经历整个圣地亚哥战役的每一位军官宣读，并经其认可。]
[副本。]

===Original===
> **Sir**: In answer to the circular issued by command of Major-General Shafter under date of September 8, 1898, containing a request for information by the Adjutant-General of September 7th, I have the honor to report as follows:

===Chinese===
> **长官**：为答复奉沙夫特少将（Major-General Shafter）之命于 1898 年 9 月 8 日颁发、载有副官长（Adjutant-General）9 月 7 日情报征询的通函，我谨荣幸地报告如下：

===Original===
> I am a little in doubt whether the fact that on certain occasions my regiment suffered for food, etc., should be put down to an actual shortage of supplies or to general defects in the system of administration. Thus, when the regiment arrived in Tampa after a four days’ journey by cars from its camp at San Antonio, it received no food whatever for twenty-four hours, and as the travel rations had been completely exhausted, food for several of the troops was purchased by their officers, who, of course, have not been reimbursed by the Government. In the same way we were short one or two meals at the time of embarking at Port Tampa on the transport; but this I think was due, not to a failure in the quantity of supplies, but to the lack of system in embarkation.

===Chinese===
> 我团在若干场合曾在口粮等方面受到困乏，此事究竟应记在补给的实际短缺账上，还是记在管理体系的普遍缺陷账上，我心中不无疑惑。譬如，全团从圣安东尼奥（San Antonio）的营地乘火车经四天旅程抵达坦帕（Tampa）后，整整二十四小时没有得到任何食物；由于行军口粮（travel rations）已经完全耗尽，有几个连的伙食是各连军官自己出钱购买的，而他们自然至今未获政府报销。同样，在坦帕港（Port Tampa）登上运输船之际，我们也短缺了一两顿饭；但依我看，这并非补给数量不足所致，而是登船组织全无章法所致。

===Original===
> As with the other regiments, no information was given in advance what transports we should take, or how we should proceed to get aboard, nor did anyone exercise any supervision over the embarkation. Each regimental commander, so far as I know, was left to find out as best he could, after he was down at the dock, what transport had not been taken, and then to get his regiment aboard it, if he was able, before some other regiment got it. Our regiment was told to go to a certain switch, and take a train for Port Tampa at twelve o’clock, midnight. The train never came. After three hours of waiting we were sent to another switch, and finally at six o’clock in the morning got possession of some coal-cars and came down in them. When we reached the quay where the embarkation was proceeding, everything was in utter confusion. The quay was piled with stores and swarming with thousands of men of different regiments, besides onlookers, etc. The commanding General, when we at last found him, told Colonel Wood and myself that he did not know what ship we were to embark on, and that we must find Colonel Humphrey, the Quartermaster-General. Colonel Humphrey was not in his office, and nobody knew where he was. The commanders of the different regiments were busy trying to find him, while their troops waited in the trains, so as to discover the ships to which they were allotted﻿—some of these ships being at the dock and some in midstream. After a couple of hours’ search, Colonel Wood found Colonel Humphrey and was allotted a ship. Immediately afterward I found that it had already been allotted to two other regiments. It was then coming to the dock. Colonel Wood boarded it in midstream to keep possession, while I double-quicked the men down from the cars and got there just ahead of the other two regiments. One of these regiments, I was afterward informed, spent the next thirty-six hours in cars in consequence. We suffered nothing beyond the loss of a couple of meals, which, it seems to me, can hardly be put down to any failure in the quantity of supplies furnished to the troops.

===Chinese===
> 与其他各团一样，事先没有任何人告知我们该搭乘哪些运输船，也没人指点我们如何上船，更没有任何人对登船实施监督。据我所知，每位团长都是到了码头上以后，才只好自己设法打听哪条船还没被别人占去，然后——如果来得及的话——赶在另一个团抢先之前把自己的人弄上船。我们团奉命开到某条铁路侧线，于午夜十二点搭乘去坦帕港的火车。火车根本没有来。等候了三个钟头之后，我们又被打发到另一条侧线，最后到早晨六点钟才占下几节煤车，坐着它们开了下来。抵达进行登船作业的码头时，一切都乱得不可名状。码头上辎重堆积如山，成千上万不同团队的士兵麇集其间，此外还有围观者等等。总指挥官——我们好不容易才找到他——对伍德上校（Colonel Wood）和我说，他不知道我们该上哪条船，我们必须自己去找总军需官（Quartermaster-General）汉弗莱上校（Colonel Humphrey）。汉弗莱上校不在他的办公室，也没人知道他在哪里。各团团长都在忙着找他，他们的部队则困在火车里干等，等着弄清各自分派的船位——这些船有的靠着码头，有的停在中流。搜寻了两个来钟头，伍德上校找到了汉弗莱上校，分到一条船。紧接着我便发现，这条船已经分给了另外两个团。船这时正驶向码头。伍德上校抢在中流登上该船以保住占用权，我则带着士兵们从车厢一路跑步冲下去，赶在那两个团之前先一步赶到。其中一个团，我后来获悉，为此在车厢里足足困了三十六个钟头。我们所受的损失，不过少吃了两顿饭而已；依我看来，这实在很难算作拨给部队的补给在数量上的短缺。

===Original===
> We were two weeks on the troopship *Yucatan*, and as we were given twelve days’ travel rations, we of course fell short toward the end of the trip, but eked things out with some of our field rations and troop stuff. The quality of the travel rations given to us was good, except in the important item of meat. The canned roast beef is worse than a failure as part of the rations, for in effect it amounts to reducing the rations by just so much, as a great majority of the men find it uneatable. It was coarse, stringy, tasteless, and very disagreeable in appearance, and so unpalatable that the effort to eat it made some of the men sick. Most of the men preferred to be hungry rather than eat it. If cooked in a stew with plenty of onions and potatoes﻿—i.e., if only one ingredient in a dish with other more savory ingredients﻿—it could be eaten, especially if well salted and peppered; but, as usual (what I regard as a great mistake), no salt was issued with the travel rations, and of course no potatoes and onions. There were no cooking facilities on the transport. When the men obtained any, it was by bribing the cook. Toward the last, when they began to draw on the field rations, they had to eat the bacon raw. On the return trip the same difficulty in rations obtained.﻿—i.e., the rations were short because the men could not eat the canned roast beef, and had no salt. We purchased of the ship’s supplies some flour and pork and a little rice for the men, so as to relieve the shortage as much as possible, and individual sick men were helped from private sources by officers, who themselves ate what they had purchased in Santiago. As nine-tenths of the men were more or less sick, the unattractiveness of the travel rations was doubly unfortunate. It would have been an excellent thing for their health if we could have had onions and potatoes, and means for cooking them. Moreover, the water was very bad, and sometimes a cask was struck that was positively undrinkable. The lack of ice for the weak and sickly men was very much felt. Fortunately there was no epidemic, for there was not a place on the ship where patients could have been isolated.

===Chinese===
> 我们在运兵船「尤卡坦」号（*Yucatan*）上待了两个星期，配发的行军口粮只有十二天的量，行程临近结束时自然不敷食用，只好拿一部分战地口粮（field rations）和连队自备的存粮凑合着撑过去。所发行军口粮的质量不差，唯独肉类这一重要项目除外。罐装烤牛肉（canned roast beef）用作口粮，比彻底失败还要糟糕，因为实际效果等于把口粮削减了相应的一份——绝大多数士兵都觉得它根本没法吃。它粗砺、多筋、毫无味道，卖相也十分倒胃口，难吃得让一些士兵硬吃下去便要作呕。多数士兵宁可饿肚子也不愿吃它。若是同大量洋葱和土豆一起炖煮——也就是说，仅仅作为一道菜里的一种配料，掺在其他更有滋味的东西之中——倒还咽得下去，尤其是盐和胡椒放足的话；但同往常一样（我认为这是一个重大失误），行军口粮不配发盐，土豆和洋葱当然更没有。运输船上没有任何烹调设施。士兵们偶尔得以用上一用，全凭贿赂船上的厨子。到临近末尾、开始动用战地口粮时，他们只好生吃咸肉。回程途中，口粮方面又遇到了同样的困难。也就是说，口粮之所以短缺，是因为士兵们吃不进那罐装烤牛肉，又没有盐。我们向船方购买了一些面粉、猪肉和少量稻米分给士兵，尽量缓解短缺；个别病号则由军官们私下接济，而军官们自己吃的，是他们在圣地亚哥买来的东西。由于十之八九的士兵都或多或少在生病，行军口粮之不可口便成了加倍的憾事。倘若我们能弄到洋葱和土豆，并有烹煮它们的手段，对他们的健康将是一件极好的事。何况船上的水很坏，有时敲开的一桶简直完全不能入口。冰块的匮乏，体弱多病的士兵感受最深。所幸没有发生疫病，因为船上根本找不出一处可以隔离病人的地方。

===Original===
> During the month following the landing of the army in Cuba the food-supplies were generally short in quantity, and in quality were never such as were best suited to men undergoing severe hardships and great exposure in an unhealthy tropical climate. The rations were, I understand, the same as those used in the Klondike. In this connection, I call especial attention to the report of Captain Brown, made by my orders when I was Brigade-Commander, and herewith appended. I also call attention to the report of my own Quartermaster. Usually we received full rations of bacon and hardtack. The hardtack, however, was often mouldy, so that parts of cases, and even whole cases, could not be used. The bacon was usually good. But bacon and hardtack make poor food for men toiling and fighting in trenches under the midsummer sun of the tropics. The ration of coffee was often short, and that of sugar generally so; we rarely got any vegetables. Under these circumstances the men lost strength steadily, and as the fever speedily attacked them, they suffered from being reduced to a bacon and hardtack diet. So much did the shortage of proper food tell upon their health that again and again officers were compelled to draw upon their private purses, or upon the Red Cross Society, to make good the deficiency of the Government supply. Again and again we sent down improvised pack-trains composed of officers’ horses, of captured Spanish cavalry ponies, or of mules which had been shot or abandoned but were cured by our men. These expeditions﻿—sometimes under the Chaplain, sometimes under the Quartermaster, sometimes under myself, and occasionally under a trooper﻿—would go to the seacoast or to the Red Cross headquarters, or, after the surrender, into the city of Santiago, to get food both for the well and the sick. The Red Cross Society rendered invaluable aid. For example, on one of these expeditions I personally brought up 600 pounds of beans; on another occasion I personally brought up 500 pounds of rice, 800 pounds of cornmeal, 200 pounds of sugar, 100 pounds of tea, 100 pounds of oatmeal, 5 barrels of potatoes, and two of onions, with cases of canned soup and condensed milk for the sick in hospitals. Every scrap of the food thus brought up was eaten with avidity by the soldiers, and put new heart and strength into them. It was only our constant care of the men in this way that enabled us to keep them in any trim at all. As for the sick in the hospital, unless we were able from outside sources to get them such simple delicacies as rice and condensed milk, they usually had the alternative of eating salt pork and hardtack or going without. After each fight we got a good deal of food from the Spanish camps in the way of beans, peas, and rice, together with green coffee, all of which the men used and relished greatly. In some respects the Spanish rations were preferable to ours, notably in the use of rice. After we had been ashore a month the supplies began to come in in abundance, and we then fared very well. Up to that time the men were underfed, during the very weeks when the heaviest drain was being made upon their vitality, and the deficiency was only partially supplied through the aid of the Red Cross, and out of the officers’ pockets and the pockets of various New York friends who sent us money. Before, during, and immediately after the fights of June 24th and July 1st, we were very short of even the bacon and hardtack. About July 14th, when the heavy rains interrupted communication, we were threatened with famine, as we were informed that there was not a day’s supply of provisions in advance nearer than the seacoast; and another twenty-four hours’ rain would have resulted in a complete breakdown of communications, so that for several days we should have been reduced to a diet of mule-meat and mangos. At this time, in anticipation of such a contingency, by foraging and hoarding we got a little ahead, so that when our supplies were cut down for a day or two we did not suffer much, and were even able to furnish a little aid to the less fortunate First Illinois Regiment, which was camped next to us. Members of the Illinois Regiment were offering our men $1 apiece for hardtacks.

===Chinese===
> 大军在古巴（Cuba）登陆后的一个月间，食品供应普遍数量不足，质量上也从来不曾适合过在不卫生的热带气候下经受严酷劳顿与长期露天煎熬的士兵。据我了解，这套口粮与当年克朗代克（Klondike）所用相同。在此，我特别提请留意布朗上尉（Captain Brown）的报告——那是我任旅长（Brigade-Commander）时命其写就的，随函附呈。同时提请留意我团军需官的报告。咸肉和硬面饼（hardtack）通常按全量配发。然而硬面饼时常发霉，以致有些箱子里的一部分、甚至整箱整箱都无法食用。咸肉一般是好的。但咸肉加硬面饼，对于在热带盛夏烈日下据壕苦干苦战的士兵而言，实在是低劣的伙食。咖啡的定量常常不足，糖则一般都缺；我们难得见到任何蔬菜。在这种境况下，士兵们的体力不断下滑，热病又迅速向他们袭来，他们苦于被压缩到纯乎咸肉硬面饼的饮食。合宜食物的短缺如此严重地损害了他们的健康，以致军官们一再被迫动用私囊，或求助于红十字会（Red Cross Society），以填补政府供应的亏空。我们还一再派出由军官坐骑、缴获的西班牙骑兵矮种马、或原先中弹和被弃置、经我方士兵治愈的骡子拼凑而成的临时驮队。这些远足队——有时由随军牧师带队，有时由军需官带队，有时由我亲自带队，偶尔也由一名士兵带队——前往海岸，或红十字会总部，或在投降之后进圣地亚哥城，为健者与病者采办食物。红十字会给予了无可估量的援助。举例来说，其中一次远足，我亲自运回 600 磅豆子；另一次，我亲自运回 500 磅稻米、800 磅玉米粉、200 磅糖、100 磅茶叶、100 磅燕麦片、5 桶土豆和 2 桶洋葱，外加成箱的罐头汤和炼乳，供医院里的病号用。这样运上来的每一口食物，士兵们都吃得津津有味，吃出了新的心气与气力。全靠我们这样不间断地照拂士兵，才使他们勉强保住了几分元气。至于医院里的病号，除非我们能从外部渠道弄到稻米、炼乳这类简单的滋补吃食，他们通常只有两条路：吃咸猪肉加硬面饼，或者干脆不吃。每次战斗之后，我们都从西班牙人营地里弄到不少吃食——豆子、豌豆、稻米，外加生咖啡，士兵们统统拿来享用了，而且吃得津津有味。西班牙人的口粮在某些方面比我们的可取，尤其是在稻米的使用上。上岸满一个月之后，补给开始大量涌到，我们此后过得很好。在那之前，正当士兵们的元气被消耗得最厉害的那几个星期，他们一直吃不饱，亏空只能部分地靠红十字会、靠军官们的腰包、以及各位给我们汇钱的纽约友人的腰包来填补。6 月 24 日与 7 月 1 日两次战斗之前、期间和刚一结束之后，我们连咸肉和硬面饼都非常短缺。约在 7 月 14 日，大雨中断了交通，我们一度面临饥荒的威胁，因为据告知，滨海一线以内再没有超出一天的存粮；倘若再下二十四小时的雨，交通便会彻底瘫痪，我们将有好几天沦落到只能以骡肉和芒果果腹。那时，为防备这一不测，我们靠四处搜掠和囤积稍稍抢到了前头，因此当补给被断绝一两天时，我们没受多少苦，甚至还能稍稍接济一下驻扎在我们旁边、运气不如我们的第一伊利诺伊义勇团（First Illinois Regiment）。伊利诺伊团的士兵当时正开价每块 1 美元，向我们的士兵求购硬面饼。

===Original===
> I wish to bear testimony to the energy and capacity of Colonel Weston, the Commissary-General with the expedition. If it had not been for his active aid, we should have fared worse than we did. All that he could do for us, he most cheerfully did.
> As regards the clothing, I have to say: As to the first issue, the blue shirts were excellent of their kind, but altogether too hot for Cuba. They are just what I used to wear in Montana. The leggings were good; the shoes were very good; the undershirts not very good, and the drawers bad﻿—being of heavy, thick canton flannel, difficult to wash, and entirely unfit for a tropical climate. The trousers were poor, wearing badly. We did not get any other clothing until we were just about to leave Cuba, by which time most of the men were in tatters; some being actually barefooted, while others were in rags, or dressed partly in clothes captured from the Spaniards, who were much more suitably clothed for the climate and place than we were. The ponchos were poor, being inferior to the Spanish raincoats which we captured.

===Chinese===
> 我愿为远征军军需总长（Commissary-General）韦斯顿上校（Colonel Weston）的干练与才具作证。若非他多方鼎力相助，我们的境况本会更糟。凡是他能为我们做的事，他都做得极其心甘情愿。
> 至于被服装具，我须申明如下：关于首批发放的服装，蓝衬衫就其品类而言品质极佳，但在古巴穿实在热得受不了。那正是我从前在蒙大拿（Montana）惯常穿的衣裳。绑腿是好的；鞋子非常好；汗衫不很好，衬裤则很差——用的是厚重密实的绒布（canton flannel），难以洗涤，完全不适合热带气候。裤子质量低劣，极不耐磨。直到我们即将撤离古巴时，才领到其他被服，而那时大多数士兵早已衣衫褴褛：有的当真打着赤脚，有的浑身破片，或者部分穿上了从西班牙人身上缴获的衣装——论对当地气候和环境的相宜，西班牙人穿得比我们合适得多。雨披质量很差，还不如我们缴获的西班牙雨衣。

===Original===
> As to the medical matters, I invite your attention, not only to the report of Dr. Church accompanying this letter, but to the letters of Captain Llewellen, Captain Day, and Lieutenant McIlhenny. I could readily produce a hundred letters on the lines of the last three. In actual medical supplies, we had plenty of quinine and cathartics. We were apt to be short on other medicines, and we had nothing whatever in the way of proper nourishing food for our sick and wounded men during most of the time, except what we were able to get from the Red Cross or purchase with our own money. We had no hospital tent at all until I was able to get a couple of tarpaulins. During much of the time my own fly was used for the purpose. We had no cots until by individual effort we obtained a few, only three or four days before we left Cuba. During most of the time the sick men lay on the muddy ground in blankets, if they had any; if not, they lay without them until some of the well men cut their own blankets in half. Our regimental surgeon very soon left us, and Dr. Church, who was repeatedly taken down with the fever, was left alone﻿—save as he was helped by men detailed from among the troopers. Both he and the men thus detailed, together with the regular hospital attendants, did work of incalculable service. We had no ambulance with the regiment. On the battlefield our wounded were generally sent to the rear in mule-wagons, or on litters which were improvised. At other times we would hire the little springless Cuban carts. But of course the wounded suffered greatly in such conveyances, and moreover, often we could not get a wheeled vehicle of any kind to transport even the most serious cases. On the day of the big fight, July 1st, as far as we could find out, there were but two ambulances with the army in condition to work﻿—neither of which did we ever see. Later there were, as we were informed, thirteen all told; and occasionally after the surrender, by vigorous representations and requests, we would get one assigned to take some peculiarly bad cases to the hospital. Ordinarily, however, we had to do with one of the makeshifts enumerated above. On several occasions I visited the big hospitals in the rear. Their condition was frightful beyond description from lack of supplies, lack of medicine, lack of doctors, nurses, and attendants, and especially from lack of transportation. The wounded and sick who were sent back suffered so much that, whenever possible, they returned to the front. Finally my brigade commander, General Wood, ordered, with my hearty acquiescence, that only in the direst need should any men be sent to the rear﻿—no matter what our hospital accommodations at the front might be. The men themselves preferred to suffer almost anything lying alone in their little shelter-tents, rather than go back to the hospitals in the rear. I invite attention to the accompanying letter of Captain Llewellen in relation to the dreadful condition of the wounded on some of the transports taking them North.

===Chinese===
> 至于医疗事项，谨请不仅留意随本函附呈的丘奇医生（Dr. Church）的报告，也留意卢埃伦上尉（Captain Llewellen）、戴上尉（Captain Day）与麦克尔亨尼中尉（Lieutenant McIlhenny）的信函。类似后三封那样的信，我可以毫不费力地再拿出一百封。在实际的医疗物资方面，奎宁与泻药我们是不缺的。其他药品则动辄短缺，而且在大部分时间里，若不是能从红十字会得到、或用自己的钱购买，我们为伤病员准备的像样滋补食物便一无所有。我们压根没有医院帐篷，直到我弄到两块防水油布（tarpaulin）才算有了着落；其后很长一段时间里，派这个用场的一直是我自己那块天幕（fly）。我们也没有帆布床，直到离开古巴前三四天，才靠个人的努力弄到寥寥几张。大部分时间里，病号们就裹着毯子躺在泥泞的地上——倘若还有毯子的话；没有的，就一直干躺着，直到一些还康健的士兵把自己的毯子剪成两半。本团的团军医很早就离开了我们，丘奇医生虽一再被热病击倒，却独自撑持——只除了从士兵中抽来帮他的那些人。他和这些被抽调的士兵，连同正规的医护勤务员一起，做出了无可估量的贡献。我们团没有一辆救护车。在战场上，我们的伤员通常是用骡车、或临时拼凑的担架送往后方的。其余时候，我们会雇那种不带弹簧的小型古巴马车。可是伤员在这样的载具里自然吃足了苦头，何况我们常常连一辆带轮子的车都弄不到手，连最危重的病例也无法转送。打大仗的那天，即 7 月 1 日，据我们所能查明的，全军只有两辆救护车处于可用状态——这两辆我们自始至终连一面都没见过。据告知，后来总计有十三辆；投降之后，靠再三的严正交涉与恳请，偶尔能争到一辆，把几个特别危重的病例送进医院。然而平常时候，我们只好将就使用上述某一种凑合办法。有几次我探访过后方的大医院。由于缺乏物资、缺乏药品、缺乏医生、护士与看护，尤其是缺乏运输，其状况之惨，骇人得非言语所能形容。后送的伤员病号们吃尽了苦头，以致一有可能，便返回前线。最后，我的旅长伍德将军（General Wood）下令——我衷心赞同——唯有在最迫切的需要下才可后送任何士兵——不管我们前线的医院条件如何。士兵们自己也宁愿独自躺在各自的小遮蔽帐篷（shelter-tent）里忍受几乎任何事情，也不肯回到后方的医院去。谨请留意随函附上的卢埃伦上尉的信，其中述及把伤员载运北上的几艘运输船上，伤员境况之惨。

===Original===
> The greatest trouble we had was with the lack of transportation. Under the order issued by direction of General Miles through the Adjutant-General on or about May 8th, a regiment serving as infantry in the field was entitled to twenty-five wagons. We often had one, often none, sometimes two, and never as many as three. We had a regimental pack-train, but it was left behind at Tampa. During most of the time our means of transportation were chiefly the improvised pack-trains spoken of above; but as the mules got well they were taken away from us, and so were the captured Spanish cavalry horses. Whenever we shifted camp, we had to leave most of our things behind, so that the night before each fight was marked by our sleeping without tentage and with very little food, so far as officers were concerned, as everything had to be sacrificed to getting up what ammunition and medical supplies we had. Colonel Wood seized some mules, and in this manner got up the medical supplies before the fight of June 24th, when for three days the officers had nothing but what they wore. There was a repetition of this, only in worse form, before and after the fight of July 1st. Of course much of this was simply a natural incident of war, but a great deal could readily have been avoided if we had had enough transportation; and I was sorry not to let my men be as comfortable as possible and rest as much as possible just before going into a fight when, as on July 1st and 2nd, they might have to be forty-eight hours with the minimum quantity of food and sleep. The fever began to make heavy ravages among our men just before the surrender, and from that time on it became a most serious matter to shift camp, with sick and ailing soldiers, hardly able to walk﻿—not to speak of carrying heavy burdens﻿—when we had no transportation. Not more than half of the men could carry their rolls, and yet these, with the officers’ baggage and provisions, the entire hospital and its appurtenances, etc., had to be transported somehow. It was usually about three days after we reached a new camp before the necessaries which had been left behind could be brought up, and during these three days we had to get along as best we could. The entire lack of transportation at first resulted in leaving most of the troop mess-kits on the beach, and we were never able to get them. The men cooked in the few utensils they could themselves carry. This rendered it impossible to boil the drinking-water. Closely allied to the lack of transportation was the lack of means to land supplies from the transports.

===Chinese===
> 我们最大的麻烦出在运输的匮乏上。依照迈尔斯将军（General Miles）责成副官长约于 5 月 8 日发布的命令，在战场上充作步兵使用的团有权配属二十五辆大车。我们常常有一辆，常常一辆也没有，有时两辆，从未有过三辆。我们有一支团属驮队，却被留在了坦帕。大部分时间里，我们的运输手段主要就是前述那些临时拼凑的驮队；可骡子们一经医好，就被从我们手里调走，缴获的西班牙军马也遭了同样的命运。每逢转移营地，我们都不得不把大部分物件留在身后，于是每次战斗前夜都落得个露宿无帐、就军官而言几乎没有吃食的境地，因为一切都得给把仅有的弹药和医疗物资运上去让路。伍德上校强征了一批骡子，用这个办法赶在 6 月 24 日之战以前把医疗物资运了上去；那一仗过后，军官们一连三天除了身上穿的以外一无所有。7 月 1 日之战的前后，这一幕又重演了一遍，只是情形更糟。当然，这里面许多不过是战争的天然遭遇，但倘若有足够的运输工具，相当一部分本可轻易避免；令我难过的是，我不能让我的士兵们在投入战斗之前尽可能舒适、尽可能多休息——而在 7 月 1 日、2 日那样的日子，他们可能得以最少量的食物和睡眠撑过四十八个钟头。投降前夕，热病开始在我们士兵中间大肆摧残，从那时起，每当没有运输工具，带着病病歪歪、几乎走不动路——更谈不上肩挑背扛——的士兵拔营，便成了极其棘手的事。能扛得动自己行囊的士兵不到一半，然而这些行囊，连同军官的行李与给养、整座医院及其一切附属设备等等，总得设法运走。通常要到抵达新营地约三天之后，留在后面的必需品才运得上来，而这三天里我们只好尽其所能地将就。起初运输的全然匮乏，致使各连的大部分炊具被撂在了滩头，我们再也没能取回。士兵们用自己背得动的寥寥几件炊具做饭。这就使饮水无法煮沸。与运输匮乏紧密相连的，是从运输船上起卸补给的手段之匮乏。

===Original===
> In my opinion, the deficiency in transportation was the worst evil with which we had to contend, serious though some of the others were. I have never served before, so have no means of comparing this with previous campaigns. I was often told by officers who had seen service against the Indians that, relatively to the size of the army, and the character of the country, we had only a small fraction of the transportation always used in the Indian campaigns. As far as my regiment was concerned, we certainly did not have one-third of the amount absolutely necessary, if it was to be kept in fair condition, and we had to partially make good the deficiency by the most energetic resort to all kinds of makeshifts and expedients.

===Chinese===
> 依我之见，运输的匮乏是我们不得不与之搏斗的最大祸患，尽管其他某些祸患也不轻。我此前从未服过役，因此无从将此役同以往的战役作比较。屡屡有同印第安人作过战的军官告诉我，相对于这支军队的规模和这一带的地形，我们所拥有的运输工具，还不及印第安诸战役惯常用量的一小部分。就我团而论，倘若要让它保持相当的状态，我们确实连绝对必要数量的三分之一都不曾拥有；我们只能以最奋发的不懈努力，穷尽各种权宜与应急之法，来部分弥补这一亏空。

===Original===
> Yours respectfully,
> Theodore Roosevelt, 

 *Colonel First United States Cavalry*.
> Forwarded through military channels. 

===Chinese===
> 谨启，
> 西奥多·罗斯福（Theodore Roosevelt）， 

 *美国第一骑兵团上校*。
> 经军事渠道转呈。 

===Original===
 (5 enclosures.)
> First Endorsement. 

===Chinese===
 （附件 5 份。）
> 第一批注（First Endorsement）。

 **Headquarters Fifth Army Corps. 

 Camp Wikoff**, September 18, 1898.

===Chinese===
 **第五军司令部。 

 维科夫营（Camp Wikoff）**，1898 年 9 月 18 日。

> William R. Shafter, 

 *Major-General Commanding*.

> 威廉·R·沙夫特（William R. Shafter）， 

 *总指挥少将*。

**总统提名**。
晋授荣誉上校（Colonel by Brevet）。
第一义勇骑兵团中校西奥多·罗斯福，因 1898 年 6 月 24 日古巴拉斯瓜西马斯战斗中的英勇表现。
晋授荣誉准将。
第一义勇骑兵团中校西奥多·罗斯福，因 1898 年 7 月 1 日古巴圣地亚哥战斗中的英勇表现。（已获提名荣誉上校，衔阶自 1898 年 6 月 24 日起算。）

---

===Original===
> **Sir**: I have the honor to invite attention to the following list of officers and enlisted men who specially distinguished themselves in the action at Las Guasimas, Cuba, June 24, 1898.
> These officers and men have been recommended for favorable consideration by their immediate commanding officers in their respective reports, and I would respectfully urge that favorable action be taken.
> **Officers.**
> ⋮
> In First United States Volunteer Cavalry﻿—Colonel Leonard Wood, Lieutenant-Colonel Roosevelt.
> ⋮
> Respectfully,
> Joseph Wheeler, 

 *Major-General United States Volunteers, Commanding*.

===Chinese===
> **长官**：谨提请垂注下列于 1898 年 6 月 24 日古巴拉斯瓜西马斯之战中表现尤为卓异的军官与士兵名单。
> 这些官兵已由其直属指挥官在各自的报告中推荐予以从优考虑，我恳切敦请予以从优处置。
> **军官。**
> ⋮
> 美国第一义勇骑兵团——伦纳德·伍德上校、罗斯福中校。
> ⋮
> 谨启，
> 约瑟夫·惠勒，

 *美国义勇军少将，指挥官*。

---

===Original===
> **Sir**: By direction of the major-general commanding the Cavalry Division, I have the honor to submit the following report of the engagement of a part of this brigade with the enemy at Guasimas, Cuba, on June 24th, accompanied by detailed reports from the regimental and other commanders engaged, and a list of the killed and wounded:
> ⋮
> I cannot speak too highly of the efficient manner in which Colonel Wood handled his regiment, and of his magnificent behavior on the field. The conduct of Lieutenant-Colonel Roosevelt, as reported to me by my two aides, deserves my highest commendation. Both Colonel Wood and Lieutenant-Colonel Roosevelt disdained to take advantage of shelter or cover from the enemy’s fire while any of their men remained exposed to it﻿—an error of judgment, but happily on the heroic side.
> ⋮
> Very respectfully,
> S. B. M. Young, 

 *Brigadier General United States Volunteers, Commanding*.

===Chinese===
> **长官**：奉骑兵师少将指挥官之命，谨呈报本旅一部于 6 月 24 日在古巴拉斯瓜西马斯与敌接战的如下报告，并附参战各团及其他指挥官之详报与阵亡负伤人员名单：
> ⋮
> 伍德上校治军之干练，及其临阵之卓绝风范，赞之不尽。据我两位副官回报，罗斯福中校的行止亦当获我最高嘉许。伍德上校与罗斯福中校二人，只要麾下尚有一名士兵暴露于敌火之下，便不屑借掩蔽以避敌军射击——此乃审断之失，幸而失在英雄的一面。
> ⋮
> 谨致敬意，
> S·B·M·扬，

 *美国义勇军准将，指挥官*。

---

===Original===
> **Sir**: I have the honor to recommend Hon. Theodore Roosevelt, late Colonel First United States Volunteer Cavalry, for a medal of honor, as a reward for conspicuous gallantry at the battle of San Juan, Cuba, on July 1, 1898.
> Colonel Roosevelt by his example and fearlessness inspired his men, and both at Kettle Hill and the ridge known as San Juan he led his command in person. I was an eye witness of Colonel Roosevelt’s action.
> As Colonel Roosevelt has left the service, a Brevet Commission is of no particular value in his case.
> Very respectfully,
> Samuel S. Sumner, 

 *Major-General United States Volunteers*.

===Chinese===
> **长官**：谨建议授予美国第一义勇骑兵团前团长西奥多·罗斯福阁下荣誉勋章，以酬其于 1898 年 7 月 1 日古巴圣胡安之战中的显著英勇。
> 罗斯福上校以身作则、临危无惧，激励了麾下将士；无论在水壶山，还是在通称圣胡安的山岭，他都亲自率领所部冲锋陷阵。我亲眼目睹了罗斯福上校的行止。
> 罗斯福上校既已离役，荣誉委任状（Brevet Commission）于他并无特别价值。
> 谨致敬意，
> 塞缪尔·S·萨姆纳，

 *美国义勇军少将*。

---

===Original===
> **My dear Colonel**: I saw you lead the line up the first hill﻿—you were certainly the first officer to reach the top﻿—and through your efforts, and your personally jumping to the front, a line, more or less thin, but strong enough to take it, was led by you to the San Juan or first hill. In this your life was placed in extreme jeopardy, as you may recall, and as it proved by the number of dead left in that vicinity. Captain Stevens, then of the Ninth Cavalry, now of the Second Cavalry, was with you, and I am sure he recalls your gallant conduct. After the line started on the advance from the first hill, I did not see you until our line was halted, under a most galling fire, at the extreme front, where you afterwards entrenched. I spoke to you there and gave instructions from General Sumner that the position was to be held and that there would be no further advance till further orders. You were the senior officer there, took charge of the line, scolded me for having my horse so high upon the ridge; at the same time you were exposing yourself most conspicuously, while adjusting the line, for the example was necessary, as was proved when several colored soldiers﻿—about eight or ten, Twenty-fourth Infantry, I think﻿—started at a run to the rear to assist a wounded colored soldier, and you drew your revolver and put a short and effective stop to such apparent stampede﻿—it quieted them. That position was hot, and now I marvel at your escaping there﻿ ﻿…
> Very sincerely yours,
> Robert L. Howze.

===Chinese===
> **亲爱的上校**：我亲眼看见您率领战线冲上第一座山——您无疑是第一个登上山顶的军官——凭借您的努力，凭借您亲身一跃居前，一条多少有些单薄、却足以夺山的战线，由您引领着冲向了圣胡安山、也就是第一座山。在此过程中，您的性命处于极度的险境，这您自己想必记得，遗留在那一带的尸体也足资证明。史蒂文斯上尉当时与您同在，他原属第九骑兵团，如今在第二骑兵团，我确信他也记得您的英勇行止。战线自第一座山向前推进之后，直到我方战线在最炽烈的压制炮火下于最前沿停止前进——也就是您随后构筑工事之处——我才再见到您。我在那里同您说过话，并传达萨姆纳将军的指示：该阵地必须坚守，未奉后续命令不得再进。您是那里军衔最高的军官，接管了整条战线，还训了我一顿，怪我把马带到了山脊上那么高的地方；与此同时，您却在调整战线时暴露得无比显眼——这个榜样是必需的，下述一事可为佐证：几名有色士兵——约八到十人，我记得是第二十四步兵团的——跑步奔向后方，去救护一名负伤的有色士兵，您拔出左轮手枪，简短而有效地制止了这场眼见要成的溃逃——他们随即安静下来。那个阵地炮火炙热，如今您竟能从那里全身而退，我回想起来仍觉称奇……
> 谨致真挚的问候，
> 罗伯特·L·豪兹。

---

===Original===
> I hereby certify that on July 1, 1898, Colonel (then Lieutenant-Colonel) Theodore Roosevelt, First Volunteer Cavalry, distinguished himself through the action, and on two occasions during the battle when I was an eyewitness, his conduct was most conspicuous and clearly distinguished above other men, as follows:
> Robert L. Howze, 

 *Captain A.A.G., U.S.V.* 

 *(First Lieutenant Sixth United States Cavalry)*

===Chinese===
> 兹证明：1898 年 7 月 1 日，第一义勇骑兵团的西奥多·罗斯福上校（时任中校）在战斗全程表现卓异；战斗期间我两次亲历亲见，其行止最为醒目，显然高出众人一等，具证如次：
> 罗伯特·L·豪兹，

 *美国义勇军助理副官长（A.A.G.）上尉*

 *（美国第六骑兵团中尉）*

---

===Original===
> **Sir**: In compliance with the request, contained in your letter of April 30th, of the Board convened to consider the awarding of brevets, medals of honor, etc., for the Santiago Campaign, that I state any facts, within my knowledge as Adjutant-General of the Brigade in which Colonel Theodore Roosevelt served, to aid the Board in determining, in connection with Colonel Roosevelt’s application for a medal of honor, whether his conduct at Santiago was such as to distinguish him above others, I have the honor to submit the following:
> My duties on July 1, 1898, brought me in constant observation of and contact with Colonel Roosevelt from early morning until shortly before the climax of the assault of the Cavalry Division on the San Juan Hill﻿—the so-called Kettle Hill. During this time, while under the enemy’s artillery fire at El Poso, and while on the march from El Poso by the San Juan ford to the point from which his regiment moved to the assault﻿—about two miles, the greater part under fire﻿—Colonel Roosevelt was conspicuous above any others I observed in his regiment in the zealous performance of duty, in total disregard of his personal danger and in his eagerness to meet the enemy. At El Poso, when the enemy opened on that place with artillery fire, a shrapnel bullet grazed and bruised one of Colonel Roosevelt’s wrists. The incident did not lessen his hazardous exposure, but he continued so exposed until he had placed his command under cover. In moving to the assault of San Juan Hill, Colonel Roosevelt was most conspicuously brave, gallant and indifferent to his own safety. He, in the open, led his regiment; no officer could have set a more striking example to his men or displayed greater intrepidity.
> Yours respectfully,
> Your obedient servant,
> A. L. Mills, 

 *Colonel United States Army, Superintendent*.

===Chinese===
> **长官**：您 4 月 30 日来函转达了为审议圣地亚哥战役荣誉晋升（brevets）、荣誉勋章等授予事宜而召集的委员会的要求，嘱我以罗斯福上校所在旅的副官长（Adjutant-General）身份，就本人见闻陈述事实，以助委员会结合罗斯福上校的荣誉勋章申请，判定其在圣地亚哥的行为是否足以使之高出侪辈。谨遵命呈报如下：
> 1898 年 7 月 1 日，我的职责使我自清晨起直至骑兵师对圣胡安山——即所谓的水壶山——发起总攻前不久，始终处于对罗斯福上校的不断观察与接触之中。在此期间，无论是在埃尔波索身处敌军炮火之下，还是在从埃尔波索经圣胡安涉渡处向该团发起冲锋之出发地点行军的途中——约两英里，大部分路程处于炮火之下——在我所注意到的该团所有人当中，罗斯福上校都最为出众：无论是在热忱履行职责上，在完全置个人安危于不顾上，还是在求战心切上，莫不如此。在埃尔波索，敌人开炮轰击该地时，一发榴霰弹的弹丸擦过并挫伤了罗斯福上校的一只手腕。这一意外并未减少他暴露于险境的程度，他继续这般暴露，直至把所部安置于掩蔽之下。向圣胡安山发起冲锋时，罗斯福上校之勇敢、豪勇与置自身安全于度外，最为醒目。他在开阔地带亲率全团；没有哪位军官能为士兵树立更惊人的榜样，或展现出更大的无畏。
> 谨致敬意，
> 您恭顺的仆人，
> A·L·米尔斯，

 *美国陆军上校，总监*。

---

===Original===
> **Sir**: I have the honor to make the following statement relative to the conduct of Colonel Theodore Roosevelt, late First United States Volunteer Cavalry, during the assault upon San Juan Hill, July 1, 1898.
> I have already recommended this officer for a medal of honor, which I understand has been denied him, upon the ground that my previous letter was too indefinite. I based my recommendation upon the fact that Colonel Roosevelt, accompanied only by four or five men, led a very desperate and extremely gallant charge on San Juan Hill, thereby setting a splendid example to the troops and encouraging them to pass over the open country intervening between their position and the trenches of the enemy. In leading this charge, he started off first, as he supposed, with quite a following of men, but soon discovered that he was alone. He then returned and gathered up a few men and led them to the charge, as above stated. The charge in itself was an extremely gallant one, and the example set a most inspiring one to the troops in that part of the line, and while it is perfectly true that everybody finally went up the hill in good style, yet there is no doubt that the magnificent example set by Colonel Roosevelt had a very encouraging effect and had great weight in bringing up the troops behind him. During the assault, Colonel Roosevelt was the first to reach the trenches in his part of the line and killed one of the enemy with his own hand.
> I earnestly recommend that the medal be conferred upon Colonel Roosevelt, for I believe that he in every way deserves it, and that his services on the day in question were of great value and of a most distinguished character.
> Very respectfully,
> Leonard Wood, 

 *Major-General, United States Volunteers*. 

 *Commanding Department of Santiago de Cuba*.

===Chinese===
> **长官**：谨就美国第一义勇骑兵团前团长西奥多·罗斯福上校于 1898 年 7 月 1 日强攻圣胡安山时的行止，陈述如下。
> 我此前已推荐这位军官授予荣誉勋章，据悉未获批准，理由是我前一封信写得过于笼统。我的推荐所依据的事实是：罗斯福上校仅带四五名士兵，对圣胡安山发起了一次万分凶险而又极端豪勇的冲锋，从而为各部队树立了辉煌的榜样，鼓舞他们越过横亘于本队位置与敌军战壕之间的开阔地带。率队冲锋时，他自以为身后跟着不少人马而率先冲出，旋即发现只身一人。他随即折返，集合起几名士兵，如前所述率队冲锋。这次冲锋本身就极其豪勇，为该段战线上的部队树立了最振奋人心的榜样；尽管人人最终都漂漂亮亮地冲上了山，这固然千真万确，但毫无疑问，罗斯福上校树立的辉煌榜样产生了极大的鼓舞作用，对带动身后的部队居功至伟。强攻期间，罗斯福上校是他那一段战线上第一个冲抵战壕的人，并亲手击毙一名敌军。
> 我恳切建议将勋章授予罗斯福上校，因为我相信他完全当之无愧，而且他当日的功绩价值重大、卓异不凡。
> 谨致敬意，
> 伦纳德·伍德，

 *美国义勇军少将*。

 *指挥古巴圣地亚哥军区*。

---

===Original===
> **Sir**: I have the honor to recommend that a “Congressional Medal of Honor” be given to Theodore Roosevelt (late Colonel First Volunteer Cavalry), for distinguished conduct and conspicuous bravery in command of his regiment in the charge on San Juan Hill, Cuba, July 1, 1898.
> In compliance with G.O. 135, A.G.O. 1898, I enclose my certificate showing my personal knowledge of Colonel Roosevelt’s conduct.
> Very respectfully,
> C. J. Stevens, 

 *Captain Second Cavalry*.

===Chinese===
> **长官**：谨建议授予西奥多·罗斯福（第一义勇骑兵团前团长）「国会荣誉勋章」（Congressional Medal of Honor），以酬其于 1898 年 7 月 1 日在古巴指挥本团冲锋圣胡安山时的卓异品行与显著英勇。
> 兹依照 1898 年副官长署（A.G.O.）第 135 号通令（G.O. 135），随函附上证书一份，陈述我亲见的罗斯福上校之行止。
> 谨致敬意，
> C·J·史蒂文斯，

 *第二骑兵团上尉*。

===Original===
> I hereby certify that on July 1, 1898, at the battle of San Juan, Cuba, I witnessed Colonel (then Lieutenant-Colonel) Roosevelt, First Volunteer Cavalry, United States of America, mounted, leading his regiment in the charge on San Juan. By his gallantry and strong personality he contributed most materially to the success of the charge of the Cavalry Division up San Juan Hill.
> Colonel Roosevelt was among the first to reach the crest of the hill, and his dashing example, his absolute fearlessness and gallant leading rendered his conduct conspicuous and clearly distinguished above other men.
> C. J. Stevens, 

 *Captain Second Cavalry*. 

 *(Late First Lieutenant Ninth Cavalry.)*

===Chinese===
> 兹证明：1898 年 7 月 1 日，在古巴圣胡安之战中，我亲见美利坚合众国第一义勇骑兵团的罗斯福上校（时任中校）骑乘于马上，率领全团冲锋圣胡安。凭借其豪勇与刚毅的人格，他为骑兵师冲上圣胡安山的胜利作出了极其重大的贡献。
> 罗斯福上校属最先抵达山顶者之列；他勇往直前的表率、全然的无所畏惧与豪勇的率领，使其行止醒目出众，显然高出众人一等。
> C·J·史蒂文斯，

 *第二骑兵团上尉*。

 *（原第九骑兵团中尉）*
