===Original===
> **Sir**: Believing that information relating to superior conduct on the part of any of the higher officers who participated in the Spanish-American War (and which information may not have been given) would be appreciated by the Department over which you preside, I have the honor to call your attention to the part borne by Colonel Theodore Roosevelt, of the late First United States Volunteer Cavalry, in the battle of July 1st last. I do this not only because I think you ought to know, but hecause his regiment as a whole were very proud of his splendid actions that day and believe they call for that most coveted distinction of the American officer, the Medal of Honor. Held in support, he brought his regiment, at exactly the right time, not only up to the line of regulars, but went through them and headed, on horseback, the charge on Kettle Hill; this being done on his own initiative, the regulars as well as his own men following. He then headed the charge on the next hill, both regulars and the First United States Volunteer Cavalry following. He was so near the intrenchments on the second hill, that he shot and killed with a revolver one of the enemy before they broke completely. He then led the cavalry on the chain of hills overlooking Santiago, where he remained in charge of all the cavalry that was at the extreme front for the rest of that day and night. His unhesitating gallantry in taking the initiative against intrenchments lined by men armed with rapid fire guns certainly won him the highest consideration and admiration of all who witnessed his conduct throughout that day.
> What I here write I can bear witness to from personally having seen.
> Very respectfully,
> M. J. Jenkins, 

 *Major Late First United States Cavalry*.

===Chinese===
> **长官**：敝见以为，凡有关美西战争（Spanish-American War）中高级军官卓异行止的情报（若有尚未呈报者），必为您所主持的部所乐闻，故谨提请您关注原美国第一义勇骑兵团的西奥多·罗斯福上校在去年 7 月 1 日之战中所担当的角色。我此举不仅因为此事您理当知情，更因为他全团上下无不为其当日辉煌的作为深感自豪，并相信这些作为足以当得起美国军官最渴慕的殊荣——荣誉勋章。当时全团留作支援兵力，他把握恰好的时机，不仅把本团带进了正规军的战线，而且穿队而过，策马率先冲锋水壶山；此举全出于他的主动，正规军连同他自己的人马都随之跟进。接着他又率队冲锋下一座山，正规军与美国第一义勇骑兵团一同跟上。他距第二座山上的战壕如此之近，以致在守军彻底崩溃之前，他便用左轮手枪击毙了一名敌人。随后他率领骑兵登上俯瞰圣地亚哥的连环山岭，当天白昼余下的时间和整夜里，他都在那里统辖处于最前沿的全部骑兵。面对由持速射枪械的士兵列守的战壕，他毫不犹豫地率先出击，这份豪勇确实为他赢得了所有目睹其当日行止者的最高敬重与叹服。
> 我在此写下的一切，均系我亲眼所见，可以作证。
> 谨致敬意，
> M·J·詹金斯，

 *美国第一义勇骑兵团前少校*。

---

===Original===
> I was Colonel Roosevelt’s orderly at the battle of San Juan Hill, and from that time on until our return to Montauk Point. I was with him all through the fighting, and believe I was the only man who was always with him, though during part of the time Lieutenants Ferguson and Greenwald were also close to him. He led our regiment forward on horseback until he came to the men of the Ninth Cavalry lying down. He led us through these and they got up and joined us. He gave the order to charge on Kettle Hill, and led us on horseback up the hill, both Rough Riders and the Ninth Cavalry. He was the first on the hill, I being very nearly alongside of him. Some Spanish riflemen were coming out of the intrenchments and he killed one with his revolver. He took the men on to the crest of the hill and bade them begin firing on the blockhouse on the hill to our left, the one the infantry were attacking. When we took it, he gave the order to charge, and led the troops on Kettle Hill forward against the blockhouse on our front. He then had charge of all the cavalry on the hills overlooking Santiago, where we afterwards dug our trenches. He had command that afternoon and night, and for the rest of the time commanded our regiment at this point.
> Yours very truly,
> H. P. Bardshar

===Chinese===
> 圣胡安山之战时，我是罗斯福上校的勤务兵，此后一直做到我们返回蒙托克角为止。整个战斗期间我都在他身边，而且我相信，始终不离他左右的唯有我一人——尽管有一段时间，弗格森中尉与格林沃尔德中尉也曾紧随其侧。他策马率领我团前进，直到遇见卧倒在地的第九骑兵团士兵。他带领我们从他们中间穿过，他们便起身加入了我们。他下达了冲锋水壶山的命令，策马率我们冲上山去，莽骑兵与第九骑兵团并肩同冲。他第一个登上山头，我几乎与他并辔而行。几名西班牙步枪手正从战壕里爬出来，他用左轮手枪击毙了其中一人。他把士兵们带向山顶，命令他们开始射击我们左面山上的碉堡，就是步兵正在攻打的那一座。拿下它之后，他下令冲锋，率领水壶山上的部队扑向我们正面的碉堡。随后，他统辖俯瞰圣地亚哥各山上的全部骑兵，我们后来便在那里掘壕据守。那天下午和夜里均由他执掌指挥，此后一段时间，我团在该处亦归他节制。
> 谨此，
> H·P·巴德沙尔

---

===Original===
> **Dear Sir**: At your request, I send you the following extracts from my diary, and from notes taken on the day of the assault on San Juan. I kept in my pocket a small pad on which incidents were noted daily from the landing until the surrender. On the day of the fight notes were taken just before Grimes fired his first gun, just after the third reply from the enemy﻿—when we were massed in the road about seventy paces from Grimes’ guns, and when I was beginning to get scared and to think I would be killed﻿—at the halt just before you advanced, and under the shelter of the hills in the evening. Each time that notes were taken, the page was put in an envelope addressed to my wife. At the first chance they were mailed to her, and on my arrival in the United States the story of the fight, taken from these notes, was entered in the diary I keep in a book. I make this lengthy explanation that you may see that everything put down was fresh in my memory.
> I quote from my diary: “The tension on the men was great. Suddenly a line of men appeared coming from our right. They were advancing through the long grass, deployed as skirmishers and were under fire. At their head, or rather in front of them and leading them, rode Colonel Roosevelt. He was very conspicuous, mounted as he was. The men were the ‘Rough Riders,’ so-called. I heard someone calling to them not to fire into us, and seeing Colonel Carrol, reported to him, and was told to go out and meet them, and caution them as to our position, we being between them and the enemy. I did so, speaking to Colonel Roosevelt. I also told him we were under orders not to advance, and asked him if he had received any orders. He replied that he was going to charge the Spanish trenches. I told this to Colonel Carrol, and to Captain Dimmick, our squadron commander. A few moments after the word passed down that our left (Captain Taylor) was about to charge. Captain McBlain called out, ‘we must go in with those troops; we must support Taylor.’ I called this to Captain Dimmick, and he gave the order to assault.”

===Chinese===
> **亲爱的先生**：应您的要求，谨寄上我日记中的若干摘录，以及强攻圣胡安当天所作的笔记。从登陆直到敌军投降，我口袋里一直揣着一个小本子，每日把种种事件记下。交战当天记笔记的时刻有：格赖姆斯（Grimes）发射第一炮之前；敌人第三次还击之后——当时我们集结在路上，距格赖姆斯的炮位约七十步，我正开始心惊，暗想自己性命休矣——在您推进之前全军停止的那一会儿；以及傍晚在山峦掩蔽之下。每记一次，便把那页纸装入写给我妻子的信封。一有机会便寄出；待我抵达合众国，便依据这些笔记把战斗始末录入我那本装订成册的日记。我作这番冗长的说明，是想让您明白，落笔的一切在我记忆中都还鲜明。
> 现从日记中引述：「士兵们的紧张到了极点。忽然，一队人自我方右翼出现。他们正穿过高草推进，以散兵线（skirmishers）展开，且身处炮火之下。走在队首——毋宁说，走在队伍前面引着队伍——的，是骑在马上的罗斯福上校。他这样骑在马上，十分显眼。这些士兵就是号称『莽骑兵』的部队。我听见有人朝他们喊话，叫他们不要朝我们开火；我见到卡罗尔上校，向他报告，奉命出列迎上前去，提醒他们注意我方位置，因为我们正夹在他们与敌人之间。我照办了，同罗斯福上校讲了话。我还告诉他，我们奉命不得推进，并问他是否接到过什么命令。他回答说，他就要去冲击西班牙人的战壕。我把这话报告了卡罗尔上校，也报告了我们的中队长迪米克上尉（Captain Dimmick）。片刻之后，口令传了下来：我方左翼（泰勒上尉）即将冲锋。麦克布莱恩上尉（Captain McBlain）喊道：『我们必须跟这些部队一起上；我们必须支援泰勒。』我把这话传达给迪米克上尉，他随即下达了强攻的命令。」

===Original===
> “The cheer was taken up and taken up again, on the left, and in the distance it rolled on and on. And so we started. Colonel Roosevelt, of the Rough Riders, started the whole movement on the left, which was the first advance of the assault.”
> The following is taken from my notes and was hastily jotted down on the field: “The Rough Riders came in line﻿—Colonel Roosevelt said he would assault﻿—Taylor joined them with his troop﻿—McBlain called to Dimmick, ‘let us go, we must go to support them.’ Dimmick said all right﻿—and so, with no orders, we went in.”
> I find many of my notes are illegible from perspiration. My authority for saying Taylor went in with you, “joined with his troop” was the word passed to me and repeated to Captain Dimmick that Taylor was about to charge with you. I could not see his troop. I have not put it in my diary, but in another place I have noted that Colonel Carrol, who was acting as brigade commander, told me to ask you if you had any orders.

===Chinese===
> 「欢呼在左翼一浪接一浪地响起，在远处滚滚不息。我们就这样出发了。莽骑兵的罗斯福上校发动了左翼的全部行动，那正是此次强攻的第一波推进。」
> 下面一段取自我的笔记，是在战场上匆匆记下的：「莽骑兵成一线开来——罗斯福上校说他要冲击——泰勒率他的连与他们会合——麦克布莱恩向迪米克喊：『走，我们必须去支援他们。』迪米克说好——就这样，没有命令，我们冲了进去。」
> 我发觉许多笔记被汗水洇得难以辨认。我说泰勒是同你们一起冲进去、「率他的连会合」，其依据是当时传到我这里、又由我转告迪米克上尉的口信，称泰勒即将与您一同冲锋。我未能看见他的连。这一点我没有写进日记，但在另一处我记下了：代理旅长之职的卡罗尔上校曾吩咐我去问您，是否接到过什么命令。

===Original===
> I have the honor to be,
> Very respectfully,
> Your obedient servant,
> Henry Anson Barber, 

 *Captain Twenty-Eighth Infantry*, 

 *(formerly of Ninth Cavalry.)*

===Chinese===
> 谨启，
> 谨致敬意，
> 您恭顺的仆人，
> 亨利·安森·巴伯，

 *第二十八步兵团上尉*，

 *（原属第九骑兵团）*

## Appendix E / 附录 E

===Original===
> **Dear Mr. President**: As some discussion has arisen in the public prints regarding the battle of San Juan, Cuba, July 1, 1898, and your personal movements during that day have been the subject of comment, it may not be amiss in me to state some facts coming under my personal observation as Commanding General of the Cavalry Division, of which your regiment formed a part. It will, perhaps, be advisable to show first how I came to be in command, in order that my statement may have due weight as an authoritative statement of facts: I was placed in command of the Cavalry Division on the afternoon of June 30th by General Shafter; the assignment was made owing to the severe illness of General Wheeler, who was the permanent commander of said Division. Brigadier General Young, who commanded the Second Cavalry Brigade, of which your regiment﻿—the First Volunteer Cavalry﻿—formed a part, was also very ill, and I found it necessary to relieve him from command and place Colonel Wood, of the Rough Riders, in command of the Brigade; this change placed you in command of your regiment.

===Chinese===
> **总统先生**：报端就 1898 年 7 月 1 日的古巴圣胡安之战已有若干议论，您当日的一应行止又成为众人评说的题目。那么，由我来陈述若干亲见的事实，或不为无益——我时任骑兵师（Cavalry Division）师长，您的团正是该师所属部队。为使我这篇陈述作为权威事实之谈具有应有的分量，或许宜先说明我如何受命指挥：6 月 30 日下午，沙夫特将军（General Shafter）委我指挥骑兵师；此番委任，缘于该师原任师长惠勒将军（General Wheeler）病重。指挥第二骑兵旅（Second Cavalry Brigade）的扬准将（Brigadier General Young）——您的团，即第一义勇骑兵团（First Volunteer Cavalry），隶属该旅——同样病得不轻，我只得解除他的指挥，改派莽骑兵（Rough Riders）的伍德上校（Colonel Wood）接掌该旅；这一变动，便使您得以执掌您的团。

===Original===
> The Division moved from its camp on the evening of June 30th, and bivouacked at and about El Poso. I saw you personally in the vicinity of El Poso, about 8 a.m., July 1st. I saw you again on the road leading from El Poso to the San Juan River; you were at the head of your regiment, which was leading the Second Brigade, and immediately behind the rear regiment of the First Brigade. My orders were to turn to the right at San Juan River and take up a line along that stream and try and connect with General Lawton, who was to engage the enemy at El Caney. On reaching the river we came under the fire of the Spanish forces posted on San Juan Ridge and Kettle Hill. The First Brigade was faced to the front in line as soon as it had cleared the road, and the Second Brigade was ordered to pass in rear of the first, and face to the front when clear of the First Brigade. This movement was very difficult owing to the heavy undergrowth, and the regiments became more or less tangled up, but eventually the formation was accomplished, and the Division stood in an irregular line along the San Juan River, the Second Brigade on the right. We were subjected to a heavy fire from the forces on San Juan Ridge and Kettle Hill; our position was untenable, and it became necessary to assault the enemy or fall back. Kettle Hill was immediately in front of the Cavalry, and it was determined to assault that hill. The First Brigade was ordered forward, and the Second Brigade was ordered to support the attack; personally, I accompanied a portion of the Tenth Cavalry, Second Brigade, and the Rough Riders were to the right. This brought your regiment to the right of the house which was at the summit of the hill. Shortly after I reached the crest of the hill you came to me, accompanied, I think, by Captain C. J. Stevens, of the Ninth Cavalry. We were then in a position to see the line of intrenchments along San Juan Ridge, and could see Kent’s Infantry Division engaged on our left, and Hawkins’ assault against Fort San Juan. You asked me for permission to move forward and assault San Juan Ridge. I gave you the order in person to move forward, and I saw you move forward and assault San Juan Ridge with your regiment and portions of the First and Tenth Cavalry belonging to your Brigade. I held a portion of the Second Brigade as a reserve on Kettle Hill, not knowing what force the enemy might have in reserve behind the ridge. The First Brigade also moved forward and assaulted the ridge to the right of Fort San Juan. There was a small lake between Kettle Hill and San Juan Ridge, and in moving forward your command passed to the right of this lake. This brought you opposite a house on San Juan Ridge﻿—not Fort San Juan proper, but a frame house surrounded by an earthwork. The enemy lost a number of men at this point, whose bodies lay in the trenches. Later in the day I rode along the line, and, as I recall it, a portion of the Tenth Cavalry was immediately about this house, and your regiment occupied an irregular semicircular position along the ridge and immediately to the right of the house. You had pickets out to your front; and several hundred yards to your front the Spaniards had a heavy outpost occupying a house, with rifle pits surrounding it. Later in the day, and during the following day, the various regiments forming the Division were rearranged and brought into tactical formation, the First Brigade on the left and immediately to the right of Fort San Juan, and the Second Brigade on the right of the First.

===Chinese===
> 6 月 30 日傍晚，骑兵师开拔离营，在埃尔波索（El Poso）及其周边露营。7 月 1 日上午 8 时左右，我在埃尔波索附近亲眼见到您。其后，在由埃尔波索通往圣胡安河（San Juan River）的路上，我又见到您；您走在本团最前面，本团担任第二旅的先头，紧跟在第一旅殿后那个团的身后。我奉到的命令是：在圣胡安河畔右转，沿该河一线展开，并设法与劳顿将军（General Lawton）取得联络——他负责在埃尔卡内（El Caney）与敌交战。抵达河边时，我们便落入据守圣胡安山岭（San Juan Ridge）与水壶山（Kettle Hill）的西班牙军队的火力之下。第一旅一让开道路，随即面向前方列成横队；第二旅奉命从第一旅身后通过，待完全越过第一旅后再面向前方。灌木丛生，这一机动极为困难，各团多少纠缠到了一起，但阵形终告完成，全师沿圣胡安河摆成一条不规则的战线，第二旅居右。我们遭到圣胡安山岭和水壶山上敌军的猛烈射击；我们的阵地无法立足，若不强攻敌军，便只得后撤。水壶山正当骑兵正前方，于是决定强攻此山。第一旅奉命前进，第二旅奉命支援攻击；我本人随第二旅第十骑兵团（Tenth Cavalry）的一部行动，莽骑兵在右。这就把您的团带到了山顶那所房子的右侧。我登上山巅后不久，您来到我处，随行的好像还有第九骑兵团（Ninth Cavalry）的 C·J·史蒂文斯上尉（Captain C. J. Stevens）。我们当时所在的位置，正可望见沿圣胡安山岭展开的壕堑防线，也看得见肯特（Kent）的步兵师在我们左翼接战，看得见霍金斯（Hawkins）对圣胡安堡（Fort San Juan）的强攻。您请求我准许您前进，强攻圣胡安山岭。我当面下达了前进命令，而且亲眼看到您率本团，连同您旅所属第一、第十骑兵团的各一部，向前推进，强攻圣胡安山岭。我把第二旅的一部分留作水壶山上的预备队，因为不知山岭背后敌军还握有多少预备兵力。第一旅也随之推进，强攻圣胡安堡右侧的山岭。水壶山与圣胡安山岭之间有一小湖，您部推进时从湖的右侧绕行。这就把您带到了圣胡安山岭上一所房子的对面——那不是圣胡安堡本堡，而是一所四周环着土工的木板房。敌军在这处阵地折损了若干人，尸身横陈于壕堑之中。当天晚些时候，我沿线骑巡，据我记忆所及，第十骑兵团的一部分就在这所房子近旁，而您的团沿山岭占据一个不规则的半圆形阵地，紧挨在房子右侧。您的前方派有警戒哨（pickets）；您前方数百码处，西班牙人（Spaniards）设有一处坚固前哨，占据一所房子，四下环绕着散兵壕（rifle pits）。当天晚些时候及次日一整天，组成本师的各团重新调整部署，结成战术队形：第一旅居左，紧靠圣胡安堡右侧；第二旅在第一旅之右。

===Original===
> This was the position occupied by the Cavalry Division until the final surrender of the Spanish forces, on July 17, 1898.

> In conclusion allow me to say, that I saw you, personally, at about 8 a.m., at El Poso; later, on the road to San Juan River; later, on the summit of Kettle Hill, immediately after its capture by the Cavalry Division. I saw you move forward with your command to assault San Juan Ridge, and I saw you on San Juan Ridge, where we visited your line together, and you explained to me the disposition of your command.

===Chinese===
> 骑兵师占据这一阵地，直到 1898 年 7 月 17 日西班牙军队最终投降为止。

> 最后请允许我说明：我亲眼见过您——上午 8 时左右，在埃尔波索；其后，在通往圣胡安河的路上；再后，在水壶山顶上，就在骑兵师攻占该山之后不久。我看见您率所部前进，强攻圣胡安山岭；我也在圣胡安山岭上见过您，我们在那里一同巡视了您的战线，您向我说明了您所部的部署。

===Original===
> I am, sir, with much respect,

> Your obedient servant,

> Samuel S. Sumner, 

 *Major-General United States Army*.

===Chinese===
> 谨此，长官，并致崇高敬意，

> 您恭顺的仆人，

> 塞缪尔·S·萨姆纳，

 *美国陆军少将*。

## Appendix F / 附录 F

===Original===
[From a brief speech made to his men by Colonel Roosevelt after Sunday services on September 4, 1898.]

I have waited this opportunity of speaking to you before we parted. I feel, and I know you all feel, that we are knit together by ties that can only be severed by death. I care much for the officers; I care even more for the men who make up the rank and file. Our trials, our hardships, our victories, we have all shared together, officers and men. There has been no distinction; we have all worked for the honor of the regiment. The men who were left in Florida did their duty as well as the men who went to Cuba, and all did it without a murmur.

What we have done only calls us to renewed exertion in the future. I know you are not likely simply to rest on your laurels. Life is a constant struggle, and no man can afford to remain idle, to rely in the present upon the deeds of an ever-fading past. After the first fight in Cuba you did not give up and rest on what you had done. On the contrary, you had gotten in touch with the Spaniards once, and each one of you had registered a vow that no one should get ahead of him the next time; and accordingly the next time you did even better.

===Chinese===
［摘自罗斯福上校 1898 年 9 月 4 日主日礼拜后对部下所作的简短讲话。］

我一直在等待这个机会，在分别之前同你们讲几句话。我感到——也知道你们大家都感到——我们被一种纽带联结在一起，唯有死亡才能割断。我极看重各位军官，更看重组成这支队伍的普通士兵。我们的磨难、我们的艰辛、我们的胜利，官兵上下共同分担，不分彼此；我们都是为了本团的荣誉出力。留在佛罗里达的人，与开赴古巴的人一样恪尽职守，而且人人做得毫无怨言。

我们已经做成的事，只会召唤我们在未来付出新的努力。我知道你们不至于就此躺在功劳簿上。人生是一场持续的奋斗，谁也闲不起，谁也不能在今日仰仗着日渐褪色的昨日之功过活。在古巴的第一仗打完之后，你们没有就此歇手，没有满足于既有的成绩。恰恰相反，你们同西班牙人（Spaniards）交过一回手了，每个人都暗暗立下誓言：下回绝不让任何人跑到自己前头；于是下一仗，你们打得更加漂亮。

===Original===
Carry that same sentiment and spirit into your life when you separate for your homes. Every man has felt in the past that the honor of the regiment was in his keeping, and that he reflected honor or dishonor on all by his own individual acts. Now, in peace, let each of you have the same feeling for the nation as a whole. Let us so act that at the end of twenty years those of us who can look back will see that each man has prospered, has become a better man, a better American; that we have shown ourselves as capable to fight the battles of peace as of war.

The world will be kind to you for about ten days; until then everything you do will be considered right. After that you will be judged by a stricter code; and if you prove worthless you will be deemed to have been spoiled by the war. For just about ten days you will be overpraised, over-petted; then you will find that the hero-business is over for good and all; and if you try to trade any longer on what you have done in Cuba, you will merely excite the laughter of derision. You will do well to remember this; and each turn to his allotted task with all his heart and strength, to win success in the only way it can be won, asking no consideration because of the past, but demanding to be judged each on his merits in the actual work of the day.

===Chinese===
待到各自还乡、就此分别，请把这份情感和这份精神带进你们的人生。过去，每个人都感到本团的荣誉系于自己一身，自己的一举一动，为全体增添荣光或招来耻辱。如今到了和平年代，愿你们每个人对整个国家也怀有同样的情感。愿我们这样行事为人：二十年之后，回首往事的人将会看到，我们每一个人都有所成就，都成了更好的人、更好的美国人；将会看到，我们打和平年代的仗，同打真刀真枪的仗一样在行。

世人对你们大约会客气十天；在这十天里，你们做什么都会被认为是对的。过后，你们就要接受一套更严格的标准的评判；倘若你们日后一无所成，人家便会认定你们是被这场战争毁掉了。大约也就十天工夫，你们会被捧过头、宠过头；随后你们就会发现，「英雄」这门行当已经永远收场；倘若还想靠在古巴的那点旧功讨好处，你们只会招来一片讪笑。你们最好把这一点记在心里，各自全心全力去干分内的差事，用唯一可行的办法赢得成功——不因过去而要求优待，只要求在眼前实实在在的工作中，各凭本事接受评判。

===Original===
[Response of Colonel Roosevelt in accepting an equestrian bronze, Camp Wikofi, Montauk Point, NY, September 13, 1898.]

**Officers and Men:**

I really do not know what to say to you. Nothing could possibly happen that would touch and please me as this has touched and pleased me. Trooper Murphy said rightly that my men were nearest my heart, for while I know I need not say to my officers in what a deep regard I hold them, they will not mind my saying that just a little bit closer come the men. I have never tried to coddle you and have never hesitated to call upon you to spend your best blood like water. But of course I tried to do all I could for you, and you are the best judges as to whether I have succeeded or not.

===Chinese===
［罗斯福上校接受骑马铜像时的答词，1898 年 9 月 13 日于纽约州蒙托克角维科夫营（Camp Wikofi）。］

**军官们，士兵们：**

我真不知道该对你们说些什么。世上再不可能有任何事，能像这件事一样令我感动、令我欣喜。士兵墨菲（Trooper Murphy）说得对：我的士兵离我的心最近。我深知，不必向各位军官表白我是何等敬重他们；他们也不会介意我补上一句——士兵们同我的心贴得还要更近一分。我从不曾娇惯你们，也从不曾犹豫要不要你们把一腔热血当水一样抛洒。当然，我尽力为你们做了我能做的一切；做得成没成，你们自己是最有资格的评判者。

===Original===
I am proud of this regiment beyond measure. I am proud of it because it is a typical American regiment. The foundation of the regiment was the cowpuncher, and we have him here in bronze. No gift could have been so appropriate as this bronze by Frederic Remington. The men of the West and Southwest, horsemen, riflemen, and herders, have been the backbone of this regiment, which demonstrates that Uncle Sam has another reserve of fighting men to call upon, if the necessity arises. The West stands ready to give tens of thousands of men like you, and we are only samples of the fighters the West can put forth.

Besides the cowpuncher, this regiment contained men from every section of the country, every State in the Union, and because of that we feel proud of it. It is primarily an American regiment, and it is American because it is composed of all the races which have made America their country by adoption and those who have claimed it as their country by inheritance. It gives me extreme pleasure to look around among you and see men of every occupation, men of means and men who work with their hands for a livelihood, and at the same time know that I have you for friends. You are men of widely different pursuits, yet you stand here side by side; you fought shoulder to shoulder. No man asked quarter for himself, and each one went in to show that he was as good as his neighbor. It shows the American spirit. You cannot imagine how proud I am of your friendship and regard.

===Chinese===
我为这个团感到无以复加的骄傲。我骄傲，因为它是一支典型的美国团队。这个团的根基是牧牛人（cowpuncher），如今他便以青铜之身立在我们中间。再没有哪件礼物，能比弗雷德里克·雷明顿（Frederic Remington）这尊青铜像更贴切的了。西部和西南部的男儿——骑手、枪手、牧人——一直是这个团的脊梁；这证明了一旦需要，山姆大叔（Uncle Sam）还有另一支可以征召的战斗兵员后备。西部随时拿得出数以万计像你们这样的人，我们不过是西部所能推出的战士中的样品罢了。

除了牧牛人，这个团还有来自全国各地、合众国每一个州的男儿，正因如此，我们为它自豪。它首先是一个美国的团；它之所以是美国的团，是因为它由各族之人组成——既有把美国选作归化之国的各族人民，也有把美国认作承袭祖业之国的人们。环顾你们之间，看到各行各业的人，看到有资财的人，也看到靠双手劳作谋生的人，同时又知道我把你们引为朋友，这使我快慰极了。你们从事的行当千差万别，此刻却并肩而立；你们曾经并肩作战。没有一个人为自己讨过饶，每个人上阵，都是要证明自己不输于身旁的人。这体现的正是美国精神。你们无法想象，你们的友谊与敬重令我多么骄傲。

===Original===
I have also a profound respect for you, because you have fighting qualities, and because you had the qualities which enabled us to get you into the fight. Outside of my own immediate family, as I said before, I shall never know as strong ties as with you. I am more than pleased that you feel the same way toward me. I realized when I took charge of you that I was taking upon myself a great responsibility. I cared for you as individuals, but did not forget that at any moment it might be necessary to sacrifice the individual for the whole. You would have scorned a commander who would have hesitated to expose you to any risk. I was bound that no other regiment should get any nearer to the Spanish lines than you got, and I do not think any did.

We parted with many in the fight who could ill be spared, and I think that the most vivid memories that we take away with us will be of those whom we left under Cuban sod and those who died in hospitals here in the United States﻿—the men who died from wounds, and the men who, with the same devotion to country, died from disease. I cannot mention all the names now, but those of Capron, O’Neill, and Fish will serve. They were men who died in the pride of their youthful strength.

===Chinese===
对你们，我还怀有深深的敬意，因为你们具有战斗的品格，也因为你们具备那种使我们得以把你们带上战场的素质。正如我先前所说，除了自己的至亲骨肉，我此生再不会知道有比同你们之间更牢固的情谊了。你们对我也有同感，这令我喜出望外。接掌你们之时，我便明白自己担起的是何等重大的责任。我关爱你们每一个人，却没有忘记：为了整体，随时都可能需要牺牲个人。倘若一位指挥官在让你们面对危险时犹疑不定，你们只会瞧不起他。我抱定一条：绝不容任何别的团比你们更逼近西班牙人的战线——而且我相信，没有哪个团做到过。

战斗中，我们还同许多万难割舍的弟兄永别了。我想，我们带走的记忆中，最鲜活的将是这样一些人：一些被我们留在古巴泥土之下的袍泽，一些殁于合众国此地医院中的袍泽——有的死于战伤，有的怀着同样的报国赤诚死于疾病。他们的名字，我此刻无法一一列举，但卡普伦（Capron）、奥尼尔（O’Neill）和菲什（Fish）几个名字足以代表。他们都是在青春豪强的顶点上死去的人。

===Original===
Now, just a word more I want to say to some of the men I see standing around not of your number. I refer to the troopers of the regular cavalry regiments; the white troopers; and the colored troopers. The latter the Spaniards called “smoked Yankees,” but we found them to be an excellent breed of Yankee. I am sure that I speak the sentiments of every man and officer in this assemblage when I say that between you and the other cavalry regiments there is a tie which we trust will never be broken.

I would have been deeply touched if the officers had given me this testimonial, but coming from you, my men, I appreciate it tenfold. It comes to me from you who shared the hardships of the campaign with me; who gave me a piece of your hardtack when I had none; and who shared with me your blankets when I had none to lie upon. To have such a gift come from this peculiarly American regiment touches me more than I can say. This is something I shall hand down to my children, and I shall value it more than I do the weapons I carried through the campaign.

===Chinese===
最后，我还想对几位站在周围、却不在你们之列的士兵多说一句。我指的是正规骑兵团的士兵们——白人士兵，以及有色士兵。西班牙人把后者称作「烟熏扬基佬」（smoked Yankees），而我们发现，他们正是扬基中一支极出色的血脉。我确信，此刻我道出的正是全场每一位士兵和军官的心声：在你们和其他骑兵团之间，存在着一条纽带，我们深愿它永不断裂。

这份纪念物若出自军官们之手，我已会深受感动；而它出自你们——我的士兵——之手，我对它的珍重更十倍于此。它是你们送我的：你们与我共历了战役的艰辛；我没有硬面饼（hardtack）的时候，你们掰一块给我；我没有毯子可卧的时候，你们与我合盖。这样一件礼物，出自这个独具美国特色的团，给我的触动非言语所能表达。这件东西，我将传给儿女；我对它的珍视，将胜过对随我走过整场战役的那几件武器的珍视。

## Appendix C Corrections / 附录 C 勘误



===Original===
It has been suggested to me that when Bucky O’Neill spoke of the vultures tearing our dead, he was thinking of no modern poet, but of the words of the prophet Ezekiel: “Speak unto every feathered fowl﻿ ﻿… ye shall eat the flesh of the mighty and drink the blood of the princes of the earth.”

At San Juan the Sixth Cavalry was under Major Lebo, a tried and gallant officer. I learn from a letter of Lieutenant McNamee that it was he, and not Lieutenant Hartwick, by whose orders the troopers of the Ninth cast down the fence to enable me to ride my horse into the lane. But one of the two lieutenants of B troop was overcome by the heat that day; Lieutenant Rynning was with his troop until dark.

One night during the siege, when we were digging trenches, a curious stampede occurred (not in my own regiment) which it may be necessary some time to relate.

Lieutenants W. E. Shipp and W. H. Smith were killed, not far from each other, while gallantly leading their troops on the slope of Kettle Hill. Each left a widow and young children.

===Chinese===
有人向我提议说，巴基·奥尼尔（Bucky O'Neill）讲到秃鹫撕食我们的死者时，心里想到的并非哪位现代诗人，而是先知以西结（Ezekiel）的话：「你要对各样的飞鸟说……你们必吃勇士的肉，喝地上众首领的血。」

圣胡安之战中，第六骑兵团由利博少校（Major Lebo）指挥，他是一位久经沙场的英勇军官。我从麦克纳米中尉（Lieutenant McNamee）的一封信中得知，下令叫第九骑兵团的士兵推倒栅栏、好让我骑马进入洼路的，是他，而不是哈特威克中尉（Lieutenant Hartwick）。不过那天，B 连的两名中尉中有一人中暑病倒；伦宁中尉（Lieutenant Rynning）则同自己的连队相守，直到天黑。

围城期间的一个夜晚，正当我们挖战壕时，（我团之外的）部队里发生了一场莫名其妙的自相惊扰，此事日后也许有必要专门写一写。

希普中尉（W. E. Shipp）与史密斯中尉（W. H. Smith）在水壶山山坡上英勇率领各自的连队冲锋时阵亡，两人倒下之处相距不远。他们身后都撇下了遗孀和年幼的孩子。

---

===Original===
Captain (now Colonel) A. L. Mills, the Brigade Adjutant-General, has written me some comments on my account of the fight on July 1st. It was he himself who first brought me word to advance. I then met Colonel Dorst﻿—who bore the same message﻿—as I was getting the regiment forward. Captain Mills was one of the officers I had sent back to get orders that would permit me to advance; he met General Sumner, who gave him the orders, and he then returned to me. In a letter to me Colonel Mills says in part:

===Chinese===
旅副官长（Brigade Adjutant-General）米尔斯上尉（Captain A. L. Mills，现已是上校）致信给我，就我对 7 月 1 日之战的记述提出了一些意见。最先给我带来前进口令的正是他本人。随后，当我在催动全团向前时，又遇到了多斯特中校（Colonel Dorst）——他带来的是同一道命令。米尔斯上尉是我派回去领取准许前进之命令的军官之一；他见到了萨姆纳将军（General Sumner），从将军手中接过命令后便回到我这里。米尔斯上校在写给我的信中有一段说道：

===Original===
> I reached the head of the regiment as you came out of the lane and gave you the orders to enter the action. These were that you were to move, with your right resting along the wire fence of the lane, to the support of the regular cavalry then attacking the hill we were facing. “The red-roofed house yonder is your objective,” I said to you. You moved out at once and quickly forged to the front of your regiment. I rode in rear, keeping the soldiers and troops closed and in line as well as the circumstances and conditions permitted. We had covered, I judge, from one-half to two-thirds the distance to Kettle Hill when Lieutenant-Colonel Garlington, from our left flank called to me that troops were needed in the meadow across the lane. I put one troop (not three, as stated in your account8) across the lane and went with it. Advancing with the troop, I began immediately to pick up troopers of the Ninth Cavalry who had drifted from their commands, and soon had so many they demanded nearly all my attention. With a line thus made up, the colored troopers on the left and yours on the right, the portion of Kettle Hill on the right of the red-roofed house was first carried. I very shortly thereafter had a strong firing-line established on the crest nearest the enemy, from the corner of the fence around the house to the low ground on the right of the hill, which fired into the strong line of conical straw hats, whose brims showed just above the edge of the Spanish trench directly west of that part of the hill.9 These hats made a fine target! I had placed a young officer of your regiment in charge of the portion of the line on top of the hill, and was about to go to the left to keep the connection of the brigade﻿—Captain McBlain, Ninth Cavalry, just then came up on the hill from the left and rear﻿—when the shot struck that put me out of the fight.

===Chinese===
> 您从洼路中出来时，我赶到了团的最前头，向您下达了投入战斗的命令。命令是：您部右翼沿洼路的铁丝栅栏行进，去支援正在攻打我们当面那座山的正规骑兵。「那边那座红屋顶的房子就是您的目标，」我对您说。您立刻出动，很快便冲到了自己全团的最前面。我骑行在后面，在情况与条件允许的范围内，尽量使士兵和各连保持密集、保持战线齐整。据我判断，我们已走完到水壶山全程的二分之一至三分之二，这时，加林顿中校（Lieutenant-Colonel Garlington）从我们的左翼向我喊话，说洼路对面的草场上需要部队。我把一个连（不是您记述8里所说的三个连）派过洼路，并随它同行。同这个连一道推进时，我立刻着手收拢那些同本队走散的第九骑兵团士兵，不多时便收了那么多，几乎占去我的全部注意力。用这样凑成的一条散兵线——左侧是有色人种士兵，右侧是您的士兵——红屋顶房子右侧的那段水壶山率先被攻取。此后不久，我便在离敌人最近的山脊上建起一条坚固的散兵线，从房子四周栅栏的拐角起，一直延展到山右侧的低地；这条线朝那密密一排锥形草帽开火，草帽的帽檐恰好露在那段山头正西面的西班牙战壕壕沿之上9。这些草帽可是绝佳的靶子！我已指派您团里的一名年轻军官掌管山顶那一段战线，正要到左翼去维系全旅的衔接——第九骑兵团的麦克布莱恩上尉（Captain McBlain）恰于此时从左后方向上了山——就在这时，一发子弹击中了我，把我打出了战斗。

===Original===
There were many wholly erroneous accounts of the Guasimas fight published at the time, for the most part written by newspapermen who were in the rear and utterly ignorant of what really occurred. Most of these accounts possess a value so purely ephemeral as to need no notice. Mr. Stephen Bonsal, however, in his book, *The Fight for Santiago*, has cast one of them in a more permanent form; and I shall discuss one or two of his statements.

Mr. Bonsal was not present at the fight, and, indeed, so far as I know, he never at any time was with the cavalry in action. He puts in his book a map of the supposed skirmish ground; but it bears to the actual scene of the fight only the well-known likeness borne by Monmouth to Macedon. There was a brook on the battleground, and there is a brook in Mr. Bonsal’s map. The real brook, flowing down from the mountains, crossed the valley road and ran down between it and the hill-trail, going nowhere near the latter. The Bonsal brook flows at right angles to the course of the real brook and crosses both trails﻿—that is, it runs up hill. It is difficult to believe that the Bonsal map could have been made by any man who had gone over the hill-trail followed by the Rough Riders and who knew where the fighting had taken place. The position of the Spanish line on the Bonsal map is inverted compared to what it really was.

On page 90 Mr. Bonsal says that in making the “precipitate advance” there was a rivalry between the regulars and Rough Riders, which resulted in each hurrying recklessly forward to strike the Spaniards first. On the contrary. The official reports show that General Young’s column waited for some time after it got to the Spanish position, so as to allow the Rough Riders (who had the more difficult trail) to come up. Colonel Wood kept his column walking at a smart pace, merely so that the regulars might not be left unsupported when the fight began; and as a matter of fact, it began almost simultaneously on both wings.

===Chinese===
当时发表的关于瓜西马斯（Guasimas）之战的记述，许多全然错误，其中大部分出自身在后方、对实际经过一无所知的报记者之手。这些记述大多价值昙花一现，无须理会。不过，斯蒂芬·邦萨尔（Stephen Bonsal）先生在他的《为圣地亚哥而战》（*The Fight for Santiago*）一书里，把其中一则铸成了较为持久的形态；我将讨论他的一两个论断。

邦萨尔先生并未亲历那场战斗；而且，据我所知，他从不曾与骑兵一同临阵。他在书里附了一张他想像中的散兵战场地图；但这份图与实际战场之间的相似，不过是众所周知的「蒙茅斯之于马其顿」（莎士比亚《亨利五世》中的著名类比）罢了。战场上有一条小溪，邦萨尔先生的地图上也有一条。真正的小溪自山间流下，横过谷中大路，在大路与山径之间向下流去，离山径远远的。邦萨尔小溪的流向却与真实小溪成直角，而且两条路都横穿——也就是说，它是往山上流的。很难相信，这张邦萨尔地图会出自身于莽骑兵所走的那条山径、又知道战斗发生在何处的人之手。邦萨尔地图上西班牙战线的位置，与实际情况恰成正相反。

第 90 页，邦萨尔先生说，「鲁莽急进」之际，正规军与莽骑兵之间存在着一种争先的较量，结果双方都不顾死活地抢着往前赶，要比谁先打到西班牙人。事实恰恰相反。官方报告表明，扬将军的纵队到达西班牙人阵地后曾等候了一阵，好让莽骑兵（他们走的是更难走的路）赶上来。伍德上校让他的纵队保持快步行进，无非是为了开打时正规军不至于无人支援；而事实上，战斗在两翼几乎同时打响。

===Original===
On page 91 Mr. Bonsal speaks of “The foolhardy formation of a solid column along a narrow trail, which brought them (the Rough Riders) within point-blank range of the Spanish rifles and within the unobstructed sweep of their machine-guns.” He also speaks as if the advance should have been made with the regiment deployed through the jungle. Of course, the only possible way by which the Rough Riders could have been brought into action in time to support the regulars was by advancing in column along the trail at a good smart gait. As soon as our advance-guard came into contact with the enemy’s outpost we deployed. No firing began for at least five minutes after Captain Capron sent back word that he had come upon the Spanish outpost. At the particular point where this occurred there was a dip in the road, which probably rendered it, in Capron’s opinion, better to keep part of his men in it. In any event, Captain Capron, who was as skilful as he was gallant, had ample time between discovering the Spanish outpost and the outbreak of the firing to arrange his troop in the formation he deemed best. His troop was not in solid formation; his men were about ten yards apart. Of course, to have walked forward deployed through the jungle, prior to reaching the ground where we were to fight, would have been a course of procedure so foolish as to warrant the summary court-martial of any man directing it. We could not have made half a mile an hour in such a formation, and would have been at least four hours too late for the fighting.

===Chinese===
第 91 页，邦萨尔先生谈到「沿狭窄小径结成实心纵队的蛮干队形，这把他们（莽骑兵）带到了西班牙步枪的直射距离之内，暴露在他们机枪毫无遮拦的扫射之下」。他的口气还仿佛，本应让全团散开、穿越丛林推进似的。当然，莽骑兵要来得及投入战斗、支援正规军，唯一可行的办法，就是沿小径以轻快步速成纵队前进。我们的前卫一同敌军前哨接触，便散开了。卡普伦上尉派人回话说他已撞上西班牙前哨之后，至少过了五分钟才开始射击。事发地点的路面上有一处洼坑，在卡普伦看来，这或许使把一部分士兵留在洼地里更为得当。无论如何，卡普伦上尉——他既英勇又干练——从发现西班牙前哨到开火之间，有充裕的时间按他认为最好的队形布置他的连。他的连并非实心队形；士兵彼此相距约十码。当然，在抵达预定战地之前便散开队形、徒步穿越丛林前进，那将是一种愚蠢得足以让任何主持其事者受即决军事审判的做法。以那种队形，我们一小时连半英里也走不完，赶到战场至少要迟到四个钟头。

===Original===
On page 92 Mr. Bonsal says that Captain Capron’s troop was ambushed, and that it received the enemy’s fire a quarter of an hour before it was expected. This is simply not so. Before the column stopped we had passed a dead Cuban, killed in the preceding day’s skirmish, and General Wood had notified me on information he had received from Capron that we might come into contact with the Spaniards at any moment, and, as I have already said, Captain Capron discovered the Spanish outpost, and we halted and partially deployed the column before the firing began. We were at the time exactly where we had expected to come across the Spaniards. Mr. Bonsal, after speaking of L Troop, adds: “The remaining troops of the regiment had travelled more leisurely, and more than half an hour elapsed before they came up to Capron’s support.” As a matter of fact, all the troops travelled at exactly the same rate of speed, although there were stragglers from each, and when Capron halted and sent back word that he had come upon the Spanish outpost, the entire regiment closed up, halted, and most of the men sat down. We then, some minutes after the first word had been received, and before any firing had begun, received instructions to deploy. I had my right wing partially deployed before the first shots between the outposts took place. Within less than three minutes I had G Troop, with Llewellen, Greenway, and Leahy, and one platoon of K Troop under Kane, on the firing-line, and it was not until after we reached the firing-line that the heavy volley-firing from the Spaniards began.

On page 94 Mr. Bonsal says: “A vexatious delay occurred before the two independent columns could communicate and advance with concerted action.﻿ ﻿… When the two columns were brought into communication it was immediately decided to make a general attack upon the Spanish position.﻿ ﻿… With this purpose in view, the following disposition of the troops was made before the advance of the brigade all along the line was ordered.” There was no communication between the two columns prior to the general attack, nor was any order issued for the advance of the brigade all along the line. The attacks were made wholly independently, and the first communication between the columns was when the right wing of the Rough Riders in the course of their advance by their firing dislodged the Spaniards from the hill across the ravine to the right, and then saw the regulars come up that hill.

===Chinese===
第 92 页，邦萨尔先生说卡普伦上尉的连中了埋伏，比预期提前一刻钟遭到敌火。这根本不是事实。纵队停下之前，我们已走过一名死于前一天小规模接战的古巴人；伍德将军已根据卡普伦传来的消息通知我，我们随时可能与西班牙人接火；况且，如我已说过的，卡普伦上尉发现了西班牙前哨，在开火之前，我们便停下脚步，将纵队部分展开。我们当时所在之处，正是我们料想会撞上西班牙人的地方。邦萨尔先生讲完 L 连之后又补了一句：「团里其余各连走得从容些，过了半个多小时才赶来支援卡普伦。」事实上，所有各连的行进速度分毫不差——尽管每连都有掉队的——当卡普伦停下、传回话说他撞见西班牙前哨时，全团收拢、停下，大部分士兵坐了下来。随后，在接到第一份口信几分钟之后、任何射击开始之前，我们接到了展开的指令。前哨间打响头几枪之前，我已将右翼部分展开。不到三分钟，我便把 G 连——有卢埃伦、格林韦和利希在——连同凯恩（Kane）率领的 K 连一个排，调上了散兵线；而西班牙人猛烈的齐射，是在我们到达散兵线之后才开始的。

第 94 页，邦萨尔先生说：「两支各自独立的纵队互通音讯、协同推进之前，发生了一段恼人的延误。……当两支纵队取得联络后，立即决定对西班牙人的阵地发起总攻。……有鉴于此，在命令全旅全线推进之前，对部队作了如下部署。」总攻之前，两支纵队之间并无任何联络，也从未下达过全旅全线推进的命令。两边的攻击全然各自进行；两支纵队之间的第一次联络，是莽骑兵右翼在推进途中以火力将西班牙人逐出右侧冲沟对面的山头之后，望见正规军开上那座山的时候。

===Original===
Mr. Bonsal’s account of what occurred among the regulars parallels his account of what occurred among the Rough Riders. He states that the squadron of the Tenth Cavalry delivered the main attack upon the hill, which was the strongest point of the Spanish position; and he says of the troopers of the Tenth Cavalry that “their better training enabled them to render more valuable service than the other troops engaged.” In reality, the Tenth Cavalrymen were deployed in support of the First, though they mingled with them in the assault proper; and so far as there was any difference at all in the amount of work done, it was in favor of the First. The statement that the Tenth Cavalry was better trained than the First, and rendered more valuable service, has not the slightest basis whatsoever of any kind, sort, or description, in fact. The Tenth Cavalry did well what it was required to do; as an organization, in this fight, it was rather less heavily engaged, and suffered less loss, actually and relatively, than either the First Cavalry or the Rough Riders. It took about the same part that was taken by the left wing of the Rough Riders, which wing was similarly rather less heavily engaged than the right and centre of the regiment. Of course, this is a reflection neither on the Tenth Cavalry nor on the left wing of the Rough Riders. Each body simply did what it was ordered to do, and did it well. But to claim that the Tenth Cavalry did better than the First, or bore the most prominent part in the fight, is like making the same claim for the left wing of the Rough Riders. All the troops engaged did well, and all alike are entitled to share in the honor of the day.

===Chinese===
邦萨尔先生对正规军方面经过的记述，与他对莽骑兵方面经过的记述如出一辙。他声称，第十骑兵团的那个中队对山头发起了主攻，而那座山正是西班牙阵地最坚固的一点；谈到第十骑兵团的士兵时他还说，「他们更胜一筹的训练，使他们得以比参战的其他部队作出更有价值的贡献」。实际上，第十骑兵团的士兵是作为第一团的支援展开的，虽然在真正的冲击中与他们混作了一处；而就所承担工作的多寡而言——倘若真有差别的话——天平是向第一团倾斜的。所谓第十骑兵团训练优于第一团、贡献更为可贵之说，事实上无论以哪种方式、形式或名目衡量，都毫无半点根据。第十骑兵团把它份内之事做得很出色；作为一个建制，它在这一仗中卷入的程度稍轻，无论按绝对数还是相对数，损失都小于第一骑兵团或莽骑兵。它所扮演的角色，与莽骑兵左翼大体相当——那一翼同样比全团的右翼和中央卷入得轻些。当然，这既非对第十骑兵团的非议，也非对莽骑兵左翼的非议。各支部队无非做了命令它们做的事，而且做得很好。但若宣称第十骑兵团比第一团打得出色，或在战斗中承担了最显要的角色，便无异于替莽骑兵的左翼作同样的宣称。参战的各部队都打得出色，全都有同等的资格分享那一天的光荣。

===Original===
Mr. Bonsal out-Spaniards the Spaniards themselves as regards both their numbers and their loss. These points are discussed elsewhere. He develops for the Spanish side, to account for their retreat, a wholly new explanation﻿—viz., that they retreated because they saw reinforcements arriving for the Americans. The Spaniards themselves make no such claim. Lieutenant Tejeiro asserts that they retreated because news had come of a (wholly mythical) American advance on Morro Castle. The Spanish official report simply says that the Americans were repulsed; which is about as accurate a statement as the other two. All three explanations, those by General Rubin, by Lieutenant Tejeiro, and by Mr. Bonsal alike, are precisely on a par with the first Spanish official report of the battle of Manila Bay, in which Admiral Dewey was described as having been repulsed and forced to retire.

There are one or two minor mistakes made by Mr. Bonsal. He states that on the roster of the officers of the Rough Riders there were ten West Pointers. There were three, one of whom resigned. Only two were in the fighting. He also states that after Las Guasimas Brigadier-General Young was made a Major-General and Colonel Wood a Brigadier-General, while the commanding officers of the First and Tenth Cavalry were ignored in this “shower of promotions.” In the first place, the commanding officers of the First and Tenth Cavalry were not in the fight﻿—only one squadron of each having been present. In the next place, there was no “shower of promotions” at all. Nobody was promoted except General Young, save to fill the vacancies caused by death or by the promotion of General Young. Wood was not promoted because of this fight. General Young most deservedly was promoted. Soon after the fight he fell sick. The command of the brigade then fell upon Wood, simply because he had higher rank than the other two regimental commanders of the brigade; and I then took command of the regiment exactly as Lieutenant-Colonels Veile and Baldwin had already taken command of the First and Tenth Cavalry when their superior officers were put in charge of brigades. After the San Juan fighting, in which Wood commanded a brigade, he was made a Brigadier-General and I was then promoted to the nominal command of the regiment, which I was already commanding in reality.

===Chinese===
在西班牙人的兵力和损失这两个问题上，邦萨尔先生比西班牙人自己还更像西班牙人。这些论点在别处已有讨论。他为西军一方杜撰了一套全新的解释来交代他们的撤退——即：他们撤退，是因为看见美军的增援开到。西班牙人自己可不曾作此说。特赫伊罗中尉（Lieutenant Tejeiro）断言，他们撤退是因为得到了美军进兵莫罗城堡的消息（纯属子虚乌有）。西班牙的官方战报则干脆说美军被击退了；这话的准确程度与前两种说法不相上下。这三种解释——鲁宾将军（General Rubin）的、特赫伊罗中尉的、邦萨尔先生的——恰好同西班牙官方关于马尼拉湾海战（battle of Manila Bay）的第一份战报旗鼓相当，那份战报里说杜威海军上将（Admiral Dewey）被击退、被迫撤退。

邦萨尔先生还犯有一两处小错。他说，莽骑兵军官名册上有十名西点出身（West Pointers）。实际是三名，其中一人已辞职，参加过战斗的只有两名。他还说，拉斯瓜西马斯（Las Guasimas）之战后，扬准将升为少将，伍德上校升为准将，而第一、第十骑兵团的团长们在这场「晋升雨」里无人问津。首先，第一、第十骑兵团的团长当时根本不在战斗中——每团只有一个中队在场。其次，压根儿就没有什么「晋升雨」。除扬将军外，无人因此晋升，只有补死亡之缺、或补扬将军晋升所遗之缺者除外。伍德并非因这一仗升迁。扬将军的晋升则完全受之无愧。战后不久他便病倒。旅的指挥权随即落到伍德身上，无非因为他军衔高于旅里另外两位团长；我也随之接掌全团，恰如维尔中校（Veile）与鲍德温中校（Baldwin）先前在各自上校被派去掌旅时，接掌第一、第十骑兵团一样。圣胡安之战后——伍德在该仗中指挥一个旅——他升任准将，我也随之晋升为全团名义上的团长，其实这个团我早就在实际指挥了。

===Original===
Mr. Bonsal’s claim of superior efficiency for the colored regular regiments as compared with the white regular regiments does not merit discussion. He asserts that General Wheeler brought on the Guasimas fight in defiance of orders. Lieutenant Miley, in his book, *In Cuba with Shafter*, on page 83, shows that General Wheeler made his fight before receiving the order which it is claimed he disobeyed. General Wheeler was in command ashore; he was told to get in touch with the enemy, and, being a man with the “fighting edge,” this meant that he was certain to fight. No general who was worth his salt would have failed to fight under such conditions; the only question would be as to how the fight was to be made. War means fighting; and the soldier’s cardinal sin is timidity.

General Wheeler remained throughout steadfast against any retreat from before Santiago. But the merit of keeping the army before Santiago, without withdrawal, until the city fell, belongs to the authorities at Washington, who at this all-important stage of the operations showed to marked advantage in overruling the proposals made by the highest generals in the field looking toward partial retreat or toward the abandonment of the effort to take the city.

===Chinese===
邦萨尔先生声称有色正规团之于白人正规团效率更优，此说不值一论。他断言，惠勒将军抗命挑起了瓜西马斯之战。米利中尉（Lieutenant Miley）在其《随沙夫特在古巴》（*In Cuba with Shafter*）一书第 83 页表明，惠勒将军是在接到那道据称被他违抗的命令之前打的那一仗。惠勒将军当时在岸上主持指挥；他奉命与敌接触，而他又是生就一身「求战锋芒」（fighting edge）的人，这就意味着他必定要打。任何够格的将领在此情形下都断无不打之理；唯一的问题只在这一仗该怎么打。战争就是要战斗；军人的头号大罪是怯懦。

惠勒将军自始至终坚决反对从圣地亚哥城下撤兵。但是，使大军在圣地亚哥城下坚守不撤、直至城陷的功劳，应归于华盛顿的当局——在战局这至为关键的阶段，他们毅然否决了前线最高将领们局部后撤或放弃夺城之议，显示出高明一筹。

---

===Original===
The following note, written by Sergeant E. G. Norton, of B Troop, refers to the death of his brother, Oliver B. Norton, one of the most gallant and soldierly men in the regiment:

> On July 1st I, together with Sergeant Campbell and Troopers Bardshar and Dudley Dean and my brother who was killed and some others, was at the front of the column right behind you. We moved forward, following you as you rode, to where we came upon the troopers of the Ninth Cavalry and a part of the First lying down. I heard the conversation between you and one or two of the officers of the Ninth Cavalry. You ordered a charge, and the regular officers answered that they had no orders to move ahead; whereupon you said: “Then let us through,” and marched forward through the lines, our regiment following. The men of the Ninth and First Cavalry then jumped up and came forward with us. Then you waved your hat and gave the command to charge and we went up the hill. On the top of Kettle Hill my brother, Oliver B. Norton, was shot through the head and in the right wrist. It was just as you started to lead the charge on the San Juan hills ahead of us; we saw that the regiment did not know you had gone and were not following, and my brother said, “For God’s sake follow the Colonel,” and as he rose the bullet went through his head.

===Chinese===
下面这则记录，出自 B 连 E·G·诺顿中士（Sergeant E. G. Norton）之手，写的是他兄弟奥利弗·B·诺顿（Oliver B. Norton）之死——那是全团最英勇、最具军人气质的人之一：

> 7 月 1 日，我同坎贝尔军士（Sergeant Campbell）、巴德沙尔（Bardshar）和达德利·迪安（Dudley Dean）两名士兵、我那位后来牺牲的兄弟，还有另外几个人，位于纵队最前部，就在您身后。我们跟着您骑行的方向向前推进，走到一处，遇上了卧倒在地的第九骑兵团士兵和第一团的一部分人。我听到了您同第九骑兵团一两位军官的交谈。您下令冲锋，正规军军官们答称他们没有接到前进的命令；于是您说：「那就让我们过去，」随即穿过他们的战线向前行进，我们团跟在后面。第九、第一骑兵团的士兵们随即一跃而起，同我们一道前进。接着您挥动帽子，下达冲锋令，我们便冲上了山。在水壶山顶，我兄弟奥利弗·B·诺顿头部和右腕各中一弹。那正是在您开始率领我们冲击前方圣胡安山岭之时；我们看到全团并不知道您已经离去、没有跟上来，我兄弟便说：「看在上帝份上，跟上上校，」他起身的一瞬，子弹穿过了他的头颅。

===Original===
In reference to Mr. Bonsal’s account of the Guasimas fight, Mr. Richard Harding Davis writes me as follows:

> We had already halted several times to give the men a chance to rest, and when we halted for the last time I thought it was for this same purpose, and began taking photographs of the men of L Troop, who were so near that they asked me to be sure and save them a photograph. Wood had twice disappeared down the trail beyond them and returned. As he came back for the second time I remember that you walked up to him (we were all dismounted then), and saluted and said: “Colonel, Doctor La Motte reports that the pace is too fast for the men, and that over fifty have fallen out from exhaustion.” Wood replied sharply: “I have no time to bother with sick men now.” You replied, more in answer, I suppose, to his tone than to his words: “I merely repeated what the Surgeon reported to me.” Wood then turned and said in explanation: “I have no time for them now; I mean that we are in sight of the enemy.”

> This was the only information we received that the men of L Troop had been ambushed by the Spaniards, and, if they were, they were very calm about it, and I certainly was taking photographs of them at the time, and the rest of the regiment, instead of being half an hour’s march away, was seated comfortably along the trail not twenty feet distant from the men of L Troop. You deployed G Troop under Captain Llewellen into the jungle at the right and sent K Troop after it, and Wood ordered Troops E and F into the field on our left. It must have been from ten to fifteen minutes after Capron and Wood had located the Spaniards before either side fired a shot. When the firing did come I went over to you and joined G Troop and a detachment of K Troop under Woodbury Kane, and we located more of the enemy on a ridge.

> If it is to be ambushed when you find the enemy exactly where you went to find him, and your scouts see him soon enough to give you sufficient time to spread five troops in skirmish order to attack him, and you then drive him back out of three positions for a mile and a half, then most certainly, as Bonsal says, “L Troop of the Rough Riders was ambushed by the Spaniards on the morning of June 24th.”

===Chinese===
关于邦萨尔先生对瓜西马斯之战的记述，理查德·哈丁·戴维斯（Richard Harding Davis）先生给我写来如下的话：

> 我们此前已停了好几回，好让士兵们有机会喘息；最后那次停下时，我还以为又是为这个，便动手给 L 连的士兵们拍照。他们近在咫尺，还叮嘱我千万记得给他们各留一张。伍德曾两次越过他们顺着小径走下去、消失又折回。他第二次回来时，我记得您迎上前去（我们那时都下了马），敬礼道：「上校，拉莫特医生（Doctor La Motte）报告说，步速对士兵们太快了，已有五十多人因力竭掉队。」伍德厉声答道：「我现在没工夫操心病人。」您回了一句——我想，与其说是答他的话，不如说是答他的口气：「我不过是转述军医向我报告的话。」伍德随即转过身来解释道：「我现在顾不上他们；我是说，我们已经望见敌人了。」

> 这便是我们得到的关于 L 连遭西班牙人伏击的唯一消息；倘若他们真中了埋伏，那他们镇定得出奇，何况我当时分明还在给他们拍照，而团里其余的人非但没有落在半个钟头的行军路程之外，正舒舒服服地坐在小径两旁，离 L 连的士兵不足二十英尺。您把卢埃伦上尉（Captain Llewellen）的 G 连展开到右边的丛林里，又派 K 连随其后跟进，伍德则命令 E、F 两连进入我们左边的田野。从卡普伦和伍德发现西班牙人，到双方打出第一枪，中间必定隔了十到十五分钟。枪声一响，我便走到您身旁，加入了 G 连和伍德伯里·凯恩（Woodbury Kane）指挥的 K 连一支分遣队，我们在一道山梁上又发现了更多敌人。

> 倘若说，当你在正是去找敌人的地方找到了敌人，你的侦察兵又及早望见了他，使你有充分时间把五个连展开成散兵队形去攻击他，随后你还把他从三道阵地上逐出、一路逐出一英里半，这也算得上「中了埋伏」——那么，诚如邦萨尔所言：「6 月 24 日上午，莽骑兵的 L 连遭西班牙人伏击」，便是千真万确的了。

===Original===
General Wood also writes me at length about Mr. Bonsal’s book, stating that his account of the Guasimas fight is without foundation in fact. He says: “We had five troops completely deployed before the first shot was fired. Captain Capron was not wounded until the fight had been going on fully thirty-five minutes. The statement that Captain Capron’s troop was ambushed is absolutely untrue. We had been informed, as you know, by Castillo’s people that we should find the dead guerilla a few hundred yards on the Siboney side of the Spanish lines.”

He then alludes to the waving of the guidon by K Troop as “the only means of communication with the regulars.” He mentions that his orders did not come from General Wheeler, and that he had no instructions from General Wheeler directly or indirectly at any time previous to the fight.

General Wood does not think that I give quite enough credit to the Rough Riders as compared to the regulars in this Guasimas fight, and believes that I greatly underestimate the Spanish force and loss, and that Lieutenant Tejeiro is not to be trusted at all on these points. He states that we began the fight ten minutes before the regulars, and that the main attack was made and decided by us. This was the view that I and all the rest of us in the regiment took at the time; but as I had found since that the members of the First and Tenth Regular Regiments held with equal sincerity the view that the main part was taken by their own commands, I have come to the conclusion that the way I have described the action is substantially correct. Owing to the fact that the Tenth Cavalry, which was originally in support, moved forward until it got mixed with the First, it is very difficult to get the exact relative position of the different troops of the First and Tenth in making the advance. Beck and Galbraith were on the left; apparently Wainwright was farthest over on the right. General Wood states that Leonardo Ros, the Civil Governor of Santiago at the time of the surrender, told him that the Spanish force at Guasimas consisted of not less than 2,600 men, and that there were nearly 300 of them killed and wounded. I do not myself see how it was possible for us, as we were the attacking party and were advancing against superior numbers well sheltered, to inflict five times as much damage as we received; but as we buried eleven dead Spaniards, and as they carried off some of their dead, I believe the loss to have been very much heavier than Lieutenant Tejeiro reports.

===Chinese===
伍德将军也就邦萨尔先生的书给我写来长信，申明他对瓜西马斯之战的记述毫无事实根据。他说：「打出第一枪之前，我们已有五个连完全展开。卡普伦上尉是在战斗足足进行了三十五分钟之后才负伤的。所谓卡普伦上尉的连中了埋伏，纯属不实之词。您知道，卡斯蒂略（Castillo）方面的人早先告知过我们：在西班牙防线靠锡博内（Siboney）一侧几百码的地方，会见到那名死去的游击兵。」

他接着提到 K 连挥动连旗一事，说那是「与正规军联络的唯一手段」。他提到，他的命令并非来自惠勒将军，而且在战斗之前的任何时候，他都不曾直接或间接地从惠勒将军那里接到过任何指示。

伍德将军认为，在这次瓜西马斯之战中，我给莽骑兵的功劳比起正规军来还嫌不足，并认为我大大低估了西班牙人的兵力与损失，认为特赫伊罗中尉在这些问题上全然不可尽信。他说，我们比正规军早十分钟开战，主攻由我们发起、由我们定局。这原是我和团里所有其他人当时的看法；但后来我发现，第一、第十正规团的官兵怀着同样的真诚，坚信主要角色是由他们自己的部队承担的，于是我得出结论：我记述这场战斗的方式大体不差。由于原本担任支援的第十骑兵团不断前移、直到与第一团搅在一起，推进时第一、第十两团各连的确切相对位置已极难查考。贝克（Beck）与加尔布雷思（Galbraith）在左翼；韦恩赖特（Wainwright）显然在最右端。伍德将军说，城降时任圣地亚哥民政总督的莱昂纳多·罗斯（Leonardo Ros）告诉他，瓜西马斯的西班牙兵力不少于 2,600 人，其中死伤近 300。我自己不明白，我们作为进攻的一方，朝着数量占优且掩蔽良好的敌人推进，怎么可能给对方造成五倍于我们所受的杀伤；但我们埋葬了十一名西班牙死者，他们又运走了自己的一部分死者，所以我深信，西军的损失要比特赫伊罗中尉所报告的重得多。

===Original===
General Wood believes that in following Lieutenant Tejeiro I have greatly underestimated the number of Spanish troops who were defending Santiago on July 1st, and here I think he completely makes out his case, he taking the view that Lieutenant Tejeiro’s statements were made for the purpose of saving Spanish honor. On this point his letter runs as follows:

> A word in regard to the number of troops in Santiago. I have had, during my long association here, a good many opportunities to get information which you have not got and probably never will get; that is, information from parties who were actually in the fight, who are now residents of the city; also information which came to me as commanding officer of the city directly after the surrender.

> To sum up briefly as follows: The Spanish surrendered in Santiago 12,000 men. We shipped from Santiago something over 14,000 men. The 2,000 additional were troops that came in from San Luis, Songo, and small upcountry posts. The 12,000 in the city, minus the force of General Iscario, 3,300 infantry and 680 cavalry, or in round numbers 4,000 men (who entered the city just after the battles of San Juan and El Caney), leaves 8,000 regulars, plus the dead, plus Cervera’s marines and bluejackets, which he himself admits landing in the neighborhood of 1,200 (and reports here are that he landed 1,380), and plus the Spanish Volunteer Battalion, which was between 800 and 900 men (this statement I have from the lieutenant-colonel of this very battalion), gives us in round numbers, present for duty on the morning of July 1st, not less than 10,500 men. These men were distributed 890 at Caney, two companies of artillery at Morro, one at Socapa, and half a company at Puenta Gorda; in all, not over 500 or 600 men, but for the sake of argument we can say a thousand. In round numbers, then, we had immediately about the city 8,500 troops. These were scattered from the cemetery around to Aguadores. In front of us, actually in the trenches, there could not by any possible method of figuring have been less than 6,000 men. You can twist it any way you want to; the figures I have given you are absolutely correct, at least they are absolutely on the side of safety.

===Chinese===
伍德将军认为，我跟着特赫伊罗中尉走，大大低估了 7 月 1 日防守圣地亚哥的西班牙兵力；在这一点上，我认为他把论点完全立住了——他的看法是，特赫伊罗中尉的那些陈述，是为保全西班牙的荣誉而作。关于这个问题，他在信中写道：

> 关于圣地亚哥的兵力数目，说上几句。我在此地长期供职期间，有过许多机会得到您没有得到、而且多半永远也不会得到的情报；也就是说，来自亲身参战、如今居住在城里的人们的情报，还有投降后我以本城指挥官的身份直接得到的情报。

> 简要归纳如下：在圣地亚哥投降的西班牙人共 12,000 名。我们从圣地亚哥运走的人数为 14,000 出头。多出的 2,000 人，是从圣路易斯（San Luis）、松戈（Songo）和内地小哨所陆续进城的部队。城内的 12,000 人，减去伊斯卡里奥将军（General Iscario）所部——步兵 3,300 人、骑兵 680 人，凑整算 4,000 人（他们是在圣胡安和埃尔卡内两战之后刚刚进城的）——余下正规军 8,000 人，再加上阵亡者，加上塞尔韦拉（Cervera）的海军陆战队和水兵——他本人承认登陆了约 1,200 人（此间的说法是他登陆了 1,380 人）——再加上西班牙义勇营——该营兵力在 800 至 900 人之间（这话我是从该营的中校本人那里听来的）——可得：7 月 1 日晨可堪应战者，凑整算不少于 10,500 人。这些人的分布是：卡内（Caney）890 人，莫罗（Morro）炮兵两个连，索卡帕（Socapa）一个连，普恩塔戈尔达（Puenta Gorda）半个连；总共不过 500 或 600 人，但为了论证起见，不妨算作一千。这样，凑整说来，紧贴城郊一带的敌军共有 8,500 人。他们从墓地一路散布到阿瓜多雷斯（Aguadores）。在我们正面、确确实实身处战壕之内的，无论怎么计算，都不可能少于 6,000 人。您爱怎么折账都行；我给您的这些数字绝对正确，至少绝对是最稳妥的一边。

===Original===
It is difficult for me to withstand the temptation to tell what has befallen some of my men since the regiment disbanded; how McGinty, after spending some weeks in Roosevelt Hospital in New York with an attack of fever, determined to call upon his captain, Woodbury Kane, when he got out, and procuring a horse rode until he found Kane’s house, when he hitched the horse to a lamppost and strolled in; how Cherokee Bill married a wife in Hoboken, and as that pleasant city ultimately proved an uncongenial field for his activities, how I had to send both himself and his wife out to the Territory; how Happy Jack, haunted by visions of the social methods obtaining in the best saloons of Arizona, applied for the position of “bouncer out” at the Executive Chamber when I was elected Governor, and how I got him a job at railroading instead, and finally had to ship him back to his own Territory also; how a valued friend from a cow ranch in the remote West accepted a pressing invitation to spend a few days at the home of another ex-trooper, a New Yorker of fastidious instincts, and arrived with an umbrella as his only baggage; how poor Holderman and Pollock both died and were buried with military honors, all of Pollock’s tribesmen coming to the burial; how Tom Isbell joined Buffalo Bill’s Wild West Show, and how, on the other hand, George Rowland scornfully refused to remain in the East at all, writing to a gallant young New Yorker who had been his bunkie: “Well, old boy, I am glad I didn’t go home with you for them people to look at, because I ain’t a Buffalo or a rhinoceros or a giraffe, and I don’t like to be stared at, and you know we didn’t do no hard fighting down there. I have been in closer places than that right here in United States, that is better men to fight than them dam Spaniards.” In another letter Rowland tells of the fate of Tom Darnell, the rider, he who rode the sorrel horse of the Third Cavalry: “There ain’t much news to write of except poor old Tom Darnell got killed about a month ago. Tom and another fellow had a fight and he shot Tom through the heart and Tom was dead when he hit the floor. Tom was sure a good old boy, and I sure hated to hear of him going, and he had plenty of grit too. No man ever called on him for a fight that he didn’t get it.”

===Chinese===
团遣散以后，我的一些士兵落得怎样的境遇，要忍住不讲的诱惑，对我说来实在困难：比如麦金蒂（McGinty），在纽约的罗斯福医院（Roosevelt Hospital）因热病住了几个星期，出院时决意去拜访他的连长伍德伯里·凯恩，于是弄到一匹马，一路骑到寻着凯恩的家，把马拴在灯柱上，踱了进去；比如「切罗基比尔」（Cherokee Bill）在霍博肯（Hoboken）娶了亲，而那座可爱的城市最终证明不是他施展身手的合适地界，我只好连他带媳妇一并送出去，送到领地（the Territory）上去；比如「快活杰克」（Happy Jack），魂牵梦萦放不下亚利桑那一流酒馆里通行的那套社交章法，在我当选州长后跑到州长官署（Executive Chamber）申请当「赶人的打手」（bouncer out），我给他另找了份铁路上的活计，末了也只好把他送回他自己的领地；比如一位来自西部僻远牧牛场的可贵朋友，应邀到另一位退伍士兵——一位天性讲究的纽约人——的家中盘桓数日，到门时全部行李只有一把雨伞；比如可怜的霍尔德曼（Holderman）和波洛克（Pollock）先后亡故，都以军礼下葬，波洛克的部落同乡全体到场送葬；比如汤姆·伊斯贝尔（Tom Isbell）加入了「水牛比尔」（Buffalo Bill）的「狂野西部」演出团，而另一面，乔治·罗兰（George Rowland）却对留在东部不屑一顾，写信给一位曾同他合铺的英勇纽约青年道：「唉，老伙计，幸亏我没跟你回家给他们当稀罕看，因为我又不是野牛，也不是犀牛、长颈鹿，我不爱让人盯着瞧，再说你也知道，我们在那边根本没打过什么硬仗。我就在这合众国本地，都到过比那更险的关口，交过手的对手也比那些该死的西班牙佬强。」在另一封信里，罗兰讲到骑手汤姆·达内尔（Tom Darnell）——就是骑过第三骑兵团那匹红栗色马的那位——的结局：「没什么新闻可写，只有可怜的老汤姆·达内尔大约一个月前没了。汤姆跟另一个家伙干了一架，那人一枪打穿汤姆的心口，汤姆挨着地板就断了气。汤姆真是个好老伙计，听到他去了，我心里真难过，他也很有种。从来没有人找他打架而没打成的。」

===Original===
My men were children of the dragon’s blood, and if they had no outland foe to fight and no outlet for their vigorous and daring energy, there was always the chance of their fighting one another: but the great majority, if given the chance to do hard or dangerous work, availed themselves of it with the utmost eagerness, and though fever sickened and weakened them so that many died from it during the few months following their return, yet, as a whole, they are now doing fairly well. A few have shot other men or been shot themselves; a few ran for office and got elected, like Llewellen and Luna in New Mexico, or defeated, like Brodie and Wilcox in Arizona; some have been trying hard to get to the Philippines; some have returned to college, or to the law, or the factory, or the counting-room; most of them have gone back to the mine, the ranch, and the hunting camp; and the great majority have taken up the threads of their lives where they dropped them when the *Maine* was blown up and the country called to arms.

===Chinese===
我的人都是龙的血脉所生的孩子，倘若没有异邦的敌人可打、没有地方倾泻他们旺盛而冒险的精力，彼此斗上一斗总是难免的事：但他们当中的绝大多数，只要有机会承担艰苦或危险的活计，无不以最大的热忱抓住不放；热病虽然把他们拖垮、削弱，以致回国后的几个月里许多人死于此病，然而总体说来，他们如今境况尚好。有几个人开枪打过别人，或自己挨了枪；有几个人竞选公职，像新墨西哥的卢埃伦和卢纳那样选上了，或像亚利桑那的布罗迪和威尔科克斯那样败选了；有些人一直在拼命设法去菲律宾；有些人回了大学，或回到法律界、工厂、账房；大多数人回到了矿山、牧场和狩猎营地；而绝大多数人，都从当年「缅因」号（*Maine*）被炸、国家征召入伍那一刻放下的地方，重新接起了各自人生的线头。

===Original===
On behalf of the Rough Riders

 I dedicate this book

 to the officers and men of the

 five regular regiments

 which together with mine made up the

 cavalry division at Santiago
Theodore Roosevelt
Executive Mansion

 Albany, New York

 May 1, 1899

===Chinese===
谨以莽骑兵全体将士之名

我谨将此书献给

五个正规团的

全体军官与士兵——

正是他们与我的团并肩

组成了圣地亚哥的骑兵师
西奥多·罗斯福（Theodore Roosevelt）
州长官邸

纽约州奥尔巴尼

1899 年 5 月 1 日

## Endnotes / 尾注

===Original===
1. See Lieutenant Müller y Tejeiro, *Combates y Capitulación de Santiago de Cuba*, page 136. The Lieutenant speaks as if only one echelon, of seven companies and two guns, was engaged on the 24th. The official report says distinctly, “General Rubin’s column,” which consisted of the companies detailed. By turning to page 146, where Lieutenant Tejeiro enumerates the strength of the various companies, it will be seen that they averaged over 110 men apiece; this probably does not include officers, and is probably an understatement anyhow. On page 261 he makes the Spanish loss at Las Guasimas, which he calls Sevilla, 9 killed and 27 wounded. Very possibly he includes only the Spanish regulars; two of the Spaniards we slew, over on the left, were in brown, instead of the light blue of the regulars, and were doubtless guerillas. ↩︎

===Chinese===
1. 参见穆列尔·伊·特赫伊罗中尉（Lieutenant Müller y Tejeiro）所著《古巴圣地亚哥之战与投降》（*Combates y Capitulación de Santiago de Cuba*）第 136 页。这位中尉的口吻，仿佛 24 日接仗的只有一个梯队，辖七个连、两门炮。而官方战报写得明明白白，是「鲁宾（Rubin）将军的纵队」，即由各支抽调来的连队编成。翻到第 146 页——特赫伊罗中尉在那里逐一开列了各连兵力——可以看出每连平均在 110 人以上；这个数字多半不包括军官，而且无论如何恐怕还是少报的。在第 261 页，他把拉斯瓜西马斯之战——他称之为塞维利亚（Sevilla）——的西班牙方面损失记作阵亡 9 人、负伤 27 人。很可能他只计了西班牙正规军；我们在左翼击毙的西班牙人当中，有两名穿的是棕色制服，而非正规军的浅蓝色，十有八九是游击兵。 ↩︎

===Original===
2. Lieutenant Tejeiro, p. 154, speaks of this attempt to retake San Juan and its failure. ↩︎

===Chinese===
2. 特赫伊罗中尉在第 154 页谈到了西军夺回圣胡安的这次尝试及其失败。 ↩︎

===Original===
3. According to the official reports, 5,104 officers and men of Kent’s infantry, and 2,649 of the cavalry had been landed. My regiment is put down as 542 strong, instead of the real figure, 490, the difference being due to men who were in hospital and on guard at the seashore, etc. In other words, the total represents the total landed; the details, etc., are included. General Wheeler, in his report of July 7th, puts these details as about fifteen percent of the whole of the force which was on the transports; about eighty-five percent got forward and was in the fight. ↩︎

===Chinese===
3. 据官方战报，肯特（Kent）所部步兵登陆官兵 5,104 人，骑兵 2,649 人。我的团被记作 542 人，而实数是 490 人，差额来自留医的、在海滨担任警戒的等等人员。换句话说，这个总数代表的是登陆总人数，把分遣留守人员等一并计入。惠勒（Wheeler）将军在 7 月 7 日的报告里估计，这类分遣人员约占运输船全部兵力的百分之十五；约有百分之八十五开抵前线，参加了战斗。 ↩︎

===Original===
4. The total Spanish force in Santiago under General Linares was 6,000: 4,000 regulars, 1,000 volunteers, and 1,000 marines and sailors from the ships. (Diary of the British Consul, Frederick W. Ramsden, entry of July 1st.) Four thousand more troops entered next day. Of the 6,000 troops, 600 or thereabouts were at El Caney, and 900 in the forts at the mouth of the harbor. Lieutenant Tejeiro states that there were 520 men at El Caney, 970 in the forts at the mouth of the harbor, and 3,000 in the lines, not counting the cavalry and civil guard which were in reserve. He certainly very much understates the Spanish force; thus he nowhere accounts for the engineers mentioned on p. 135; and his figures would make the total number of Spanish artillerymen but 32. He excludes the cavalry, the civil guard, and the marines which had been stationed at the Plaza del Toros; yet he later mentions that these marines were brought up, and their commander, Bustamente, severely wounded; he states that the cavalry advanced to cover the retreat of the infantry, and I myself saw the cavalry come forward, for the most part dismounted, when the Spaniards attempted a forward movement late in the afternoon, and we shot many of their horses; while later I saw and conversed with officers and men of the civil guard who had been wounded at the same time﻿—this in connection with returning them their wives and children, after the latter had fled from the city. Although the engineers are excluded, Lieutenant Tejeiro mentions that their colonel, as well as the colonel of the artillery, was wounded. Four thousand five hundred is surely an understatement of the forces which resisted the attack of the forces under Wheeler. Lieutenant Tejeiro is very careless in his figures. Thus in one place he states that the position of San Juan was held by two companies comprising 250 soldiers. Later he says it was held by three companies, whose strength he puts at 300﻿—thus making them average 100 instead of 125 men apiece. He then mentions another echelon of two companies, so situated as to cross their fire with the others. Doubtless the blockhouse and trenches at Fort San Juan proper were only held by three or four hundred men; they were taken by the Sixth and Sixteenth Infantry under Hawkins’s immediate command; and they formed but one point in the line of hills, trenches, ranch-houses, and blockhouses which the Spaniards held, and from which we drove them. When the city capitulated later, over 8,000 unwounded troops and over 16,000 rifles and carbines were surrendered; by that time the marines and sailors had of course gone, and the volunteers had disbanded.
 In all these figures I have taken merely the statements from the Spanish side. I am inclined to think the actual numbers were much greater than those here given. Lieutenant Wiley, in his book *In Cuba with Shafter*, which is practically an official statement, states that nearly 11,000 Spanish troops were surrendered; and this is the number given by the Spaniards themselves in the remarkable letter the captured soldiers addressed to General Shafter, which Wiley quotes in full. Lieutenant Tejeiro, in his chap. XIV, explains that the volunteers had disbanded before the end came, and the marines and sailors had of course gone, while nearly a thousand men had been killed or captured or had died of wounds and disease, so that there must have been at least 14,000 all told. Subtracting the reinforcements who arrived on the 2nd, this would mean about 10,000 Spaniards present on the 1st; in which case Kent and Wheeler were opposed by at least equal numbers.
 In dealing with the Spanish losses, Lieutenant Tejeiro contradicts himself. He puts their total loss on this day at 593, including 94 killed, 121 missing, and 2 prisoners﻿—217 in all. Yet he states that of the 520 men at Caney but 80 got back, the remaining 440 being killed, captured, or missing. When we captured the city we found in the hospitals over 2,000 seriously wounded and sick Spaniards; on making inquiries, I found that over a third were wounded. From these facts I feel that it is safe to put down the total Spanish loss in battle as at least 1,200, of whom over a thousand were killed and wounded.
 Lieutenant Tejeiro, while rightly claiming credit for the courage shown by the Spaniards, also praises the courage and resolution of the Americans, saying that they fought, “*con un arrojo y una decision verdaderamente admirables*.” He dwells repeatedly upon the determination with which our troops kept charging though themselves unprotected by cover. As for the Spanish troops, all who fought them that day will most freely admit the courage they showed. At El Caney, where they were nearly hemmed in, they made a most desperate defence; at San Juan the way to retreat was open, and so, though they were seven times as numerous, they fought with less desperation, but still very gallantly. ↩︎

===Chinese===
4. 利纳雷斯将军（General Linares）麾下驻圣地亚哥的西班牙军队总计 6,000 人：正规军 4,000 人，义勇军 1,000 人，另有来自各舰的海军陆战队和水兵 1,000 人。（录自英国领事弗雷德里克·W·拉姆斯登〔Frederick W. Ramsden〕日记，7 月 1 日条。）次日又有 4,000 名援军开入。这 6,000 人中，约有 600 人在埃尔卡内，900 人在港湾入口处的炮台里。特赫伊罗中尉称，埃尔卡内有 520 人，港湾入口炮台有 970 人，防线之内有 3,000 人，且不计留作预备队的骑兵和治安警卫队。他无疑大大低报了西军兵力：比如他对第 135 页提到的工兵处处未作交代；照他的数字算来，西班牙炮兵总共不过 32 人。他把骑兵、治安警卫队和原先驻扎斗牛场（Plaza del Toros）的海军陆战队一概排除在外，可他后来又提到，这些陆战队被调了上来，其指挥官布斯塔门特（Bustamente）身负重伤；他写道，骑兵曾前出掩护步兵撤退，而西班牙人那天傍晚试图向前推进时，我亲眼看见他们的骑兵开了上来——大多是下了马的——我们还打死了他们的许多马匹；再后来，我又见到并同当时一并负伤的治安警卫队军官和士兵谈过话﻿——那是在他们的妻小逃出城后，我把这些妻小送还给他们时的事。工兵虽被排除在外，特赫伊罗中尉却又提到，工兵上校同炮兵上校一样负了伤。四千五百这个数字，对抵抗惠勒所部进攻的西军兵力而言，无疑仍是少报。特赫伊罗中尉的数字漫不经心：他在一处说圣胡安阵地由两个连共 250 名士兵据守，随后又改称由三个连据守，兵力记作 300﻿——这么一来每连平均 100 人，而非 125 人。接着他又提到另一梯队的两个连，位置恰与其他各连构成交叉火力。毫无疑问，圣胡安堡本堡的碉堡与堑壕仅由三四百人据守；它们是霍金斯（Hawkins）亲率第六和第十六步兵团攻取的；而且它们不过是西班牙人所据守的那条由丘陵、堑壕、牧场屋舍与碉堡构成的防线上的一个点，我们把西军从整条防线上逐了出去。后来城池投降时，交出的未负伤部队逾 8,000 人，步枪和卡宾枪逾 16,000 支；到那时，海军陆战队和水兵当然早已撤走，义勇军也已解散。
以上这些数字，我一概取自西班牙一方的说法。我倾向于认为，实际数目远大于此处所列。怀利中尉（Lieutenant Wiley）在其著作《随沙夫特在古巴》（*In Cuba with Shafter*）——此书实际上近乎一份官方陈述——中说，投降的西班牙军队将近 11,000 人；被俘士兵致沙夫特将军的那封引人瞩目的信里，西班牙人自己给出的也是这个数字，怀利将该信全文引录。特赫伊罗中尉在其书第十四章解释说，义勇军在战事结束前业已解散，海军陆战队和水兵当然早已撤走，另有近千人阵亡、被俘或死于创伤与疾病，如此算来，西军总数当不少于 14,000 人。扣除 2 日开到的援军，这意味着 1 日当天在场的西班牙人约有 10,000 名；果真如此，肯特和惠勒当面之敌至少与他们兵力相当。
在西班牙人的伤亡数字上，特赫伊罗中尉自相矛盾。他把西军当日总损失记作 593 人，包括阵亡 94 人、失踪 121 人、被俘 2 人﻿——合计 217 人。可他又说，埃尔卡内的 520 人中仅有 80 人生还，其余 440 人或死或俘，或下落不明。我们攻陷城池后，在各医院里发现重伤和患病的西班牙人逾 2,000 名；一经查问，我发现其中超过三分之一是伤员。由这些事实看来，我认为把西班牙人的战斗总损失记作至少 1,200 人是稳妥的，其中死伤合计逾千。
特赫伊罗中尉在为西班牙人的勇烈正当争誉之余，也称许美国人的胆气与果决，说他们以「一种真正令人叹服的勇猛与决绝」（*con un arrojo y una decision verdaderamente admirables*）作战。他反复致意于我军自身毫无掩体护身、却仍冲锋不辍的那股决绝。至于西班牙军队，凡当天与之交手的人，无不乐于承认他们展现的勇气。在埃尔卡内，他们几乎被团团围住，作了极为殊死的抵抗；在圣胡安，退路敞开，因此尽管他们人多七倍，作战便少了那股拼死之劲，但仍十分英勇。 ↩︎

===Original===
5. This is probably an understatement. Lieutenant Müller, in chap. XXXVIII of his book, says that there were “eight or nine thousand;” this is exclusive of the men from the fleet, and apparently also of many of the volunteers (see chap. XIV), all of whom were present on July 2nd. I am inclined to think that on the evening of that day there were more Spanish troops inside Santiago than there were American troops outside. ↩︎

===Original===
> Hark! I hear the tramp of thousands,

 And of armed men the hum;

 Lo! a nation’s hosts have gathered

 Round the quick alarming drum﻿—

 Saying, “Come,

 Freemen, come!

 Ere your heritage be wasted,” said the quick-alarming drum.

===Chinese===
> 听啊！我听见千万人的踏步，

还有武装人群的嗡嗡低语；

看哪！一国的军旅已然集结，

围在那急促示警的战鼓旁——

趁你们的祖业还未糟蹋殆尽，”急促示警的战鼓说道。

但战鼓

回响道：“来！

死神将收割更英勇的庄稼，”肃穆深沉的战鼓说道。

这笔账你们得亲手算过才作数，”那扬基腔作答的战鼓说道。

## III General Young’s Fight at Las Guasimas / III 扬将军的拉斯瓜西马斯之战

===Original===
Just before leaving Tampa we had been brigaded with the First (white) and Tenth (colored) Regular Cavalry under Brigadier-General S. B. M. Young. We were the Second Brigade, the First Brigade consisting of the Third and Sixth (white), and the Ninth (colored) Regular Cavalry under Brigadier-General Sumner. The two brigades of the cavalry division were under Major-General Joseph Wheeler, the gallant old Confederate cavalry commander.

General Young was﻿—and is﻿—as fine a type of the American fighting soldier as a man can hope to see. He had been in command, as Colonel, of the Yellowstone National Park, and I had seen a good deal of him in connection therewith, as I was President of the Boone and Crockett Club, an organization devoted to hunting big game, to its preservation, and to forest preservation. During the preceding winter, while he was in Washington, he had lunched with me at the Metropolitan Club, Wood being one of the other guests. Of course, we talked of the war, which all of us present believed to be impending, and Wood and I told him we were going to make every effort to get in, somehow; and he answered that we must be sure to get into his brigade, if he had one, and he would guarantee to show us fighting. None of us forgot the conversation. As soon as our regiment was raised General Young applied for it to be put in his brigade. We were put in; and he made his word good; for he fought and won the first fight on Cuban soil.

Yet, even though under him, we should not have been in this fight at all if we had not taken advantage of the chance to disembark among the first troops, and if it had not been for Wood’s energy in pushing our regiment to the front.

===Chinese===
就在离开坦帕前夕，我们与第一（白人）和第十（黑人）正规骑兵团一起，编入 S·B·M·扬（S. B. M. Young）准将麾下，是为第二旅；第一旅由第三、第六（白人）与第九（黑人）正规骑兵团组成，归萨姆纳准将指挥。骑兵师所属的这两个旅，都归约瑟夫·惠勒少将（Joseph Wheeler）统辖——那位英勇豪迈的邦联老骑兵统帅。

扬将军当年是——如今依然是——人们所能期望见到的美国战斗军人的最佳典范。他曾以上校身份执掌黄石国家公园，我因担任布恩与克罗克俱乐部（Boone and Crockett Club）主席而与他颇多过从——那是一个致力于猎捕大型猎物、保护猎物资源与保护森林的组织。前一年冬天他在华盛顿期间，曾来大都会俱乐部（Metropolitan Club）与我共进午餐，伍德（Leonard Wood）也是座上客之一。我们自然谈到了战争——在座诸君都相信它已迫在眉睫——我和伍德告诉他，我们打算千方百计挤进去；他答道，倘若日后他能带上一个旅，我们务必进他的旅，他保管让我们见识真正的战斗。我们谁也没忘记那次谈话。我们的团一经组建，扬将军便申请把它编入自己的旅。我们果然编了进去；他也兑现了诺言——古巴土地上的第一仗，就是他指挥并打赢的。

然而，即便在他麾下，倘若不是我们抓住机会随第一批部队登了岸，倘若不是伍德奋力把我们团往前推，我们压根儿就轮不上打这一仗。

===Original===
On landing we spent some active hours in marching our men a quarter of a mile or so inland, as boatload by boatload they disembarked. Meanwhile one of the men, Knoblauch, a New Yorker, who was a great athlete and a champion swimmer, by diving in the surf off the dock, recovered most of the rifles which had been lost when the boatload of colored cavalry capsized. The country would have offered very great difficulties to an attacking force had there been resistance. It was little but a mass of rugged and precipitous hills, covered for the most part by dense jungle. Five hundred resolute men could have prevented the disembarkation at very little cost to themselves. There had been about that number of Spaniards at Daiquiri that morning, but they had fled even before the ships began shelling. In their place we found hundreds of Cuban insurgents, a crew of as utter tatterdemalions as human eyes ever looked on, armed with every kind of rifle in all stages of dilapidation. It was evident, at a glance, that they would be no use in serious fighting, but it was hoped that they might be of service in scouting. From a variety of causes, however, they turned out to be nearly useless, even for this purpose, so far as the Santiago campaign was concerned.

We were camped on a dusty, brush-covered flat, with jungle on one side, and on the other a shallow, fetid pool fringed with palm-trees. Huge land-crabs scuttled noisily through the underbrush, exciting much interest among the men. Camping was a simple matter, as each man carried all he had, and the officers had nothing. I took a light mackintosh and a toothbrush. Fortunately, that night it did not rain; and from the palm-leaves we built shelters from the sun.

General Lawton, a tall, fine-looking man, had taken the advance. A thorough soldier, he at once established outposts and pushed reconnoitring parties ahead on the trails. He had as little baggage as the rest of us. Our own Brigade-Commander, General Young, had exactly the same impedimenta that I had, namely, a mackintosh and a toothbrush.

===Chinese===
登陆之后，士兵们一船接一船地卸载上岸，我们忙了好几个钟头，把队伍朝内陆带了约四百米。这期间，我团一个名叫克诺布劳赫（Knoblauch）的纽约士兵——他是个出色的运动员、游泳冠军——在码头外的浪涛里反复潜水，捞回了先前一船黑人骑兵翻船时落水的大多数步枪。这片地方倘若有人抵抗，本会给进攻部队造成极大的困难。这里简直就是一大片崎岖陡峭的丘陵，绝大部分覆盖着茂密的丛林。五百名意志坚决的士兵，本可以付出很小的代价就阻止这次登陆。那天早晨，代基里（Daiquiri）本有大约这个数目的西班牙兵，可军舰还没开炮，他们就早已逃之夭夭。我们在原地遇见的是数百名古巴起义军——一伙世人眼中所未见的彻头彻尾的褴褛之徒，扛着破烂程度不一的各式步枪。一眼便知，真打起硬仗来他们派不上用场，但大家指望他们在侦察上或许能出些力。然而由于种种原因，就圣地亚哥战役而言，连这点用途他们也几乎没能派上。

我们的营地设在一块尘土飞扬、灌木丛生的平地上，一侧是丛林，另一侧是一泓边缘长满棕榈树的浅浅臭水塘。巨大的陆蟹在灌木丛里窸窣乱窜，引得士兵们大为好奇。扎营是再简单不过的事：每个人都把全部家当穿在身上，军官们更是两手空空。我随身带了一件轻便雨衣和一把牙刷。所幸那晚没有下雨；我们还用棕榈叶搭起了遮阳的凉棚。

劳顿将军（General Lawton）身材高挑、仪表堂堂，负责率部担任前卫。他是个彻头彻尾的军人，一到任便立即设立前哨，并派出侦察队沿小径前出。他的行装和我们大家的一样少。我们自己的旅长扬将军，随身辎重与我的一模一样，也就是一件雨衣加一把牙刷。

===Original===
Next morning we were hard at work trying to get the stuff unloaded from the ship, and succeeded in getting most of it ashore, but were utterly unable to get transportation for anything but a very small quantity. The great shortcoming throughout the campaign was the utterly inadequate transportation. If we had been allowed to take our mule-train, we could have kept the whole cavalry division supplied.

In the afternoon word came to us to march. General Wheeler, a regular gamecock, was as anxious as Lawton to get first blood, and he was bent upon putting the cavalry division to the front as quickly as possible. Lawton’s advance-guard was in touch with the Spaniards, and there had been a skirmish between the latter and some Cubans, who were repulsed. General Wheeler made a reconnaissance in person, found out where the enemy was, and directed General Young to take our brigade and move forward so as to strike him next morning. He had the power to do this, as when General Shafter was afloat he had command ashore.

I had succeeded in finding Texas, my surviving horse, much the worse for his fortnight on the transport and his experience in getting off, but still able to carry me.

===Chinese===
第二天早晨，我们忙着把物资从船上卸运，大部分都弄上了岸，但除了极少量之外，运输工具完全无着，什么也拉不走。整个战役期间最大的短板，就是运输能力严重不足。当初若获准带上我们的骡队，我们本可以保障整个骑兵师的补给。

下午，开拔的命令传来。惠勒将军活像一只好斗的公鸡，跟劳顿一样巴望抢先见血，铁了心要让骑兵师尽快顶到最前面。劳顿的前卫已与西班牙人接触，后者曾同一股古巴人打了一场小仗，古巴人被击退了。惠勒将军亲自实施侦察，摸清了敌军位置，随即指示扬将军率我们的旅向前推进，以便次日凌晨对敌实施打击。他有权这样做——沙夫特将军（General Shafter）尚在舰上时，岸上的指挥权归他。

我找到了我那匹幸存的战马“得克萨斯”。它在运输舰上熬了两个星期，又经受了下船时的那番折腾，已经大伤元气，但还驮得动我。

===Original===
It was mid-afternoon and the tropic sun was beating fiercely down when Colonel Wood started our regiment﻿—the First and Tenth Cavalry and some of the infantry regiments having already marched. Colonel Wood himself rode in advance, while I led my squadron, and Major Brodie followed with his. It was a hard march, the hilly jungle trail being so narrow that often we had to go in single file. We marched fast, for Wood was bound to get us ahead of the other regiments, so as to be sure of our place in the body that struck the enemy next morning. If it had not been for his energy in pushing forward, we should certainly have missed the fight. As it was, we did not halt until we were at the extreme front.

The men were not in very good shape for marching, and moreover they were really horsemen, the majority being cowboys who had never done much walking. The heat was intense and their burdens very heavy. Yet there was very little straggling. Whenever we halted they instantly took off their packs and threw themselves on their backs. Then at the word to start they would spring into place again. The captains and lieutenants tramped along, encouraging the men by example and word. A good part of the time I was by Captain Llewellen, and was greatly pleased to see the way in which he kept his men up to their work. He never pitied or coddled his troopers, but he always looked after them. He helped them whenever he could, and took rather more than his full share of hardship and danger, so that his men naturally followed him with entire devotion. Jack Greenway was under him as lieutenant, and to him the entire march was nothing but an enjoyable outing, the chance of fight on the morrow simply adding the needed spice of excitement.

It was long after nightfall when we tramped through the darkness into the squalid coast hamlet of Siboney. As usual when we made a night camp, we simply drew the men up in column of troops, and then let each man lie down where he was. Black thunderclouds were gathering. Before they broke the fires were made and the men cooked their coffee and pork, some frying the hardtack with the pork. The officers, of course, fared just as the men did.
