Hardly had we finished eating when the rain came, a regular tropic downpour. We sat about, sheltering ourselves as best we could, for the hour or two it lasted; then the fires were relighted and we closed around them, the men taking off their wet things to dry them, so far as possible, by the blaze.

===Chinese===
下午三时许，热带的骄阳正狠狠炙烤着大地，伍德上校率我们的团出发——第一、第十骑兵团和几个步兵团此前已经开拔。伍德上校亲自骑行在最前面，我统带自己的中队，布罗迪少校（Major Brodie）率他的中队随后。这是一次艰苦的行军，丘陵间的丛林小径窄得我们常常只得一路纵队鱼贯而行。我们走得很快，因为伍德铁了心要让我们赶在其他团前头，确保次晨迎击敌军的部队里有我们的位置。若不是他这股奋励向前的劲头，我们肯定要与这场战斗失之交臂。事实上，我们一直推进到最前沿方才停驻。

士兵们的状态本不宜于徒步行军，何况他们骨子里是骑马的人，大多数是平素不怎么走路的牛仔。暑热难当，负荷又极重。然而掉队的寥寥无几。每次一停下来，他们立即卸下行囊，仰面朝天躺倒；开拔令一下，又即刻弹回队列。上尉们和中尉们一路徒步，以身作则、言语鼓劲。有相当一段路我走在卢埃伦上尉（Captain Llewellen）身旁，很欣慰地看到他如何让部下始终恪尽其职。他从不怜惜娇惯自己的骑兵，却总是照拂有加。只要力所能及，他就帮他们一把，艰难险阻总比份内多担几分，部下因此自然而然地死心塌地追随他。杰克·格林韦（Jack Greenway）在他手下任中尉，对这位老兄来说，整场行军不过是一次惬意的郊游，明日可能的一战，只是平添了几分恰到好处的刺激。

夜幕降临许久之后，我们才摸黑跋涉进海岸边那座脏乱的小村锡博内（Siboney）。同往常夜营一样，我们只是把各连列成纵队，然后让每个人原地躺下。乌黑的雷雨云正在聚拢。雨点落下之前，篝火已经生起，士兵们煮上咖啡和猪肉，有人还用猪油煎起了硬面饼。军官们的吃食自然与士兵无异。我们刚一吃完，大雨便倾盆而至，是一场地道的热带暴雨。在它持续的一两个钟头里，大家坐着各显神通地避雨；随后篝火重新点起，我们围拢过去，士兵们脱下湿衣，尽可能就着火光烘干。

===Original===
Wood had gone off to see General Young, as General Wheeler had instructed General Young to hit the Spaniards, who were about four miles away, as soon after daybreak as possible. Meanwhile I strolled over to Captain Capron’s troop. He and I, with his two lieutenants, Day and Thomas, stood around the fire, together with two or three noncommissioned officers and privates; among the latter were Sergeant Hamilton Fish and Trooper Elliot Cowdin, both of New York. Cowdin, together with two other troopers, Harry Thorpe and Munro Ferguson, had been on my Oyster Bay Polo Team some years before. Hamilton Fish had already shown himself one of the best noncommissioned officers we had. A huge fellow, of enormous strength and endurance and dauntless courage, he took naturally to a soldier’s life. He never complained and never shirked any duty of any kind, while his power over his men was great. So good a sergeant had he made that Captain Capron, keen to get the best men under him, took him when he left Tampa﻿—for Fish’s troop remained behind. As we stood around the flickering blaze that night I caught myself admiring the splendid bodily vigor of Capron and Fish﻿—the captain and the sergeant. Their frames seemed of steel, to withstand all fatigue; they were flushed with health; in their eyes shone high resolve and fiery desire. Two finer types of the fighting man, two better representatives of the American soldier, there were not in the whole army. Capron was going over his plans for the fight when we should meet the Spaniards on the morrow, Fish occasionally asking a question. They were both filled with eager longing to show their mettle, and both were rightly confident that if they lived they would win honorable renown and would rise high in their chosen profession. Within twelve hours they both were dead.

I had lain down when toward midnight Wood returned. He had gone over the whole plan with General Young. We were to start by sunrise toward Santiago, General Young taking four troops of the Tenth and four troops of the First up the road which led through the valley; while Colonel Wood was to lead our eight troops along a hill-trail to the left, which joined the valley road about four miles on, at a point where the road went over a spur of the mountain chain and from thence went down hill toward Santiago. The Spaniards had their lines at the junction of the road and the trail.

Before describing our part in the fight, it is necessary to say a word about General Young’s share, for, of course, the whole fight was under his direction, and the fight on the right wing under his immediate supervision. General Young had obtained from General Castillo, the commander of the Cuban forces, a full description of the country in front. General Castillo promised Young the aid of eight hundred Cubans, if he made a reconnaissance in force to find out exactly what the Spanish strength was. This promised Cuban aid did not, however, materialize, the Cubans, who had been beaten back by the Spaniards the day before, not appearing on the firing-line until the fight was over.

===Chinese===
伍德去见扬将军了，因为惠勒将军已指示扬将军：西班牙人就在约六公里外，破晓之后要尽早对其打击。趁这工夫，我踱到卡普伦上尉（Captain Capron）的连队去。他和我，加上他手下的两名尉官戴（Day）与托马斯（Thomas），同两三名军士、列兵一起围着火站着；后者当中有汉密尔顿·菲什中士（Sergeant Hamilton Fish）和骑兵埃利奥特·考丁（Elliot Cowdin），两人都是纽约人。考丁和另外两名骑兵——哈里·索普（Harry Thorpe）与门罗·弗格森（Munro Ferguson）——几年前都在我的牡蛎湾马球队打过球。汉密尔顿·菲什已经显露出他是我们最出色的军士之一。他身材魁伟，力大无穷、耐力超群、胆气过人，天生就是吃兵粮的料。他从不抱怨，任何差事从不推诿，而在部下面前又极有威望。他把中士当得如此出色，以至于一心要把最精锐之士收入麾下的卡普伦上尉离开坦帕时把他要了过去——因为菲什原属的连队留在了后方。那天夜里我们围着跳动的火光站着，我不禁暗自赞叹卡普伦和菲什——上尉与中士——那副虎虎有生气的体魄。他们的筋骨仿佛钢铸，足以扛住一切疲乏；满面都是健康的红光；眼里闪耀着高昂的决意与炽热的渴望。纵观全军，也找不出两个比他们更优秀的战斗军人典型、更能代表美国军人的人了。卡普伦正推敲着明日与西班牙人接战时的打法，菲什不时插问一句。两人都满心渴望一显身手，也都有理由自信：只要活下来，他们必将赢得体面的声名，在各自选定的事业中步步高升。十二个小时之内，两人双双殒命。

我刚躺下，伍德在将近午夜时回来了。他已与扬将军把整个作战方案推演了一遍。我们将在日出时分朝圣地亚哥进发：扬将军率第十团的四个连和第一团的四个连，沿穿过山谷的大路推进；伍德上校则率我们的八个连走左边的山间小径。这条小径前行约六公里后，在大道翻越山脉支岭、转向下坡通往圣地亚哥之处与谷中大道相接。西班牙人的防线就设在大路与小径的交汇点上。

在描述我们在这场战斗中的作为之前，有必要先讲讲扬将军的那一份，因为整场战斗自然都在他的指挥之下，右翼的战事更由他亲自督阵。扬将军从古巴部队的指挥官卡斯蒂略将军（General Castillo）那里，得到了前方地形的详尽介绍。卡斯蒂略将军向扬承诺，只要他实施武力侦察、切实查明西班牙人的兵力，便拨八百名古巴人助战。然而这承诺的古巴援兵终归落了空——前一天被西班牙人击退的那些古巴人，直到战斗打完才在火线上露面。

===Original===
General Young had in his immediate command a squadron of the First Regular Cavalry, two hundred and forty-four strong, under the command of Major Bell, and a squadron of the Tenth Regular Cavalry, two hundred and twenty strong, under the command of Major Norvell. He also had two Hotchkiss mountain guns, under Captain Watson of the Tenth. He started at a quarter before six in the morning, accompanied by Captain A. L. Mills, as aide. It was at half-past seven that Captain Mills, with a patrol of two men in advance, discovered the Spaniards as they lay across where the two roads came together, some of them in pits, others simply lying in the heavy jungle, while on their extreme right they occupied a big ranch. Where General Young struck them they held a high ridge a little to the left of his front, this ridge being separated by a deep ravine from the hill-trail still farther to the left, down which the Rough Riders were advancing. That is, their forces occupied a range of high hills in the form of an obtuse angle, the salient being toward the space between the American forces, while there were advance parties along both roads. There were stone breastworks flanked by blockhouses on that part of the ridge where the two trails came together. The place was called Las Guasimas, from trees of that name in the neighborhood.

General Young, who was riding a mule, carefully examined the Spanish position in person. He ordered the canteens of the troops to be filled, placed the Hotchkiss battery in concealment about nine hundred yards from the Spanish lines, and then deployed the white regulars, with the colored regulars in support, having sent a Cuban guide to try to find Colonel Wood and warn him. He did not attack immediately, because he knew that Colonel Wood, having a more difficult route, would require a longer time to reach the position. During the delay General Wheeler arrived; he had been up since long before dawn, to see that everything went well. Young informed him of the dispositions and plan of attack he made. General Wheeler approved of them, and with excellent judgment left General Young a free hand to fight his battle.

So, about eight o’clock Young began the fight with his Hotchkiss guns, he himself being up on the firing-line. No sooner had the Hotchkiss one-pounders opened than the Spaniards opened fire in return, most of the time firing by volleys executed in perfect time, almost as on parade. They had a couple of light guns, which our people thought were quick firers. The denseness of the jungle and the fact that they used absolutely smokeless powder, made it exceedingly difficult to place exactly where they were, and almost immediately Young, who always liked to get as close as possible to his enemy, began to push his troops forward. They were deployed on both sides of the road in such thick jungle that it was only here and there that they could possibly see ahead, and some confusion, of course, ensued, the support gradually getting mixed with the advance. Captain Beck took A Troop of the Tenth in on the left, next Captain Galbraith’s troop of the First; two other troops of the Tenth were on the extreme right. Through the jungle ran wire fences here and there, and as the troops got to the ridge they encountered precipitous heights. They were led most gallantly, as American regular officers always lead their men; and the men followed their leaders with the splendid courage always shown by the American regular soldier. There was not a single straggler among them, and in not one instance was an attempt made by any trooper to fall out in order to assist the wounded or carry back the dead, while so cool were they and so perfect their fire discipline, that in the entire engagement the expenditure of ammunition was not over ten rounds per man. Major Bell, who commanded the squadron, had his leg broken by a shot as he was leading his men. Captain Wainwright succeeded to the command of the squadron. Captain Knox was shot in the abdomen. He continued for some time giving orders to his troops, and refused to allow a man in the firing-line to assist him to the rear. His First Lieutenant, Byram, was himself shot, but continued to lead his men until the wound and the heat overcame him and he fell in a faint. The advance was pushed forward under General Young’s eye with the utmost energy, until the enemy’s voices could be heard in the entrenchments. The Spaniards kept up a very heavy firing, but the regulars would not be denied, and as they climbed the ridges the Spaniards broke and fled.

===Chinese===
扬将军直接掌握的兵力，是贝尔少校（Major Bell）指挥的第一正规骑兵团一个中队，全队二百四十四人；以及诺维尔少校（Major Norvell）指挥的第十正规骑兵团一个中队，全队二百二十人。他还有两门霍奇基斯山炮（Hotchkiss），由第十团的沃森上尉（Captain Watson）执掌。早晨差一刻六点，他在副官 A·L·米尔斯上尉（Captain A. L. Mills）陪同下出发。七点半钟，米尔斯上尉带着两名士兵在前巡逻，发现了横踞在两条道路汇合处的西班牙人：有的蹲在掩坑里，有的干脆趴在密林中，其极右翼还占据着一座大牧场。在扬将军向他们发起攻击的地方，他们占据着他正面稍偏左的一道高岭；这道高岭又与更左方的山间小径隔着一条深涧，莽骑兵（Rough Riders）们正沿着那条小径推进。也就是说，他们的兵力占据着呈钝角形展开的一列高地，凸角指向美军两支部队之间的空隙，且两条道路沿线都部署了前哨分队。在两条小径相会的那段山岭上，筑有石砌胸墙，两翼辅以碉堡。这地方名叫拉斯瓜西马斯（Las Guasimas），得名于附近生长的同名树木。

扬将军骑着一头骡子，亲自仔细察看了西班牙军的阵地。他命令各连灌满水壶，把霍奇基斯炮隐蔽配置在距西班牙人防线约八百米处，随后展开白人正规军，以黑人正规军作支援，同时派了一名古巴向导去设法寻找伍德上校、向他示警。他没有立即进攻，因为他知道伍德上校那一路更加难走，需要更长时间才能抵达阵地。就在这段等待中，惠勒将军赶到了；天亮前许久他就起身了，为的是亲自察看各项事宜是否妥帖。扬向他通报了自己所作的部署和攻击计划。惠勒将军深表赞同，并以极高明的判断放手让扬将军自主指挥这一仗。

于是，八点钟左右，扬用霍奇基斯炮开启了战斗，他本人就顶在火线上。一磅炮刚一开火，西班牙人便还击，大多数时候以齐射进行，节拍分毫不乱，简直像阅兵一般。他们有两门轻炮，我方将士还以为是速射炮。丛林浓密，加上他们使用的又是全然无烟的火药，使人极难判定他们的确切位置；而扬这个人向来越贴近敌人越好，几乎立刻便开始把部队向前压。部队沿大路两侧展开，丛林密得只有零星几处才望得见前方，难免出现一些混乱，支援队渐渐与前卫搅在了一起。贝克上尉（Captain Beck）率第十团的 A 连从左侧楔入，挨着的是第一团加尔布雷思上尉（Captain Galbraith）的连；第十团的另外两个连位于极右翼。丛林间处处横着铁丝网，部队抵近山岭时又遭遇陡峭的高坡。军官们率队冲锋时英勇卓绝，一如美国正规军军官素来的风范；士兵们追随长官，展现出美国正规军士兵一贯的堂堂勇气。他们当中没有一个掉队的，也从未有一名士兵为救护伤员或抬回死者而离队的先例；而且他们如此沉着、射击纪律如此严整，以致整场战斗中人均耗弹不足十发。指挥中队的贝尔少校率队冲锋时腿部中弹骨折。韦恩赖特上尉（Captain Wainwright）接掌了中队。诺克斯上尉（Captain Knox）腹部中弹，仍继续向本连下达命令好一阵子，并且拒绝让火线上的任何一名士兵搀扶他去后方。他的中尉拜勒姆（Byram）自己也中了弹，却坚持率队前进，直到创伤与暑热将他压垮、晕厥倒地。在扬将军的亲自注视下，前卫以极充沛的锐气向前推进，直到听得见堑壕里敌人的说话声。西班牙人的火力始终很猛，但正规军锐不可当；待他们攀上山岭，西班牙人便崩溃溃逃了。

===Original===
Meanwhile, at six o’clock, the Rough Riders began their advance. We first had to climb a very steep hill. Many of the men, footsore and weary from their march of the preceding day, found the pace up this hill too hard, and either dropped their bundles or fell out of line, with the result that we went into action with less than five hundred men﻿—as, in addition to the stragglers, a detachment had been left to guard the baggage on shore. At the time I was rather inclined to grumble to myself about Wood setting so fast a pace, but when the fight began I realized that it had been absolutely necessary, as otherwise we should have arrived late and the regulars would have had very hard work indeed.

Tiffany, by great exertions, had corralled a couple of mules and was using them to transport the Colt automatic guns in the rear of the regiment. The dynamite gun was not with us, as mules for it could not be obtained in time.

Captain Capron’s troop was in the lead, it being chosen for the most responsible and dangerous position because of Capron’s capacity. Four men, headed by Sergeant Hamilton Fish, went first; a support of twenty men followed some distance behind; and then came Capron and the rest of his troop, followed by Wood, with whom General Young had sent Lieutenants Smedburg and Rivers as aides. I rode close behind, at the head of the other three troops of my squadron, and then came Brodie at the head of his squadron. The trail was so narrow that for the most part the men marched in single file, and it was bordered by dense, tangled jungle, through which a man could with difficulty force his way; so that to put out flankers was impossible, for they could not possibly have kept up with the march of the column. Every man had his canteen full. There was a Cuban guide at the head of the column, but he ran away as soon as the fighting began. There were also with us, at the head of the column, two men who did not run away, who, though noncombatants﻿—newspaper correspondents﻿—showed as much gallantry as any soldier in the field. They were Edward Marshall and Richard Harding Davis.

===Chinese===
与此同时，六点钟，莽骑兵开始了推进。我们先得攀一座非常陡的山。许多士兵经前一天行军已脚伤体乏，觉得冲上这座山的节奏实在吃力，有的扔掉了行囊，有的掉了队，结果我们投入战斗时不足五百人——因为除掉队者外，还留了一支分队在岸上看管行李。当时我心里还挺有些埋怨伍德把速度带得这么紧，可战斗一打响我就明白了：这绝对是必要的，否则我们便会迟到，正规军那一仗就真要无比艰苦了。

蒂法尼（Tiffany）费了九牛二虎之力圈住两匹骡子，用它们在全团后方驮运柯尔特速射自动炮。甘油炸药炮（dynamite gun）没有随行，因为驮运它的骡子没能及时弄到。

卡普伦上尉的连走在最前头——这个责任最重、危险也最大的位置之所以派给它，正是看中卡普伦的才干。以汉密尔顿·菲什中士为首的四名士兵充任尖兵；一支二十人的支援队在后面隔开一段距离跟进；再后面是卡普伦和他连里的其余人马，伍德紧随其后——扬将军派了斯梅德堡（Smedburg）与里弗斯（Rivers）两名中尉到他身边任副官。我紧随其后骑行，统带本中队其余三个连；再后面是布罗迪率他的中队。小径窄得部队大半时间只能单行前进，两侧是密密缠绕的丛林，一个人硬挤过去都难；因此根本无法放出侧卫，因为他们绝无可能跟上行军的纵队。每人的水壶都灌得满满的。纵队头部有一名古巴向导，可战斗刚一打响他就跑了。与我们一起走在纵队头部的，还有两个没有跑的人；他们虽是非战斗人员——随军记者——却展现了不逊于战场上任何军人的英勇。他们是爱德华·马歇尔（Edward Marshall）与理查德·哈丁·戴维斯（Richard Harding Davis）。

===Original===
After reaching the top of the hill the walk was very pleasant. Now and then we came to glades or rounded hill-shoulders, whence we could look off for some distance. The tropical forest was very beautiful, and it was a delight to see the strange trees, the splendid royal palms and a tree which looked like a flat-topped acacia, and which was covered with a mass of brilliant scarlet flowers. We heard many bird-notes, too, the cooing of doves and the call of a great brush cuckoo. Afterward we found that the Spanish guerillas imitated these bird-calls, but the sounds we heard that morning, as we advanced through the tropic forest, were from birds, not guerillas, until we came right up to the Spanish lines. It was very beautiful and very peaceful, and it seemed more as if we were off on some hunting excursion than as if were about to go into a sharp and bloody little fight.

Of course, we accommodated our movements to those of the men in front. After marching for somewhat over an hour, we suddenly came to a halt, and immediately afterward Colonel Wood sent word down the line that the advance guard had come upon a Spanish outpost. Then the order was passed to fill the magazines, which was done.

The men were totally unconcerned, and I do not think they realized that any fighting was at hand; at any rate, I could hear the group nearest me discussing in low murmurs, not the Spaniards, but the conduct of a certain cowpuncher in quitting work on a ranch and starting a saloon in some New Mexican town. In another minute, however, Wood sent me orders to deploy three troops to the right of the trail, and to advance when we became engaged; while, at the same time, the other troops, under Major Brodie, were deployed to the left of the trail where the ground was more open than elsewhere﻿—one troop being held in reserve in the centre, besides the reserves on each wing. Later all the reserves were put into the firing-line.

===Chinese===
爬上山顶之后，一路走得十分惬意。我们不时走到林间空地或浑圆的山肩上，从那里可以望出去相当远。热带森林美不胜收，看到那些奇树异木真是一桩乐事——气派的王棕，还有一种形似平顶金合欢的树，满树覆着一团团明艳的猩红花朵。我们还听到许多鸟鸣：鸽子的咕咕声，还有大灌丛杜鹃的啼唤。后来我们才知道，西班牙的游击队员会模仿这些鸟叫，但那天早晨我们穿越热带森林推进时听到的，都是鸟儿而非游击队员的声音，直到我们直逼西班牙人的防线为止。一切都是那么美丽、那么安宁，倒更像是出门游猎，而不像是即将投入一场短促而血腥的小战斗。

当然，我们的行动得迁就前面的人。行军一小时多一点之后，我们突然停了下来，紧接着伍德上校传令下来：前卫已与西班牙人的前哨遭遇。随后又传下给弹仓压满子弹的命令，大家照办了。

士兵们全然不以为意，我看他们并没有意识到战斗已迫在眉睫；总之，我能听见离我最近的那群人低声议论的，不是西班牙人，而是某个赶牛人辞掉牧场的活计、跑到新墨西哥某镇上去开酒馆的是非长短。然而不过一分钟，伍德便给我传来命令：以三个连在小径右侧展开，一俟接火即向前进；与此同时，其余各连在布罗迪少校统率下于小径左侧展开，那里地面比别处开阔——除两翼各置预备队外，中央还留了一个连作预备队。后来，所有预备队都投入了火线。

===Original===
To the right the jungle was quite thick, and we had barely begun to deploy when a crash in front announced that the fight was on. It was evidently very hot, and L Troop had its hands full; so I hurried my men up abreast of them. So thick was the jungle that it was very difficult to keep together, especially when there was no time for delay, and while I got up Llewellen’s troops and Kane’s platoon of K Troop, the rest of K Troop under Captain Jenkins which, with Bucky O’Neill’s troop, made up the right wing, were behind, and it was some time before they got into the fight at all.

Meanwhile I had gone forward with Llewellen, Greenway, Kane and their troopers until we came out on a kind of shoulder, jutting over a ravine, which separated us from a great ridge on our right. It was on this ridge that the Spaniards had some of their intrenchments, and it was just beyond this ridge that the Valley Road led, up which the regulars were at that very time pushing their attack; but, of course, at the moment we knew nothing of this. The effect of the smokeless powder was remarkable. The air seemed full of the rustling sound of the Mauser bullets, for the Spaniards knew the trails by which we were advancing, and opened heavily on our position. Moreover, as we advanced we were, of course, exposed, and they could see us and fire. But they themselves were entirely invisible. The jungle covered everything, and not the faintest trace of smoke was to be seen in any direction to indicate from whence the bullets came. It was some time before the men fired; Llewellen, Kane, and I anxiously studying the ground to see where our opponents were, and utterly unable to find out.

We could hear the faint reports of the Hotchkiss guns and the reply of two Spanish guns, and the Mauser bullets were singing through the trees over our heads, making a noise like the humming of telephone wires; but exactly where they came from we could not tell. The Spaniards were firing high and for the most part by volleys, and their shooting was not very good, which perhaps was not to be wondered at, as they were a long way off. Gradually, however, they began to get the range and occasionally one of our men would crumple up. In no case did the man make any outcry when hit, seeming to take it as a matter of course; at the outside, making only such a remark as: “Well, I got it that time.” With hardly an exception, there was no sign of flinching. I say with hardly an exception, for though I personally did not see an instance, and though all the men at the front behaved excellently, yet there were a very few men who lagged behind and drifted back to the trail over which we had come. The character of the fight put a premium upon such conduct, and afforded a very severe test for raw troops; because the jungle was so dense that as we advanced in open order, every man was, from time to time, left almost alone and away from the eyes of his officers. There was unlimited opportunity for dropping out without attracting notice, while it was peculiarly hard to be exposed to the fire of an unseen foe, and to see men dropping under it, and yet to be, for some time, unable to return it, and also to be entirely ignorant of what was going on in any other part of the field.

===Chinese===
右侧的丛林相当密，我们刚刚开始展开，前方一阵爆响便宣告战斗已经打响。那里的战况显然极其激烈，L 连穷于应付；于是我催促手下赶上去与他们齐头并进。丛林密得队伍极难收拢，尤其是在刻不容缓之际；我把卢埃伦的连和凯恩（Kane）的 K 连一个排带上来时，K 连其余人员在詹金斯上尉（Captain Jenkins）率领下——他们与巴基·奥尼尔（Bucky O'Neill）的连共同构成右翼——还落在后面，过了一阵子他们才总算投入战斗。

这当口，我已同卢埃伦、格林韦、凯恩和他们的士兵前出到一处山肩上；它探临一道深涧，把我们与右方一道大山岭分隔开来。西班牙人的一部分堑壕就设在那道山岭上，而山谷大路正通到山岭的那一侧，正规军此刻正沿着它发起攻击；不过我们当时对此一无所知。无烟火药的效果实在触目惊心。空气中似乎满是毛瑟子弹掠过的嗖嗖声，因为西班牙人摸清了我们推进的小径，正朝我们的位置猛烈开火。况且我们推进时自然暴露在外，他们看得见我们，尽可以从容瞄准。可他们自己却全然不见踪影。丛林遮蔽了一切，四面八方连一丝最淡的硝烟也看不见，无从判断子弹究竟从何而来。过了好一阵子士兵们才开枪；卢埃伦、凯恩和我焦灼地端详地形，想找出对手的位置，却全然无从捉摸。

我们听得见霍奇基斯炮低沉的炮声和两门西班牙火炮的还击；毛瑟子弹从头顶的树梢间穿行而过，嗡嗡作响，像电话线的鸣响；可它们究竟从何处射来，我们说不上来。西班牙人打得偏高，多数时候用齐射，枪法并不很高明——这或许不足为奇，他们隔得远呢。然而渐渐地他们摸到了距离，偶尔我们这边便有人中弹瘫倒。无论哪一次，中弹的人都不哼一声，仿佛把中弹看作理所当然；充其量来一句：“好嘛，这回我挨了一下。”几乎无人有半分畏缩。我说“几乎”，是因为虽然我本人未曾亲见一例，虽然前沿的士兵个个表现卓越，但确有极少数人落在后面，慢慢蹭回了我们一路走来的那条小径。这场战斗的性质恰恰助长了这种行为，对新兵更是一场极其严峻的考验；因为丛林太密，我们疏开队形推进时，每个人都不时落到几乎孤身一人、脱离军官视线的境地。溜号而不被察觉的机会无穷无尽，而承受一个看不见的敌人的射击、眼见身边的人接连栽倒、自己却一时无法还击、而且对战场其余地段的动静一概懵然无知——这滋味尤其难熬。

===Original===
It was Richard Harding Davis who gave us our first opportunity to shoot back with effect. He was behaving precisely like my officers, being on the extreme front of the line, and taking every opportunity to study with his glasses the ground where we thought the Spaniards were. I had tried some volley firing at points where I rather doubtfully believed the Spaniards to be, but had stopped firing and was myself studying the jungle-covered mountain ahead with my glasses, when Davis suddenly said: “There they are, Colonel; look over there; I can see their hats near that glade,” pointing across the valley to our right. In a minute I, too, made out the hats, and then pointed them out to three or four of our best shots, giving them my estimate of the range. For a minute or two no result followed, and I kept raising the range, at the same time getting more men on the firing-line. Then, evidently, the shots told, for the Spaniards suddenly sprang out of the cover through which we had seen their hats, and ran to another spot; and we could now make out a large number of them.

I accordingly got all of my men up in line and began quick firing. In a very few minutes our bullets began to do damage, for the Spaniards retreated to the left into the jungle, and we lost sight of them. At the same moment a big body of men who, it afterward turned out, were Spaniards, came in sight along the glade, following the retreat of those whom we had just driven from the trenches. We supposed that there was a large force of Cubans with General Young, not being aware that these Cubans had failed to make their appearance, and as it was impossible to tell the Cubans from the Spaniards, and as we could not decide whether these were Cubans following the Spaniards we had put to flight, or merely another troop of Spaniards retreating after the first (which was really the case) we dared not fire, and in a minute they had passed the glade and were out of sight.

At every halt we took advantage of the cover, sinking down behind any mound, bush, or tree trunk in the neighborhood. The trees, of course, furnished no protection from the Mauser bullets. Once I was standing behind a large palm with my head out to one side, very fortunately; for a bullet passed through the palm, filling my left eye and ear with the dust and splinters.

===Chinese===
正是理查德·哈丁·戴维斯，给了我们第一次有效还击的机会。他的做派与我的军官们毫无二致：身处全线的最前端，一有机会便用望远镜研究我们认为藏有西班牙人的地段。我曾在几个自己将信将疑的地点试过几轮齐射，之后便停了火，正亲自用望远镜观察前方丛林覆盖的山地，戴维斯忽然开口：“上校，他们在那儿；往那边看；我看得见那片林间空地附近有他们的帽子，”他指着山谷对面我们的右翼。一分钟后，我也辨认出了那些帽子，随即指给我方三四名最好的射手，并报出我对距离的估测。有一两分钟毫无战果，我便不断上调距离，同时把更多士兵派上火线。随后，枪弹显然奏了效：西班牙人突然从我们望见帽子的那片掩蔽中蹿出，奔向另一处地点；这下我们能看清他们为数众多的一大群了。

于是我把手下全部拉上战线，开始急速射击。没几分钟，我们的子弹便开始奏效，西班牙人向左退入丛林，从我们视野中消失了。就在同一时刻，一大队人马顺着那片林间空地进入视野，跟在我们刚刚逐出堑壕的那批人后面撤退——后来才弄清，他们是西班牙人。我们原以为扬将军身边带有大批古巴人，并不知道这些古巴人根本没有露面；由于无从分辨古巴人和西班牙人，又断不定这些人是追击溃敌的古巴人，还是尾随第一批之后撤退的另一队西班牙兵（实情正是后者），我们没敢开火，转瞬之间他们已越过空地，消失得无影无踪。

每次停顿，我们都利用掩蔽物，就近伏到旁边的土丘、灌木或树干后面。树木当然挡不住毛瑟子弹。有一次我站在一棵大棕榈树后，头偏向一侧探出来——幸亏如此；一颗子弹穿透了棕榈树，打得尘土和木屑飞溅，糊满了我的左眼和左耳。

===Original===
No man was allowed to drop out to help the wounded. It was hard to leave them there in the jungle, where they might not be found again until the vultures and the land-crabs came, but war is a grim game and there was no choice. One of the men shot was Harry Heffner of G Troop, who was mortally wounded through the hips. He fell without uttering a sound, and two of his companions dragged him behind a tree. Here he propped himself up and asked to be given his canteen and his rifle, which I handed to him. He then again began shooting, and continued loading and firing until the line moved forward and we left him alone, dying in the gloomy shade. When we found him again, after the fight, he was dead.

At one time, as I was out of touch with that part of my wing commanded by Jenkins and O’Neill, I sent Greenway, with Sergeant Russell, a New Yorker, and trooper Rowland, a New Mexican cowpuncher, down in the valley to find out where they were. To do this the three had to expose themselves to a very severe fire, but they were not men to whom this mattered. Russell was killed; the other two returned and reported to me the position of Jenkins and O’Neill. They then resumed their places on the firing-line. After awhile I noticed blood coming out of Rowland’s side and discovered that he had been shot, although he did not seem to be taking any notice of it. He said the wound was only slight, but as I saw he had broken a rib, I told him to go to the rear to the hospital. After some grumbling he went, but fifteen minutes later he was back on the firing-line again and said he could not find the hospital﻿—which I doubted. However, I then let him stay until the end of the fight.

After we had driven the Spaniards off from their position to our right, the firing seemed to die away so far as we were concerned, for the bullets no longer struck around us in such a storm as before, though along the rest of the line the battle was as brisk as ever. Soon we saw troops appearing across the ravine, not very far from where we had seen the Spaniards whom we had thought might be Cubans. Again we dared not fire, and carefully studied the newcomers with our glasses; and this time we were right, for we recognized our own cavalrymen. We were by no means sure that they recognized us, however, and were anxious that they should, but it was very difficult to find a clear spot in the jungle from which to signal; so Sergeant Lee of Troop K climbed a tree and from its summit waved the troop guidon. They waved their guidon back, and as our right wing was now in touch with the regulars, I left Jenkins and O’Neill to keep the connection, and led Llewellen’s troop back to the path to join the rest of the regiment, which was evidently still in the thick of the fight. I was still very much in the dark as to where the main body of the Spanish forces were, or exactly what lines the battle was following, and was very uncertain what I ought to do; but I knew it could not be wrong to go forward, and I thought I would find Wood and then see what he wished me to do. I was in a mood to cordially welcome guidance, for it was most bewildering to fight an enemy whom one so rarely saw.

===Chinese===
谁也不许离队去救护伤员。把他们撂在丛林里，实在于心难忍——说不定要到秃鹫和陆蟹寻来时才会被人再度发现——可战争是一门冷酷的行当，别无选择。中弹者之一是 G 连的哈里·赫夫纳（Harry Heffner），髋部被射穿，伤势致命。他一声不吭地栽倒，两名同伴把他拖到一棵树后。他在那里支起身子，要求把水壶和步枪递给他，我照办了。随后他再度开枪，装填、射击，一直打到战线前移、我们把他独自留在那片幽暗的树荫里等死。战斗结束后我们再找到他时，他已经死了。

有那么一阵子，我同本翼由詹金斯和奥尼尔指挥的那部分失去了联络，便派格林韦带着纽约人拉塞尔中士（Sergeant Russell）和新墨西哥赶牛人、骑兵罗兰（Rowland）下到谷底，去查明他们的位置。为办成这件事，三人不得不暴露在极其凶猛的火力之下，但他们不是把生死放在心上的人。拉塞尔阵亡了；另外两人回来向我报告了詹金斯和奥尼尔的位置，随即回到火线各自的岗位上。过了一会儿，我注意到罗兰的肋部渗出血来，才发现他中了弹，而他自己似乎全然不当回事。他说伤势甚轻，可我看出他断了一根肋骨，便叫他回后方进医院。他嘟囔了一阵才去，可十五分钟后他又回到了火线上，说找不着医院——这话我很怀疑。不过，后来我就让他留到了战斗结束。

我们把西班牙人从右翼的阵地上赶跑之后，就我们这一段而言，枪声似乎渐渐止息，子弹不再像先前那样狂风暴雨般打在我们四周，尽管战线其余地段的战斗依旧激烈。很快，我们看见深涧对面出现一支部队，离我们先前望见那批疑似古巴人的西班牙人之处不远。我们照旧不敢开火，用望远镜仔细端详这些新来者；这一回我们看对了，认出是自己人的骑兵。然而我们拿不准他们是否也认出了我们，又巴望他们认出来，可丛林里很难找到一块开阔地发信号；于是 K 连的李中士（Sergeant Lee）爬上一棵树，从树顶挥动连旗。对方也挥旗回应。既然右翼已与正规军建立了联络，我便留下詹金斯和奥尼尔保持衔接，自己带着卢埃伦的连回到小径上，去与本团其余部队会合——那边显然仍在鏖战。西班牙军主力究竟在哪里、战斗正沿着怎样的战线展开，我心里依然全无头绪，也不知道自己究竟该做什么；但我知道向前总归不会错，我想先找到伍德，再看他有何吩咐。这时的我巴不得有人指点迷津，因为同一个几乎照不着面的敌人作战，实在令人晕头转向。

===Original===
I had not seen Wood since the beginning of the skirmish, when he hurried forward. When the firing opened some of the men began to curse. “Don’t swear﻿—shoot!” growled Wood, as he strode along the path leading his horse, and everyone laughed and became cool again. The Spanish outposts were very near our advance guard, and some minutes of the hottest kind of firing followed before they were driven back and slipped off through the jungle to their main lines in the rear.

Here, at the very outset of our active service, we suffered the loss of two as gallant men as ever wore uniform. Sergeant Hamilton Fish at the extreme front, while holding the point up to its work and firing back where the Spanish advance guards lay, was shot and instantly killed; three of the men with him were likewise hit. Captain Capron, leading the advance guard in person, and displaying equal courage and coolness in the way that he handled them, was also struck, and died a few minutes afterward. The command of the troop then devolved upon the First Lieutenant, young Thomas. Like Capron, Thomas was the fifth in line from father to son who had served in the American army, though in his case it was in the volunteer and not the regular service; the four preceding generations had furnished soldiers respectively to the Revolutionary War, the War of 1812, the Mexican War, and the Civil War. In a few minutes Thomas was shot through the leg, and the command devolved upon the Second Lieutenant, Day (a nephew of “Albemarle” Cushing, he who sunk the great Confederate ram). Day, who proved himself to be one of our most efficient officers, continued to handle the men to the best possible advantage, and brought them steadily forward. L Troop was from the Indian Territory. The whites, Indians, and half-breeds in it, all fought with equal courage. Captain McClintock was hurried forward to its relief with his Troop B of Arizona men. In a few minutes he was shot through the leg and his place was taken by his First Lieutenant, Wilcox, who handled his men in the same soldierly manner that Day did.

Among the men who showed marked courage and coolness was the tall color-sergeant, Wright; the colors were shot through three times.

===Chinese===
战斗刚打响伍德匆匆向前赶去之后，我就再没见到过他。开火时，有些士兵开始咒骂。“别骂娘——开枪！”伍德牵着马、大步走在小径上低喝道，众人大笑，随即又冷静下来。西班牙人的前哨离我们的前卫很近，接下来是几分钟最炽烈的对射，他们才被击退，穿过丛林溜向后方的主体防线。

就在我们实战生涯的开端，我们便痛失穿军装的人中最英勇的两位。汉密尔顿·菲什中士在全线的最前端，一面督率尖兵组坚守职责、朝西班牙前卫的潜伏处回击，一面中弹，当场阵亡；与他同组的三名士兵也相继中弹。卡普伦上尉亲自率领前卫，在指挥上展现出同样的胆识与沉着，也身负重伤，几分钟后捐躯。连队的指挥权遂落到中尉小托马斯（Thomas）肩上。与卡普伦一样，托马斯也是父子相承在美国军队中服役的第五代，只是他这一支是在义勇军而非正规军；前面四代分别在独立战争、1812 年战争、美墨战争和南北战争中出过军人。几分钟后，托马斯腿部被射穿，指挥权又移交至少尉戴（Day）——他是“阿尔伯马尔”库欣（"Albemarle" Cushing）的侄子，就是那位击沉邦联巨型铁甲冲角舰的库欣。戴后来证明是我们最能干的军官之一，他继续把部队调度得滴水不漏，稳稳带向前进。L 连来自印第安领地（Indian Territory），连里的白人、印第安人和混血儿作战同样勇敢。麦克林托克上尉（Captain McClintock）奉命率他那一连亚利桑那人赶来接应。几分钟后他也腿部中弹，其职由他的中尉威尔科克斯（Wilcox）接替，后者带兵的老练干练与戴如出一辙。

表现出过人胆识与沉着的人当中，有高个子掌旗中士赖特（Wright）；军旗被子弹三次射穿。

===Original===
When I had led G Troop back to the trail I ran ahead of them, passing the dead and wounded men of L Troop, passing young Fish as he lay with glazed eyes under the rank tropic growth to one side of the trail. When I came to the front I found the men spread out in a very thin skirmish line, advancing through comparatively open ground, each man taking advantage of what cover he could, while Wood strolled about leading his horse, Brodie being close at hand. How Wood escaped being hit, I do not see, and still less how his horse escaped. I had left mine at the beginning of the action, and was only regretting that I had not left my sword with it, as it kept getting between my legs when I was tearing my way through the jungle. I never wore it again in action. Lieutenant Rivers was with Wood, also leading his horse. Smedburg had been sent off on the by no means pleasant task of establishing communications with Young.

Very soon after I reached the front, Brodie was hit, the bullet shattering one arm and whirling him around as he stood. He had kept on the extreme front all through, his presence and example keeping his men entirely steady, and he at first refused to go to the rear; but the wound was very painful, and he became so faint that he had to be sent. Thereupon, Wood directed me to take charge of the left wing in Brodie’s place, and to bring it forward; so over I went.

I now had under me Captains Luna, Muller, and Houston, and I began to take them forward, well spread out, through the high grass of a rather open forest. I noticed Goodrich, of Houston’s troop, tramping along behind his men, absorbed in making them keep at good intervals from one another and fire slowly with careful aim. As I came close up to the edge of the troop, he caught a glimpse of me, mistook me for one of his own skirmishers who was crowding in too closely, and called out, “Keep your interval, sir; keep your interval, and go forward.”

===Chinese===
我把 G 连带回小径之后，便撇下他们往前跑，一路经过 L 连的死伤士兵，经过躺在路边繁茂热带草木之下、双眼已然失神的小菲什。赶到前面时，只见士兵们散成一条极薄的散兵线，正穿过相对开阔的地带推进，人人各自利用力所能及的掩蔽，而伍德牵着马信步走动，布罗迪就在近旁。伍德是怎么躲过中弹的，我看不明白；他的马又是怎么躲过的，我更想不通。交火一开始我就把自己的马留下了，只后悔没把军刀也一并留下——在丛林里披荆斩棘之际，它老是横在我两腿之间碍事。此后我在战斗中再没有佩过它。里弗斯中尉跟着伍德，也牵着马。斯梅德堡则被打发去执行一项绝不轻松的任务——与扬建立通讯联络。

我抵达前沿后不久，布罗迪便中弹了，子弹打碎了他的一条手臂，打得他站在原地打了个旋。他自始至终顶在最前端，以自身的身影和表率把部下稳得纹丝不动；起初他拒绝去后方，但伤口痛得厉害，人又虚弱到站立不住，只得被送了下去。于是，伍德命我接替布罗迪执掌左翼并率之前进；我便过去了。

这时我手下有卢纳（Luna）、穆勒（Muller）和休斯敦（Houston）三位上尉，我开始让他们疏开队形，穿过一片相当疏朗的林间高草地向前推进。我注意到休斯敦连里的古德里奇（Goodrich）跟在自己士兵身后跋涉，一门心思督促他们彼此保持适当间隔、放慢射速、仔细瞄准。待我走近该连的边缘，他瞥见我，把我错当成自己手下一名挤得过近的散兵，扬声喊道：“保持间隔，长官；保持间隔，往前推进。”

===Original===
A perfect hail of bullets was sweeping over us as we advanced. Once I got a glimpse of some Spaniards, apparently retreating, far in the front, and to our right, and we fired a couple of rounds after them. Then I became convinced, after much anxious study, that we were being fired at from some large red-tiled buildings, part of a ranch on our front. Smokeless powder, and the thick cover in our front, continued to puzzle us, and I more than once consulted anxiously the officers as to the exact whereabouts of our opponents. I took a rifle from a wounded man and began to try shots with it myself. It was very hot and the men were getting exhausted, though at this particular time we were not suffering heavily from bullets, the Spanish fire going high. As we advanced, the cover became a little thicker and I lost touch of the main body under Wood; so I halted and we fired industriously at the ranch buildings ahead of us, some five hundred yards off. Then we heard cheering on the right, and I supposed that this meant a charge on the part of Wood’s men, so I sprang up and ordered the men to rush the buildings ahead of us. They came forward with a will. There was a moment’s heavy firing from the Spaniards, which all went over our heads, and then it ceased entirely. When we arrived at the buildings, panting and out of breath, they contained nothing but heaps of empty cartridge-shells and two dead Spaniards, shot through the head.

The country all around us was thickly forested, so that it was very difficult to see any distance in any direction. The firing had now died out, but I was still entirely uncertain as to exactly what had happened. I did not know whether the enemy had been driven back or whether it was merely a lull in the fight, and we might be attacked again; nor did I know what had happened in any other part of the line, while as I occupied the extreme left, I was not sure whether or not my flank was in danger. At this moment one of our men who had dropped out, arrived with the information (fortunately false) that Wood was dead. Of course, this meant that the command devolved upon me, and I hastily set about taking charge of the regiment. I had been particularly struck by the coolness and courage shown by Sergeants Dame and McIlhenny, and sent them out with small pickets to keep watch in front and to the left of the left wing. I sent other men to fill the canteens with water, and threw the rest out in a long line in a disused sunken road, which gave them cover, putting two or three wounded men, who had hitherto kept up with the fighting-line, and a dozen men who were suffering from heat exhaustion﻿—for the fighting and running under that blazing sun through the thick dry jungle was heartbreaking﻿—into the ranch buildings. Then I started over toward the main body, but to my delight encountered Wood himself, who told me the fight was over and the Spaniards had retreated. He also informed me that other troops were just coming up. The first to appear was a squadron of the Ninth Cavalry, under Major Dimick, which had hurried up to get into the fight, and was greatly disappointed to find it over. They took post in front of our lines, so that our tired men were able to get a rest, Captain McBlain, of the Ninth, good-naturedly giving us some points as to the best way to station our outposts. Then General Chaffee, rather glum at not having been in the fight himself, rode up at the head of some of his infantry, and I marched my squadron back to where the rest of the regiment was going into camp, just where the two trails came together, and beyond﻿—that is, on the Santiago side of﻿—the original Spanish lines.

The Rough Riders had lost eight men killed and thirty-four wounded, aside from two or three who were merely scratched and whose wounds were not reported. The First Cavalry, white, lost seven men killed and eight wounded; the Tenth Cavalry, colored, one man killed and ten wounded; so, out of 964 men engaged on our side, 16 were killed and 52 wounded. The Spaniards were under General Rubin, with, as second in command, Colonel Alcarez. They had two guns, and eleven companies of about a hundred men each: three belonging to the Puerto Rico regiment, three to the San Fernandino, two to the Talavero, two being so-called mobilized companies from the mineral districts, and one a company of engineers; over twelve hundred men in all, together with two guns.1

===Chinese===
我们推进时，密集如冰雹的子弹劈头盖脑地扫过。有一次，我远远瞥见前方偏右有一小股西班牙人似在撤退，我们朝他们打了几枪。随后，经过反复焦急的观察，我确信我们正遭到前方一座牧场中几栋红瓦大房子的射击。无烟火药加上前方浓密的掩蔽，始终困扰着我们，我不止一次焦灼地同军官们商讨对手的确切下落。我从一名伤员手里拿过一支步枪，亲自试射起来。天气酷热，士兵们渐显力竭，尽管在这个当口我们并未因子弹吃多大亏——西班牙人的火力偏高。随着推进，前方掩蔽物更密了些，我与伍德统率的主力失去了联络；于是我们停下，朝着前方约四百五十米开外的牧场房舍勤奋射击。这时右翼传来欢呼声，我料想那是伍德的人马发起冲锋了，便一跃而起，命令士兵们冲向前面的房舍。他们奋勇向前。西班牙人有一刹那火力极猛，但子弹全都从我们头顶飞过，随即彻底沉寂。等我们气喘吁吁赶到那几栋房子跟前，里面除了成堆的空弹壳和两个头部中弹的西班牙死人，什么也没有。

我们四周遍地密林，朝任何方向都难以望出多远。枪炮声此刻已然止息，但究竟出了什么状况，我心里依旧全然无数。我不知道敌军是已被击退，还是战斗仅仅暂歇、我们可能再度遭到攻击；我也不知道战线其余地段情形如何；而由于我身处极左翼，拿不准自己的侧翼是否有虞。正在这时，一名掉队归来的士兵带来消息（所幸是讹传）：伍德阵亡了。这当然意味着指挥权落到了我肩上，我赶忙着手接管全团。戴姆（Dame）与麦克尔亨尼（McIlhenny）两位中士的沉着勇敢给我印象尤深，我派他们各带小股警戒哨，在左翼前方及左侧警戒；另派一些人去灌水；其余的人则沿一条废弃的洼地旧路摆开一条长线——那里可以提供掩蔽——又把两三名一直咬住战斗线未掉的伤员，连同十几个中暑虚脱的士兵（在那样的烈日下穿越干燥密林又打又跑，实在磨人心志），一并安顿进牧场的房舍里。随后我动身往主力方向去，却喜出望外地迎面遇上伍德本人，他告诉我战斗结束了，西班牙人已经撤退。他还告知另有部队正开上来。最先出现的是迪米克少校（Major Dimick）率领的第九骑兵团一个中队，他们火速赶来得一参战，却发现仗已打完，大失所望。他们在我们战线前方就位，好让我们疲惫的士兵歇上一歇，第九团的麦克布莱恩上尉（Captain McBlain）还好心地指点我们布置前哨的门道。随后，查菲将军（General Chaffee）率部分步兵策马赶到——没能亲自上阵让他颇为郁郁——我便率本中队返回团里其余部队设营之处，那正是两条小径相会的地点，并且在西班牙人原防线之外——也就是朝圣地亚哥的一侧。

莽骑兵阵亡八人、负伤三十四人，另有二三人只是擦破了点皮、伤情未予申报，不在其列。第一骑兵团（白人）阵亡七人、负伤八人；第十骑兵团（黑人）阵亡一人、负伤十人；如此，我方参战的 964 人中，共 16 人阵亡、52 人负伤。西班牙军由鲁宾将军（General Rubin）统率，副手为阿尔卡雷斯上校（Colonel Alcarez）。他们有两门火炮和十一个连，每连约百人：三个连隶属波多黎各团，三个隶属圣费尔南迪诺团，两个隶属塔拉韦罗团，两个是来自矿区的所谓动员连，还有一个工兵连；总计一千二百余人，连同两门火炮。1

===Original===
General Rubin reported that he had repulsed the American attack, and Lieutenant Tejeiro states in his book that General Rubin forced the Americans to retreat, and enumerates the attacking force as consisting of three regular regiments of infantry, the Second Massachusetts and the Seventy-first New York (not one of which fired a gun or were anywhere near the battle), in addition to the sixteen dismounted troops of cavalry. In other words, as the five infantry regiments each included twelve companies, he makes the attacking force consist of just five times the actual amount. As for the “repulse,” our line never went back ten yards in any place, and the advance was practically steady; while an hour and a half after the fight began we were in complete possession of the entire Spanish position, and their troops were fleeing in masses down the road, our men being too exhausted to follow them.

General Rubin also reports that he lost but seven men killed. This is certainly incorrect, for Captain O’Neill and I went over the ground very carefully and counted eleven dead Spaniards, all of whom were actually buried by our burying squads. There were probably two or three men whom we missed, but I think that our official reports are incorrect in stating that forty-two dead Spaniards were found; this being based upon reports in which I think some of the Spanish dead were counted two or three times. Indeed, I should doubt whether their loss was as heavy as ours, for they were under cover, while we advanced, often in the open, and their main lines fled long before we could get to close quarters. It was a very difficult country, and a force of good soldiers resolutely handled could have held the pass with ease against two or three times their number. As it was, with a force half of regulars and half of volunteers, we drove out a superior number of Spanish regular troops, strongly posted, without suffering a very heavy loss. Although the Spanish fire was very heavy, it does not seem to me it was very well directed; and though they fired with great spirit while we merely stood at a distance and fired at them, they did not show much resolution, and when we advanced, always went back long before there was any chance of our coming into contact with them. Our men behaved very well indeed﻿—white regulars, colored regulars, and Rough Riders alike. The newspaper press failed to do full justice to the white regulars, in my opinion, from the simple reason that everybody knew that they would fight, whereas there had been a good deal of question as to how the Rough Riders, who were volunteer troops, and the Tenth Cavalry, who were colored, would behave; so there was a tendency to exalt our deeds at the expense of those of the First Regulars, whose courage and good conduct were taken for granted. It was a trying fight beyond what the losses show, for it is hard upon raw soldiers to be pitted against an unseen foe, and to advance steadily when their comrades are falling around them, and when they can only occasionally see a chance to retaliate. Wood’s experience in fighting Apaches stood him in good stead. An entirely raw man at the head of the regiment, conducting, as Wood was, what was practically an independent fight, would have been in a very trying position. The fight cleared the way toward Santiago, and we experienced no further resistance.

That afternoon we made camp and dined, subsisting chiefly on a load of beans which we found on one of the Spanish mules which had been shot. We also looked after the wounded. Dr. Church had himself gone out to the firing-line during the fight, and carried to the rear some of the worst wounded on his back or in his arms. Those who could walk had walked in to where the little field-hospital of the regiment was established on the trail. We found all our dead and all the badly wounded. Around one of the latter the big, hideous land-crabs had gathered in a gruesome ring, waiting for life to be extinct. One of our own men and most of the Spanish dead had been found by the vultures before we got to them; and their bodies were mangled, the eyes and wounds being torn.

===Chinese===
鲁宾将军报告说他击退了美军的进攻；特赫伊罗中尉（Lieutenant Tejeiro）也在其书中声称，鲁宾将军迫使美国人后退，并把进攻兵力开列为三个正规步兵团，外加马萨诸塞第二团和第七十一纽约义勇团（这几支部队一枪未发，也根本不在战场附近），再加上十六个下马作战的骑兵连。换句话说，由于这五个步兵团每团辖十二个连，他把进攻兵力恰好夸大了五倍。至于那场“击退”，我方战线在任何地点都从未后退十码，推进实际上始终稳步向前；战斗开始一个半小时后，我们已完全占领西班牙人的全部阵地，他们的部队正成群结队地沿大路溃逃，只是我们的士兵精疲力竭，无力追击。

鲁宾将军还报告说，己方仅阵亡七人。这肯定不确，因为奥尼尔上尉和我极其仔细地踏勘了战场，数出十一具西班牙人的尸体，全部由我们的掩埋队实际掩埋。可能还有两三个我们漏数的，但我认为我方官方报告所称发现四十二具西班牙人尸体也不准确——其所依据的战报里，恐怕有些西军死者被数了两三遍。说实话，我甚至怀疑他们的损失是否真比我们的重：他们躲在掩体后面，而我们常常在开阔地上推进；他们的主体防线早在我们逼近之前许久就已遁走。这是一片极其难攻的地形，一队精兵若指挥坚决，以一敌二敌三都可轻松扼守这道隘口。而实际上，我们以半正规军、半义勇军的兵力，把据守坚固阵地、数量占优的西班牙正规军逐了出去，自身损失并不为重。西班牙人的火力虽猛，依我看准头欠佳；我们只是远远站着朝他们射击时，他们打得很起劲，可一旦我们推进，他们总是在我们尚无机会与之接触之前许久便退走了。我们的士兵表现得的确非常出色——白人正规军、黑人正规军和莽骑兵同样优异。依我看，报界未能充分公正地对待白人正规军，原因很简单：谁都知道他们能打，而莽骑兵这支义勇部队、第十骑兵团这支黑人部队表现如何，此前颇多疑问；于是便有了以贬低第一正规骑兵团为代价来拔高我们战功的倾向——后者的勇气与良好表现被视作了理所当然。这场战斗的严酷远超伤亡数字所示，因为让新兵对抗看不见的敌人、在战友纷纷倒下之际仍稳步前进、只是偶尔才逮到还击的机会，实在是一种煎熬。伍德当年与阿帕奇人作战的经验，此刻大有用场。换一个全然的新手处在他那个位置上，像他这样实际上独立指挥一场战斗，处境会极其为难。这一仗扫清了通往圣地亚哥的道路，此后我们再未遭遇抵抗。

当天下午，我们设营吃饭，主要靠在一匹被打死的西班牙骡子身上搜出的一驮豆子果腹。我们还照看了伤员。战斗期间，丘奇医生（Dr. Church）亲自上了火线，或背或抱，把几名伤势最重的士兵送到后方。能走的都自己走到团里开设在小径上的小型战地医疗站去了。我们的阵亡者和重伤员全部寻获。其中一名重伤员周围，那些丑陋的大陆蟹已围成一圈森然的环，静候气息断绝。我们自己的一名阵亡者和大多数西班牙死者，在我们赶到之前就已被秃鹫光顾；尸体残缺不全，眼睛和伤口都被撕啄过。

===Original===
The Rough Rider who had been thus treated was in Bucky O’Neill’s troop; and as we looked at the body, O’Neill turned to me and asked, “Colonel, isn’t it Whitman who says of the vultures that ‘they pluck the eyes of princes and tear the flesh of kings’?” I answered that I could not place the quotation. Just a week afterward we were shielding his own body from the birds of prey.

One of the men who fired first, and who displayed conspicuous gallantry was a Cherokee half-breed, who was hit seven times, and of course had to go back to the States. Before he rejoined us at Montauk Point he had gone through a little private war of his own; for on his return he found that a cowboy had gone off with his sweetheart, and in the fight that ensued he shot his rival. Another man of L Troop who also showed marked gallantry was Elliot Cowdin. The men of the plains and mountains were trained by lifelong habit to look on life and death with iron philosophy. As I passed by a couple of tall, lank, Oklahoma cowpunchers, I heard one say, “Well, some of the boys got it in the neck!” to which the other answered with the grim plains proverb of the South: “Many a good horse dies.”

Thomas Isbell, a half-breed Cherokee in the squad under Hamilton Fish, was among the first to shoot and be shot at. He was wounded no less than seven times. The first wound was received by him two minutes after he had fired his first shot, the bullet going through his neck. The second hit him in the left thumb. The third struck near his right hip, passing entirely through the body. The fourth bullet (which was apparently from a Remington and not from a Mauser) went into his neck and lodged against the bone, being afterward cut out. The fifth bullet again hit his left hand. The sixth scraped his head and the seventh his neck. He did not receive all of the wounds at the same time, over half an hour elapsing between the first and the last. Up to receiving the last wound he had declined to leave the firing-line, but by that time he had lost so much blood that he had to be sent to the rear. The man’s wiry toughness was as notable as his courage.

===Chinese===
遭此残害的那名莽骑兵在巴基·奥尼尔的连里；我们望着遗体时，奥尼尔转头问我：“上校，说起秃鹫，那不是惠特曼的诗句吗——‘它们啄去王公的眼睛，撕下国王的血肉’？”我回答说，这话出自何处我记不真切了。仅仅一个星期之后，我们便在为他的遗体遮挡猛禽了。

最早开枪、表现尤为英勇的士兵之一，是个切罗基混血儿，身上七处中弹，自然只得回美国本土去了。他在蒙托克角（Montauk Point）归队之前，还经历过一场小小的私人战争：他回去时发现一个牛仔拐跑了他的心上人，随后爆发的争斗中，他一枪打翻了情敌。L 连另一个同样表现卓著的，是埃利奥特·考丁。这些平原与山地的汉子，一生的习惯早已把他们磨炼得用铁一般的冷峻看待生死。我从两个瘦高个的俄克拉何马赶牛人身边走过时，听见一个说：“唉，有几个小伙子把命丢了！”另一个用南方平原那句冷峻的谚语回答：“好马也难免一死。”

汉密尔顿·菲什班里的切罗基混血儿托马斯·伊斯贝尔（Thomas Isbell），是最早开枪、也最早挨枪的人之一。他负伤多达七处。第一处伤是在他打出第一枪两分钟后中的，子弹穿透颈部。第二处打中左手拇指。第三处打在右髋附近，贯穿全身。第四颗子弹（显然出自雷明顿而非毛瑟）射入颈部，抵在骨头上，后来被剜了出来。第五颗再度打中他的左手。第六颗擦过头部，第七颗擦过颈侧。这些伤并非一时所致，第一处与最后一处之间隔了半个多小时。直到最后一处负伤之前，他一直拒绝离开火线，但到那时他失血太多，只得被送往后方。这人钢筋铁骨般的坚韧，与他的勇气同样罕见。

===Original===
We improvised litters, and carried the more sorely wounded back to Siboney that afternoon and the next morning; the others walked. One of the men who had been most severely wounded was Edward Marshall, the correspondent, and he showed as much heroism as any soldier in the whole army. He was shot through the spine, a terrible and very painful wound, which we supposed meant that he would surely die; but he made no complaint of any kind, and while he retained consciousness persisted in dictating the story of the fight. A very touching incident happened in the improvised open-air hospital after the fight, where the wounded were lying. They did not groan, and made no complaint, trying to help one another. One of them suddenly began to hum, “My Country ’tis of Thee,” and one by one the others joined in the chorus, which swelled out through the tropic woods, where the victors lay in camp beside their dead. I did not see any sign among the fighting men, whether wounded or unwounded, of the very complicated emotions assigned to their kind by some of the realistic modern novelists who have written about battles. At the front everyone behaved quite simply and took things as they came, in a matter-of-course way; but there was doubtless, as is always the case, a good deal of panic and confusion in the rear where the wounded, the stragglers, a few of the packers, and two or three newspaper correspondents were, and in consequence the first reports sent back to the coast were of a most alarming character, describing, with minute inaccuracy, how we had run into ambush, etc. The packers with the mules which carried the rapid-fire guns were among those who ran, and they let the mules go in the jungle; in consequence the guns were never even brought to the firing-line, and only Fred Herrig’s skill as a trailer enabled us to recover them. By patient work he followed up the mules’ tracks in the forest until he found the animals.

Among the wounded who walked to the temporary hospital at Siboney was the trooper, Rowland, of whom I spoke before. There the doctors examined him, and decreed that his wound was so serious that he must go back to the States. This was enough for Rowland, who waited until nightfall and then escaped, slipping out of the window and making his way back to camp with his rifle and pack, though his wound must have made all movement very painful to him. After this, we felt that he was entitled to stay, and he never left us for a day, distinguishing himself again in the fight at San Juan.

Next morning we buried seven dead Rough Riders in a grave on the summit of the trail, Chaplain Brown reading the solemn burial service of the Episcopalians, while the men stood around with bared heads and joined in singing, “Rock of Ages.” Vast numbers of vultures were wheeling round and round in great circles through the blue sky overhead. There could be no more honorable burial than that of these men in a common grave﻿—Indian and cowboy, miner, packer, and college athlete﻿—the man of unknown ancestry from the lonely Western plains, and the man who carried on his watch the crests of the Stuyvesants and the Fishes, one in the way they had met death, just as during life they had been one in their daring and their loyalty.

===Chinese===
我们临时赶制担架，当天下午和次日清晨把重伤员抬回锡博内；其余的人自己走着去。伤势最重的人之一是记者爱德华·马歇尔，他展现的英雄气概不逊于全军任何一名士兵。他被子弹打穿脊椎，那是一种可怕而剧痛的伤，我们都以为他必死无疑；可他毫无怨言，只要神志尚清，便坚持口述这场战斗的报道。战斗结束后，在伤员们躺卧的临时露天医院里，出现了一幕动人的情景。他们不呻吟，不叫苦，还彼此扶助。忽然有人哼起《我的祖国，这是为你》（"My Country 'tis of Thee"），其余的人一个接一个加入合唱，歌声在热带林莽间回荡升腾——得胜的将士们正伴着阵亡的战友在林中扎营。在作战的士兵中间，无论伤者还是未伤者，我都没有看到某些描写战争的现代写实小说家硬派给他们的那种复杂纠结的情绪。在前线，人人行事质朴，遇到什么便担当什么，全然出于本分；但在后方——伤员、掉队者、几名驮夫和两三名随军记者所在之处——无疑照例有不少惊慌与混乱，结果最先发回海岸的战报耸人听闻，以细致入微的不准确，描述了我们如何中了埋伏云云。驮运速射枪炮的那几名骡夫也在溃逃者之列，他们把骡子撒在了丛林里；结果那些枪炮压根未能运上火线，全靠弗雷德·赫里希（Fred Herrig）的循迹本领，我们才把它们找了回来。他耐着性子在林中一路追索骡子的蹄印，终于找回了那些牲口。

步行前往锡博内临时医院的伤员当中，就有我前面说过的骑兵罗兰。医生们给他作了检查，断定伤势严重，必须送回美国本土。对罗兰来说，这话就足够了：他捱到夜幕降临便逃之夭夭，翻窗而出，带着步枪和行囊一路摸回营地——尽管那伤口必然使他每动一下都痛楚难当。经此一遭，我们都觉得他够资格留下了；此后他一天也不曾离开我们，后来在圣胡安（San Juan）之战中再度建功。

次日清晨，我们把七名阵亡的莽骑兵安葬在小径顶端的一座墓穴里，布朗随军牧师（Chaplain Brown）诵念圣公会庄严的葬礼祷文，士兵们环立四周，免冠肃立，同声唱起《万古磐石》（"Rock of Ages"）。头顶湛蓝的天空中，成群结队的秃鹫一圈圈盘旋。让这些人同穴而葬——印第安人与牛仔、矿工、驮夫和大学运动员——身世不详的西部荒原孤子，与怀表上带着斯泰弗森特家和菲什家纹章的贵胄子弟，没有比这更体面的葬礼了；他们赴死的方式浑然如一，正如生前在胆魄与忠忱上亲如一体。

===Original===
On the afternoon of the 25th we moved on a couple of miles, and camped in a marshy open spot close to a beautiful stream. Here we lay for several days. Captain Lee, the British attaché, spent some time with us; we had begun to regard him as almost a member of the regiment. Count von Gotzen, the German attaché, another good fellow, also visited us. General Young was struck down with the fever, and Wood took charge of the brigade. This left me in command of the regiment, of which I was very glad, for such experience as we had had is a quick teacher. By this time the men and I knew one another, and I felt able to make them do themselves justice in march or battle. They understood that I paid no heed to where they came from; no heed to their creed, politics, or social standing; that I would care for them to the utmost of my power, but that I demanded the highest performance of duty; while in return I had seen them tested, and knew I could depend absolutely on their courage, hardihood, obedience, and individual initiative.

There was nothing like enough transportation with the army, whether in the way of wagons or mule-trains; exactly as there had been no sufficient number of landing-boats with the transports. The officers’ baggage had come up, but none of us had much, and the shelter-tents proved only a partial protection against the terrific downpours of rain. These occurred almost every afternoon, and turned the camp into a tarn, and the trails into torrents and quagmires. We were not given quite the proper amount of food, and what we did get, like most of the clothing issued us, was fitter for the Klondyke than for Cuba. We got enough salt pork and hardtack for the men, but not the full ration of coffee and sugar, and nothing else. I organized a couple of expeditions back to the seacoast, taking the strongest and best walkers and also some of the officers’ horses and a stray mule or two, and brought back beans and canned tomatoes. These I got partly by great exertions on my part, and partly by the aid of Colonel Weston of the Commissary Department, a particularly energetic man whose services were of great value. A silly regulation forbade my purchasing canned vegetables, etc., except for the officers; and I had no little difficulty in getting round this regulation, and purchasing (with my own money, of course) what I needed for the men.

===Chinese===
25 日下午，我们朝前推进了几公里，在一处傍着美丽溪流的沼泽状开阔地扎营，在这里一连驻了好几天。英国武官阿瑟·李上尉（Captain Lee）来我们这里盘桓了一阵；我们已开始把他几乎当作全团的一员。德国武官冯·戈岑伯爵（Count von Gotzen）——又一条好汉——也来看望过我们。扬将军被热病击倒，伍德接掌了全旅。这一来，全团便归我指挥，我对此深感欣幸，因为我们刚经受的这种历练，是一位速成的老师。到这时，士兵们与我已彼此知根知底，我觉得无论行军还是作战，我都有把握让他们尽显其才。他们明白，我从不计较他们出身何处，不计较他们的信仰、政见或社会地位；我会竭尽全力爱护他们，但我也要求他们把职责履行到极致；而作为回报，我已亲眼见过他们经受考验，深知他们的勇气、坚韧、服从与个人主动精神绝对靠得住。

全军的运输工具，无论大车还是骡队，都远远不够——正如当初运输舰上登陆艇数量不足一样。军官的行李运上来了，但谁也没有多少东西，而小帐篷对付倾盆暴雨只能算聊胜于无。这样的暴雨几乎每天下午都要来一场，把营地泡成水泽，把小径冲成激流和泥沼。配给我们的口粮数量不足，而且领到的东西同发给我们的多数服装一样，更适合克朗代克（Klondyke），不适合古巴。士兵的咸猪肉和硬面饼尚且够，咖啡和糖却不足额，其他则一无所有。我组织了两支分队返回海岸，带上脚力最健的士兵、几匹军官的马和一两只散骡，运回了豆子和番茄罐头。这些东西，一部分靠我使出浑身解数弄来，一部分多亏军需部的韦斯顿上校（Colonel Weston）相助——他是个精力格外旺盛的人，效力极大。一条愚蠢的规定禁止我为士兵购买罐装蔬菜等物，只许为军官购买；我费了九牛二虎之力才绕开这条规定，（当然是用我自己的钱）买回了士兵们需要的东西。

===Original===
One of the men I took with me on one of these trips was Sherman Bell, the former Deputy Marshal of Cripple Creek, and Wells-Fargo Express rider. In coming home with his load, through a blinding storm, he slipped and opened the old rupture. The agony was very great and one of his comrades took his load. He himself, sometimes walking, and sometimes crawling, got back to camp, where Dr. Church fixed him up with a spike bandage, but informed him that he would have to be sent back to the States when an ambulance came along. The ambulance did not come until the next day, which was the day before we marched to San Juan. It arrived after nightfall, and as soon as Bell heard it coming, he crawled out of the hospital tent into the jungle, where he lay all night; and the ambulance went off without him. The men shielded him just as schoolboys would shield a companion, carrying his gun, belt, and bedding; while Bell kept out of sight until the column started, and then staggered along behind it. I found him the morning of the San Juan fight. He told me that he wanted to die fighting, if die he must, and I hadn’t the heart to send him back. He did splendid service that day, and afterward in the trenches, and though the rupture opened twice again, and on each occasion he was within a hair’s breadth of death, he escaped, and came back with us to the United States.

The army was camped along the valley, ahead of and behind us, our outposts being established on either side. From the generals to the privates all were eager to march against Santiago. At daybreak, when the tall palms began to show dimly through the rising mist, the scream of the cavalry trumpets tore the tropic dawn; and in the evening, as the bands of regiment after regiment played the “Star-Spangled Banner,” all, officers and men alike, stood with heads uncovered, wherever they were, until the last strains of the anthem died away in the hot sunset air.

===Chinese===
这些远征中，有一次我带上了谢尔曼·贝尔（Sherman Bell）——克里普尔克里克的前任副联邦执法官，当过韦尔斯–法戈快运的骑手。驮着物资顶着遮天蔽日的暴风雨归营途中，他脚下一滑，旧疝气复发了。剧痛钻心，一名战友替他扛下了行囊。他自己一时走、一时爬，总算挪回了营地。丘奇医生用人字形绷带给他作了处理，却告知他：救护车一到，就得送回美国本土。救护车第二天才来，而那天正是我们向圣胡安开拔的前一天。车是天黑之后到的，贝尔一听见车声，便从医疗帐篷爬进丛林，在那里躺了一整夜；救护车只好不带他开走了。士兵们像学童包庇同伴一样包庇他，替他扛枪、腰带和铺盖；贝尔则始终避不露面，等纵队开拔之后，才踉踉跄跄地跟在后面。圣胡安战斗那天早晨，我发现了他。他对我说，倘若非死不可，他也情愿战死，我实在狠不下心送他回去。那天他建立了赫赫战功，后来在堑壕中亦复如此；虽然疝气又复发过两次，每一次都离死亡只差毫发，他还是挺了过来，随我们一起回了美国。

大军沿山谷设营，前后相衔，我们的前哨分设两侧。从将军到列兵，人人都渴望向圣地亚哥进军。破晓时分，高大的棕榈树刚在升腾的雾霭中显出朦胧的轮廓，骑兵军号的尖啸便撕开了热带的黎明；傍晚，当一团接一团的军乐队奏响《星条旗》（"Star-Spangled Banner"）时，官兵无论身在何处，一概脱帽肃立，直到国歌的余音消散在灼热的落日气息里。

## V In the Trenches / 五 战壕之中

===Original===
When the shrapnel burst among us on the hillside we made up our minds that we had better settle down to solid siege work. All of the men who were not in the trenches I took off to the right, back of the Gatling guns, where there was a valley, and dispersed them by troops in sheltered parts. It took us an hour or two’s experimenting to find out exactly what spots were free from danger, because some of the Spanish sharpshooters were in trees in our front, where we could not possibly place them from the trenches; and these were able to reach little hollows and depressions where the men were entirely safe from the Spanish artillery and from their trench-fire. Moreover, in one hollow, which we thought safe, the Spaniards succeeded in dropping a shell, a fragment of which went through the head of one of my men, who, astonishing to say, lived, although unconscious, for two hours afterward. Finally, I got all eight troops settled, and the men promptly proceeded to make themselves as much at home as possible. For the next twenty-four hours, however, the amount of comfort was small, as in the way of protection and covering we only had what blankets, raincoats, and hammocks we took from the dead Spaniards. Ammunition, which was, of course, the most vital need, was brought up in abundance; but very little food reached us. That afternoon we had just enough to allow each man for his supper two hardtacks, and one hardtack extra for every four men.

During the first night we had dug trenches sufficient in length and depth to shelter our men and insure safety against attack, but we had not put in any traverses or approaches, nor had we arranged the trenches at all points in the best places for offensive work; for we were working at night on ground which we had but partially explored. Later on an engineer officer stated that he did not think our work had been scientific; and I assured him that I did not doubt that he was right, for I had never before seen a trench, excepting those we captured from the Spaniards, or heard of a traverse, save as I vaguely remembered reading about them in books. For such work as we were engaged in, however, the problem of intrenchment was comparatively simple, and the work we did proved entirely adequate. No man in my regiment was ever hit in the trenches or going in or out of them.

But on the first day there was plenty of excitement connected with relieving the firing line. Under the intense heat, crowded down in cramped attitudes in the rank, newly dug, poisonous soil of the trenches, the men needed to be relieved every six hours or so. Accordingly, in the late morning, and again in the afternoon, I arranged for their release. On each occasion I waited until there was a lull in the firing and then started a sudden rush by the relieving party, who tumbled into the trenches every which way. The movement resulted on each occasion in a terrific outburst of fire from the Spanish lines, which proved quite harmless; and as it gradually died away the men who had been relieved got out as best they could. Fortunately, by the next day I was able to abandon this primitive, though thrilling and wholly novel, military method of relief.

===Chinese===
山坡上榴霰弹在我们中间炸开之后，我们打定了主意：还是安下心来干扎实的围城活计为妙。我把不在战壕里的士兵统统带到右边、加特林机枪（Gatling gun）后面的一个山谷里，按连分散在有遮蔽的地方。我们试了一两个钟头，才弄清究竟哪些地点没有危险，因为有些西班牙神枪手（sharpshooter）就蹲在我们正面的树上，从战壕里根本发现不了他们；而他们反倒够得着那些小洼地和小坑洼——士兵们待在那里，本可完全避开西班牙人的炮火和战壕射击。不但如此，在一处我们自以为安全的洼地里，西班牙人居然成功落下一发炮弹，一块弹片穿透了我一名士兵的头部；说来令人称奇，他竟又活了两个钟头，一直不省人事。最后，我把八个连统统安顿妥当，士兵们随即着手尽量安下家来。不过此后二十四小时里，舒适实在有限：说到防护与遮盖，我们手里只有从阵亡西班牙人身上取来的毯子、雨衣和吊床。弹药——这自然是最要命的需要——源源运到，极为充裕；送到我们手里的食物却少得可怜。那天下午，口粮刚够每人晚饭发两块硬面饼（hardtack），每四个人再多发一块。

第一夜，我们挖出的战壕在长度和深度上已足以掩护士兵、确保不虞攻击，但我们没有构筑任何横墙（traverse）和交通壕，也没有把战壕在各处都安排在最利于进攻的位置上；因为我们是在夜里、在一片只勘察过一半的地面上作业。后来有位工兵军官表示，他认为我们的工程算不上科学；我向他保证，此话我毫不怀疑，因为除了从西班牙人手里缴获的那些战壕，我从前连战壕都没见过，横墙更是闻所未闻——只有读书时依稀记得读到过，算是例外。然而就我们所干的这种活计而言，掘壕的问题相对简单，我们做的工事也证明完全够用。我团没有一个人在战壕里、或进出战壕时中过弹。

不过第一天，换防火线这桩事够刺激的。酷热之下，士兵们蜷在战壕那新掘的、腐臭有毒的泥土里，姿势局促，每隔六个钟头左右就得换一次班。于是，上午近午时分和下午，我又各安排了一次换班。每一回我都等到枪声间歇，然后让接防的队伍突然冲刺，他们连滚带爬、七颠八倒地翻进战壕。每次行动都招来西班牙防线一阵可怕的猛烈射击，结果却全然无害；等枪声渐渐平息，被换下来的人再各自设法爬出去。所幸到第二天，我就能弃用这种原始的、虽然惊心动魄却全然新奇的军事换班法了。

===Original===
When the hardtack came up that afternoon I felt much sympathy for the hungry unfortunates in the trenches and hated to condemn them to six hours more without food; but I did not know how to get food into them. Little McGinty, the bronco buster, volunteered to make the attempt, and I gave him permission. He simply took a case of hardtack in his arms and darted toward the trenches. The distance was but short, and though there was an outburst of fire, he was actually missed. One bullet, however, passed through the case of hardtack just before he disappeared with it into the trench. A trooper named Shanafelt repeated the feat, later, with a pail of coffee. Another trooper, George King, spent a leisure hour in the rear making soup out of some rice and other stuff he found in a Spanish house; he brought some of it to General Wood, Jack Greenway, and myself, and nothing could have tasted more delicious.

At this time our army in the trenches numbered about 11,000 men; and the Spaniards in Santiago about 9,000,5 their reinforcements having just arrived. Nobody on the firing line, whatever was the case in the rear, felt the slightest uneasiness as to the Spaniards being able to break out; but there were plenty who doubted the advisability of trying to rush the heavy earthworks and wire defenses in our front.

All day long the firing continued﻿—musketry and cannon. Our artillery gave up the attempt to fight on the firing line, and was withdrawn well to the rear out of range of the Spanish rifles; so far as we could see, it accomplished very little. The dynamite gun was brought up to the right of the regimental line. It was more effective than the regular artillery because it was fired with smokeless powder, and as it was used like a mortar from behind the hill, it did not betray its presence, and those firing it suffered no loss. Every few shots it got out of order, and the Rough Rider machinists and those furnished by Lieutenant Parker﻿—whom we by this time began to consider as an exceedingly valuable member of our own regiment﻿—would spend an hour or two in setting it right. Sergeant Borrowe had charge of it and handled it well. With him was Sergeant Guitilias, a gallant old fellow, a veteran of the Civil War, whose duties were properly those of standard-bearer, he having charge of the yellow cavalry standard of the regiment; but in the Cuban campaign he was given the more active work of helping run the dynamite gun. The shots from the dynamite gun made a terrific explosion, but they did not seem to go accurately. Once one of them struck a Spanish trench and wrecked part of it. On another occasion one struck a big building, from which there promptly swarmed both Spanish cavalry and infantry, on whom the Colt automatic guns played with good effect, during the minute that elapsed before they could get other cover.

===Chinese===
那天下午硬面饼送上来时，我对战壕里那些饥肠辘辘的倒霉人心怀怜悯，实在不愿再判他们六个钟头无粮可进；可我不知道怎样把食物送进去。驯烈马师小个子麦金蒂（McGinty）自告奋勇去试，我准了他。他索性抱起一箱硬面饼，朝战壕直冲过去。距离本就不长，虽然引来一阵排射，他竟然没被打着。不过有一发子弹穿透了那箱硬面饼，就在他抱着箱子闪进战壕的前一刻。一个名叫沙纳费尔特（Shanafelt）的士兵后来提着一桶咖啡重演了这手绝活。另一个士兵乔治·金（George King）在后头得了一个钟头的闲暇，用他在一户西班牙人家里找到的米和别的东西熬了一锅汤；他端了一些给伍德将军、杰克·格林韦和我，天下再没有比这更美的滋味了。

这时我军据壕兵力约为 11,000 人；圣地亚哥城里的西班牙人约有 9,000⁵，他们的增援刚刚开到。火线上没有人——不管后方情形如何——对西班牙人能否突围有过一丝不安；可是，对我们正面那些厚重的土垒工事和铁丝防御，怀疑强攻是否明智的却大有人在。

射击终日不停——步枪加火炮。我们的炮兵放弃了在火线上作战的尝试，撤到后方远处、西班牙步枪射程之外；据我们看来，它没干成什么名堂。甘油炸药炮（dynamite gun）被调到团防线的右侧。它比正规炮兵更见成效，因为它用无烟火药发射，又是躲在山后当臼炮使，不致暴露行藏，操炮的人也不受损折。每打几发它就出毛病，莽骑兵的机械师们和帕克中尉（Lieutenant Parker）拨来的人手——到这时我们已开始把帕克视为本团自己人里极有价值的一员——就得花上一两个钟头把它修好。博罗中士（Sergeant Borrowe）掌管此炮，操弄得颇为得法。与他搭档的是吉蒂利亚斯中士（Sergeant Guitilias），一位英勇的老伙计、内战老兵，本职是掌旗，掌管着全团那面黄色骑兵军旗；但在古巴战役中，他领了更起劲的差事——帮着操射甘油炸药炮。甘油炸药炮的炮弹炸起来声势骇人，但似乎打得不准。有一发命中一条西班牙战壕，掀翻了它的一段。另有一发命中一幢大建筑，里面顿时蜂拥出西班牙的骑兵和步兵，在他们找到别的掩蔽之前的那一分钟里，柯尔特速射自动炮（Colt automatic gun）对他们一顿好打，颇有战果。

===Original===
These Colt automatic guns were not, on the whole, very successful. The gun detail was under the charge of Sergeant (afterward Lieutenant) Tiffany, assisted by some of our best men, like Stevens, Crowninshield, Bradley, Smith, and Herrig. The guns were mounted on tripods. They were too heavy for men to carry any distance and we could not always get mules. They would have been more effective if mounted on wheels, as the Gatlings were. Moreover, they proved more delicate than the Gatlings, and very readily got out of order. A further and serious disadvantage was that they did not use the Krag ammunition, as the Gatlings did, but the Mauser ammunition. The Spanish cartridges which we captured came in quite handily for this reason. Parker took the same fatherly interest in these two Colts that he did in the dynamite gun, and finally I put all three and their men under his immediate care, so that he had a battery of seven guns.

In fact, I think Parker deserved rather more credit than any other one man in the entire campaign. I do not allude especially to his courage and energy, great though they were, for there were hundreds of his fellow-officers of the cavalry and infantry who possessed as much of the former quality, and scores who possessed as much of the latter; but he had the rare good judgment and foresight to see the possibilities of the machine-guns, and, thanks to the aid of General Shafter, he was able to organize his battery. He then, by his own exertions, got it to the front and proved that it could do invaluable work on the field of battle, as much in attack as in defence. Parker’s Gatlings were our inseparable companions throughout the siege. After our trenches were put in final shape, he took off the wheels of a couple and placed them with our own two Colts in the trenches. His gunners slept beside the Rough Riders in the bomb-proofs, and the men shared with one another when either side got a supply of beans or of coffee and sugar; for Parker was as wide-awake and energetic in getting food for his men as we prided ourselves upon being in getting food for ours. Besides, he got oil, and let our men have plenty for their rifles. At no hour of the day or night was Parker anywhere but where we wished him to be in the event of an attack. If I was ordered to send a troop of Rough Riders to guard some road or some break in the lines, we usually got Parker to send a Gatling along, and whether the change was made by day or by night, the Gatling went, over any ground and in any weather. He never exposed the Gatlings needlessly or unless there was some object to be gained, but if serious fighting broke out, he always took a hand. Sometimes this fighting would be the result of an effort on our part to quell the fire from the Spanish trenches; sometimes the Spaniards took the initiative; but at whatever hour of the twenty-four serious fighting began, the drumming of the Gatlings was soon heard through the cracking of our own carbines.

I have spoken thus of Parker’s Gatling detachment. How can I speak highly enough of the regular cavalry with whom it was our good fortune to serve? I do not believe that in any army of the world could be found a more gallant and soldierly body of fighters than the officers and men of the First, Third, Sixth, Ninth, and Tenth United States Cavalry, beside whom we marched to blood-bought victory under the tropic skies of Santiago. The American regular sets the standard of excellence. When we wish to give the utmost possible praise to a volunteer organization, we say that it is as good as the regulars. I was exceedingly proud of the fact that the regulars treated my regiment as on a complete equality with themselves, and were as ready to see it in a post of danger and responsibility as to see any of their own battalions. Lieutenant Colonel Dorst, a man from whom praise meant a good deal, christened us “the Eleventh United States Horse,” and we endeavored, I think I may say successfully, to show that we deserved the title by our conduct, not only in fighting and in marching, but in guarding the trenches and in policing camp. In less than sixty days the regiment had been raised, organized, armed, equipped, drilled, mounted, dismounted, kept for a fortnight on transports, and put through two victorious aggressive fights in very difficult country, the loss in killed and wounded amounting to a quarter of those engaged. This is a record which it is not easy to match in the history of volunteer organizations. The loss was but small compared to that which befell hundreds of regiments in some of the great battles of the later years of the Civil War; but it may be doubted whether there was any regiment which made such a record during the first months of any of our wars.

===Chinese===
总的说来，这几门柯尔特速射自动炮算不得很成功。炮组由蒂法尼中士（Sergeant Tiffany，后升少尉）统领，辅佐他的是我们一些最出色的人，如史蒂文斯、克朗宁希尔、布拉德利、史密斯和赫里希。炮架在三脚架上。它们重得没法让人扛多远，骡子又不是随时要得到。倘若像加特林那样装上轮子，效用本会更好。何况它们比加特林娇气，动不动就出故障。还有一个严重的短处：它们不像加特林那样用克拉格弹药，而是用毛瑟（Mauser）弹药。正因如此，我们缴获的西班牙子弹派上了大用场。帕克对这两门柯尔特怀着他对甘油炸药炮同样的慈父般的关切，最后我把三门炮连同人手统统交给他直接照管，这样一来他手下就有了一支七门炮的炮列。

其实，我以为在整个战役里，帕克一人应得的功劳比其他任何一个人都要多些。我并非专指他的勇气与精力——尽管两者都大得很——因为骑兵和步兵里与他同辈的军官，勇气不输他的有数百人，精力相当他的也有数十人；但他有一种难得的良识与远见，看得出机枪的种种可能，又多亏沙夫特将军相助，才得以组建起自己的炮列。随后他凭一己之力把它弄上火线，证明它在战场上能建立无可估量的功勋，攻守两皆宜。围城期间，帕克的加特林是我们须臾不离的伙伴。我们的战壕最后定型之后，他卸下其中两门的轮子，把它们同我们自己的两门柯尔特一起安置进战壕。他的炮手们在防炮掩体（bomb-proof）里与莽骑兵同眠，哪一方弄到了豆子、咖啡和糖，彼此便分着用；因为在为部下张罗吃食这件事上，帕克同我们自诩的样子一样精明肯干。此外他还弄到了油，让我们的人给步枪上了个饱。无论昼夜何时，一有战事，帕克从来都待在我们但愿他在的地方。每逢我奉命派一连莽骑兵去守某条道路或防线上的某处缺口，我们通常请帕克派一门加特林同去，而无论白天还是黑夜调动，加特林都去，什么地形、什么天气都去。他从不白白暴露加特林，除非有便宜可占；可一旦恶战打响，他总要插上一手。有时这仗起于我们想压下西班牙战壕的火力，有时是西班牙人先动的手；但一天二十四小时里，无论恶战何时开场，我们自己的卡宾枪爆响之中，很快就听得见加特林咚咚的轰鸣。

关于帕克的加特林分队，我已经说了这许多。对那些我们有幸并肩服役的正规骑兵，我又怎能褒扬得尽？我不相信世界任何一国的军队里，能找出一支比美国第一、第三、第六、第九、第十骑兵团的官兵更英勇、更军人气的战斗队伍——正是在他们身旁，我们在圣地亚哥的热带天空下，走向以鲜血换来的胜利。美国正规军立着卓越的标准。每当我们想给一支义勇部队以无以复加的赞誉，就说：它跟正规军一样棒。正规军把我团当作与他们完全平等的队伍看待，让我团同他们自己的任何营一样乐于承担危险而责任重大的岗位，这一点使我极为自豪。多斯特中校（Lieutenant Colonel Dorst）——此人的赞许分量极重——给我们起了「美国第十一骑兵」（the Eleventh United States Horse）的名号；我以为不妨说，我们努力以行动证明自己当得起这个称号——不但在战斗和行军中，也在守壕和整肃营地中。不到六十天，这个团已经征募、组建、武装、装备、操练完毕，上过马、下过马，在运输船上困了两个星期，又在极其难行的地界打了两场获胜的攻坚恶仗，伤亡合计达参战人数的四分之一。在义勇部队的历史上，这是一份不易匹敌的纪录。同内战后期几场大战役中数以百计的团队所遭的损失相比，这点损失还算小；但在我们历次战争的头几个月里，可曾有过哪个团创下这样的纪录，却大可怀疑。

===Original===
After the battle of San Juan my men had really become veterans; they and I understood each other perfectly, and trusted each other implicitly; they knew I would share every hardship and danger with them, would do everything in my power to see that they were fed, and so far as might be, sheltered and spared; and in return I knew that they would endure every kind of hardship and fatigue without a murmur and face every danger with entire fearlessness. I felt utter confidence in them, and would have been more than willing to put them to any task which any crack regiment of the world, at home or abroad, could perform. They were natural fighters, men of great intelligence, great courage, great hardihood, and physical prowess; and I could draw on these qualities and upon their spirit of ready, soldierly obedience to make up for any deficiencies in the technique of the trade which they had temporarily adopted. It must be remembered that they were already good individual fighters, skilled in the use of the horse and the rifle, so that there was no need of putting them through the kind of training in which the ordinary raw recruit must spend his first year or two.

On July 2nd, as the day wore on, the fight, though raging fitfully at intervals, gradually died away. The Spanish guerillas were causing us much trouble. They showed great courage, exactly as did their soldiers who were defending the trenches. In fact, the Spaniards throughout showed precisely the qualities they did early in the century, when, as every student will remember, their fleets were a helpless prey to the English warships, and their armies utterly unable to stand in the open against those of Napoleon’s marshals, while on the other hand their guerillas performed marvellous feats, and their defence of intrenchments and walled towns, as at Saragossa and Gerona, were the wonder of the civilized world.

In our front their sharpshooters crept up before dawn and either lay in the thick jungle or climbed into some tree with dense foliage. In these places it proved almost impossible to place them, as they kept cover very carefully, and their smokeless powder betrayed not the slightest sign of their whereabouts. They caused us a great deal of annoyance and some little loss, and though our own sharpshooters were continually taking shots at the places where they supposed them to be, and though occasionally we would play a Gatling or a Colt all through the top of a suspicious tree, I but twice saw Spaniards brought down out of their perches from in front of our lines﻿—on each occasion the fall of the Spaniard being hailed with loud cheers by our men.

===Chinese===
圣胡安一战之后，我的士兵们真正成了老兵；他们和我彼此了如指掌，相互绝对信任；他们知道，每一分艰苦与危险我都要同他们共尝，我会竭尽所能让他们吃饱，并尽可能让他们有处栖身、得以喘息；作为回报，我也知道，他们会毫无怨言地忍受一切艰难疲惫，全无惧色地面对一切危险。我对他们怀着完全的信心，世上任何一支精锐团队——无论国内国外——担当得了的任务，我都乐于放手交给他们。他们是天生的战士，智力出众，胆气过人，吃苦耐劳，体魄强健；我可以倚仗这些品质，加上他们那种闻令即从的军人精神，来弥补他们临时改行吃这行饭在技术上的任何欠缺。必须记住，他们本已是出色的单打独斗的战士，精于使马、精于使枪，因此无须再让他们去受普通新兵头一两年非受不可的那套训练。

7 月 2 日，随着白昼推移，战斗虽间或还狂烈一阵，渐渐平息了下去。西班牙游击兵（guerilla）给我们添了许多麻烦。他们表现出极大的勇气，同守卫战壕的他们的士兵一般无二。事实上，西班牙人自始至终显露的，正是他们本世纪初显露过的那些品质——凡读过史书的人都记得，那时他们的舰队是英国军舰手到擒来的猎物，他们的陆军在开阔地上全然招架不住拿破仑麾下元帅们的大军；可另一方面，他们的游击队建立了惊人的功勋，他们对堑壕与城墙城镇的死守，如萨拉戈萨（Saragossa）与赫罗纳（Gerona）之守，曾是文明世界的奇观。

在我们正面，他们的神枪手天亮前就爬了上来，或伏在密林里，或攀上枝叶浓密的树。在这样的地方要发现他们几乎不可能，因为他们掩蔽得极为小心，无烟火药又不露半点行藏。他们给我们添了无穷烦恼，也造成了一点小小的损失；虽然我们自己的神枪手不断朝他们估计藏身的地方开火，虽然我们偶尔把一门加特林或柯尔特的火力从头到尾扫过可疑的树冠，从我们战线正面，我只两次见过西班牙人从栖身的树上被打落——每回那西班牙人坠落，我们的士兵都高声欢呼相庆。

===Original===
These sharpshooters in our front did perfectly legitimate work, and were entitled to all credit for their courage and skill. It was different with the guerillas in our rear. Quite a number of these had been posted in trees at the time of the San Juan fight. They were using, not Mausers, but Remingtons, which shot smokeless powder and a brass-coated bullet. It was one of these bullets which had hit Winslow Clark by my side on Kettle Hill; and though for long-range fighting the Remingtons were, of course, nothing like as good as the Mausers, they were equally serviceable for short-range bush work, as they used smokeless powder. When our troops advanced and the Spaniards in the trenches and in reserve behind the hill fled, the guerillas in the trees had no time to get away and in consequence were left in the rear of our lines. As we found out from the prisoners we took, the Spanish officers had been careful to instil into the minds of their soldiers the belief that the Americans never granted quarter, and I suppose it was in consequence of this that the guerillas did not surrender; for we found that the Spaniards were anxious enough to surrender as soon as they became convinced that we would treat them mercifully. At any rate, these guerillas kept up in their trees and showed not only courage but wanton cruelty and barbarity. At times they fired upon armed men in bodies, but they much preferred for their victims the unarmed attendants, the doctors, the chaplains, the hospital stewards. They fired at the men who were bearing off the wounded in litters; they fired at the doctors who came to the front, and at the chaplains who started to hold burial service; the conspicuous Red Cross brassard worn by all of these noncombatants, instead of serving as a protection, seemed to make them the special objects of the guerilla fire. So annoying did they become that I sent out that afternoon and next morning a detail of picked sharpshooters to hunt them out, choosing, of course, first-class woodsmen and mountain men who were also good shots. My sharpshooters felt very vindictively toward these guerillas and showed them no quarter. They started systematically to hunt them, and showed themselves much superior at the guerillas’ own game, killing eleven, while not one of my men was scratched. Two of the men who did conspicuously good service in this work were Troopers Goodwin and Proffit, both of Arizona, but one by birth a Californian and the other a North Carolinian. Goodwin was a natural shot, not only with the rifle and revolver, but with the sling. Proffit might have stood as a type of the mountaineers described by John Fox and Miss Murfree. He was a tall, sinewy, handsome man of remarkable strength, an excellent shot and a thoroughly good soldier. His father had been a Confederate officer, rising from the ranks, and if the war had lasted long enough the son would have risen in the same manner. As it was, I should have been glad to have given him a commission, exactly as I should have been glad to have given a number of others in the regiment commissions, if I had only had them. Proffit was a saturnine, reserved man, who afterward fell very sick with the fever, and who, as a reward for his soldierly good conduct, was often granted unusual privileges; but he took the fever and the privileges with the same iron indifference, never grumbling, and never expressing satisfaction.

The sharpshooters returned by nightfall. Soon afterward I established my pickets and outposts well to the front in the jungle, so as to prevent all possibility of surprise. After dark, fires suddenly shot up on the mountain passes far to our right. They all rose together and we could make nothing of them. After a good deal of consultation, we decided they must be some signals to the Spaniards in Santiago, from the troops marching to reinforce them from without﻿—for we were ignorant that the reinforcements had already reached the city, the Cubans being quite unable to prevent the Spanish regulars from marching wherever they wished. While we were thus pondering over the watch-fires and attributing them to Spanish machinations of some sort, it appears that the Spaniards, equally puzzled, were setting them down as an attempt at communication between the insurgents and our army. Both sides were accordingly on the alert, and the Spaniards must have strengthened their outlying parties in the jungle ahead of us, for they suddenly attacked one of our pickets, wounding Crockett seriously. He was brought in by the other troopers. Evidently the Spanish lines felt a little nervous, for this sputter of shooting was immediately followed by a tremendous fire of great guns and rifles from their trenches and batteries.
